On Galois representations associated to mod p p Hilbert modular forms
Fred Diamond, Shu Sasaki

Abstract
. We consider mod p p Hilbert modular forms for a totally real field F F , viewed as sections of automorphic line bundles on Hilbert modular varieties in prime characteristic p p . For a Hecke eigenform of arbitrary weight, we prove the existence of an associated two-dimensional representation of the absolute Galois group of F F . Furthermore, for any such irreducible Galois representation, we formulate a conjecture predicting the set of weights of eigenforms from which it arises. This generalizes Edixhoven’s variant of the weight part of Serre’s Conjecture (in the case F = ℚ F=\mathbb{Q} ), and removes the restriction that p p be unramified in F F from prior work in this direction. We also establish one direction of a conjectural relation with the algebraic analogue of the weight part of Serre’s Conjecture in this context. Finally, we prove results towards our conjecture in the case of partial weight one for real quadratic fields F F in which p p is ramified.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background and motivation

In
[
Ser87
]
, Serre formulated a remarkable conjecture, later remarkably proved by Khare and Wintenberger
[
KW09a
,
KW09b
]
, predicting the modularity of certain two-dimensional Galois representations in prime characteristic
p
.
More precisely, the conjecture (now theorem) asserts that every odd, continuous, irreducible representation of the form
\rho:{\operatorname{Gal}\,}({\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}/\mathbb{Q})\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
arises as the reduction of a
p
-adic Galois representation associated to a Hecke eigenform
f\in S_{k}(\Gamma_{1}(N))
, where
N=N_{\rho}
is the (prime-to-
p
) Artin conductor of
\rho
and
k=k_{\rho}
is described explicitly in terms of the restriction of
\rho
to a decomposition group at
p
.

In his original formulation, Serre restricts his attention to modular forms in characteristic zero and of weight
k\geq 2
, but remarks
[
Ser87
, pp.197–8]
:

Au lieu de définir les formes paraboliques à coefficients dans
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
par réduction à partir de la caractéristique
0
, comme nous l’avons fait, nous aurions pu utiliser la définition de Katz [23],
1
1

1

[
Kat73
]
in the references herein.
qui conduit à un espace a priori plus grand…  Il serait également intéressant d’étudier de ce point de vue le cas
k=1
, que nous avons exclu jusqu’ici; peut-être la définition de Katz donne-t-elle alors beaucoup plus de représentations
\rho_{f}
?

The referenced definition of Katz interprets the space of modular forms in characteristic
p
more directly as sections of line bundles on the reduction mod
p
of the modular curve
X_{1}(N)
.
The question above was addressed by Edixhoven in his paper
[
Edi92
]
proving the weight part of Serre’s Conjecture (for odd
p
), i.e., that if
\rho
arises from a Hecke eigenform of some weight
k\geq 2
and level
N
prime to
p
, then in fact it arises from one of weight
k_{\rho}
and level
N
(in the classical sense above or equivalently that of Katz).
To answer Serre’s question, Edixhoven furthermore proves that such a
\rho
is modular of weight one and level
N
in Katz’s sense if and only if
\rho
is unramified at
p
. (In general, only the forward implication holds using the classical sense of modularity in the original formulation.)

In his paper, Serre also goes on to ask
[
Ser87
, 3.4(2)]
:

Peut-on reformuler ces conjectures dans le cadre d’une théorie des représentations (mod
p
) des groupes adéliques? Autrement dit, existe-t-il une ‘‘philosophie de Langlands modulo p’’, comme le demandent Ash et Stevens dans [2]
2
2

2

[
AS00
]
in the references herein
? Si oui, cela permettrait peut-être:

de donner une définition plus naturelle du poids
k
attaché à
\rho
;

de remplacer
\operatorname{GL}_{2}
par
\operatorname{GL}_{N}
, ou même par un groupe réductif;

de remplacer
\mathbb{Q}
par d’autres corps globaux.

These questions have received a great deal of attention, thanks in part to their significance in the context of the
p
-adic Langlands Programme. Firstly, it turns out that the most natural characterizations of the weight
k_{\rho}
are provided by
p
-adic Hodge theory; furthermore, this weight can be viewed as a manifestation of a finer invariant associated to
\rho
by the Breuil–Mézard Conjecture
[
BM02
]
(see
[
Wie21b
]
, for example). The question of replacing
\operatorname{GL}_{2}
by
\operatorname{GL}_{N}
was first considered by Ash and Sinnott
[
AS00
]
, and that of replacing
\mathbb{Q}
by a number field by the first author with Buzzard and Jarvis
[
BDJ10
]
. Many people have continued work in these directions, formulating (and proving results towards) Serre weight and Breuil–Mézard Conjectures in the context of Galois representations associated to cohomological automorphic forms. See especially
[
GHS18
]
for a formulation in significant generality and a discussion of the history of the problem, and
[
LLH22
]
and the references therein for more recent results.

There has, however, been relatively little consideration in more general settings of the variant addressed by Edixhoven.
In view of this, we initiated a study in
[
DS23
]
of this problem in the context of Galois representations associated to Hilbert modular forms, where
F
is a totally real number field.
In particular, we formulated a conjecture that predicts the set of weights of all mod
p
Hilbert modular eigenforms giving rise to a fixed
\rho:{\operatorname{Gal}\,}(\overline{F}/F)\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
, under the hypothesis that
p
is unramified in
F
. While it is still the case that the set of weights is conjecturally determined (via
p
-adic Hodge theory) by the local behavior of
\rho
at primes over
p
, several new features emerge. To begin with, the interpretation of modularity in terms of sections of automorphic bundles on Shimura varieties allows for the consideration of non-cohomological weights, which are necessarily excluded by the framework of
[
BDJ10
]
and its generalizations discussed above. In the setting of classical modular forms considered by Edixhoven (i.e., the case
F=\mathbb{Q}
), this extension ultimately pertains only to weight one modular forms and Galois representations unramified at
p
, but the analogous extension in the Hilbert modular setting involves richer structure and behavior on both the automorphic and Galois sides of the relation. Another new feature revealed in
[
DS23
]
is the determinative role played by a certain cone of weights, which we call the
minimal cone
. Input from the geometry of Shimura varieties also enables generalizations of weight entailment phenomena that were provided in the classical setting by Hasse invariants and
\Theta
-operators.

The primary motivation for this paper is to remove the restriction from the conjectures and results in
[
DS23
]
that
p
be unramified in
F
. Thus for an arbitrary prime
p
and totally real field
F
, we consider a continuous, irreducible, totally odd
3
3

3

By
totally odd,
we mean that
\det(\rho(c))=-1
for every complex conjugation
c
.
representation

\rho:{\operatorname{Gal}\,}(\overline{F}/F)\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})

and formulate conjectures predicting the weights of
mod
p
Hilbert modular eigenforms of level prime to
p

giving rise to
\rho
in terms of its local behavior at primes over
p
. Since
p
may be ramified in
F
, we use the Pappas–Rapoport model for the Hilbert modular variety to define Hilbert modular forms in characteristic
p
. In order for the conjectures to be meaningful, we must first prove the existence of Galois representations associated to such eigenforms, thus removing parity hypotheses on the weight from prior results of Reduzzi and Xiao
[
RX17
]
. To that end, we adopt a different approach from the one used in
[
DS23
]
(for
p
unramified in
F
); in this paper we make more systematic use of congruences involving forms that are neither
L
-algebraic nor
C
-algebraic, along with the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence. This perspective also enables us to prove one direction of a conjectural relationship between the sets of weights giving rise to
\rho
in the sense of this paper and that of
[
BDJ10
]
. (This is new even when
p
is unramified in
F
.)

The geometric Serre weight conjecture formulated in this paper is qualitatively similar to the one from
[
DS23
]
that it generalizes, but it clarifies how features such as minimal cone and weight-shifting phenomena manifest when
p
is ramified in
F
. In particular, our formulation in terms of line bundles on the Pappas–Rapoport model captures aspects of the numerology, including that of weight entailment phenomena, implicit in Gee’s generalization (
[
Gee11a
]
) of the Buzzard–Diamond–Jarvis Conjecture to the case where
p
is ramified in
F
.
Furthermore, the ramified setting more readily exhibits how geometric Serre weights provide a finer invariant of local Galois representations than their algebraic analogue. For example, if
p
is highly totally ramified in
F
, then there is a class of representations
\rho
as above whose set of algebraic Serre weights consists of
all
those with the same central character (see
[
GS11
]
), but whose (conjectural) geometric Serre weights are distinguished by a hierarchy of possible minimal weights.

One direction of our conjecture is an assertion about the local behavior at primes over
p
of all Galois representations
\rho
arising from mod
p
Hilbert modular forms of a given weight. It can thus be interpreted as a local-global compatibility statement in the context of Langlands correspondences associating Galois representations to automorphic forms in characteristic
p
. The other direction asserts that
all
continuous, irreducible, totally odd
\rho
satisfying those local conditions indeed arise from forms of that weight. One can envision the two directions as providing a starting point to using the approach of Calegari–Geraghty
[
CG18
]
to propagate such an equivalence to suitable deformations of
\rho
.

As in
[
DS23
]
, we provide evidence for our conjecture by proving strong results describing which Galois representations (a priori modular of
some
weight) arise from forms of partial weight one for real quadratic
F
, except that we now we assume
p
is ramified in
F
. Our overall approach to this is similar to that of
[
DS23
]
, but we must now make extensive use of the
\Theta
-operators constructed and studied in the ramified case in
[
Dia23
]
.

We remark that the results from
[
DS23
]
in the quadratic unramified case have since been strengthened by Yang (see
[
Yan25a
, Cor. 4.31]
) and partly generalized to higher degree by Wiersema
[
Wie22
,
Wie25
]
. Further results in the unramified case are expected from forthcoming work of Kansal, Levin and Savitt (on local-global compatibility) and Wiersema (on geometric modularity in prescribed weight).

1.2.
Outline of the paper

We now describe the contents of the paper in more detail.

Sections 2–4 mainly recall definitions and prior results we will use on Hilbert modular forms, and more generally automorphic forms for unit groups of quaternion algebras over a totally real field
F
. Section 2 reviews the classical theory over
\mathbb{C}
, including the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence. In Section 3, we recall aspects of the theory with integral coefficients, or more precisely over the ring of integers of a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

In Section 4, we focus on our main objects of study: the spaces
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
of Hilbert modular forms of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
and level
U
with coefficients in a finite field
E
of characteristic
p
, where
\Sigma
is the set of embeddings
F\to\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
. The component
\vec{m}
plays a secondary role related to twisting by characters, so for the purpose of the introduction, we assume it is trivial and suppress it from the notation.
The level
U
is an open compact subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
, which we generally assume is prime to
p
in the sense that it contains a maximal compact subgroup of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F_{p})
(where
F_{p}=F\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{p}
).

The spaces
M_{\vec{k}}(U,E)
are equipped with commuting endomorphisms (
Hecke operators
)
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for all but finitely primes
v
of
F
. Furthermore these are compatible with weight-shifting maps defined by application of partial
\Theta
-operators and multiplication by partial Hasse invariants.
The partial Hasse invariants are elements
H_{\sigma}\in M_{\vec{h}_{\sigma}}(U,E)
for certain weights
\vec{h}_{\sigma}
indexed by
\sigma\in\Sigma
, while the partial
\Theta
-operators are certain differential operators (which, strictly speaking, also shift the component
\vec{m}
we just suppressed).

Motivated by the conjectures being formulated for this paper, the first author and Kassaei proved in
[
DK23a
]
that every
Hilbert modular form arises by multiplication by partial Hasse invariants from one whose weight lies in a certain cone
\Xi_{\min}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\Sigma}
, which we call the
minimal cone
. Using an idea of Deo, Dimitrov and Wiese
[
DDW24
]
, we prove a refinement in this paper (Theorem
4.2.3
) showing that
\Xi_{\min}
can be replaced by
\Xi_{\min}^{+}\cup\{\vec{0}\}
, where
\Xi_{\min}^{+}=\Xi_{\min}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}_{>0}
.

In Section 5, we prove the existence of Galois representations associated to Hilbert modular eigenforms (Theorem
5.2.1
):

Theorem A
.

Suppose that
f\in M_{\vec{k}}(U,E)
is an eigenform for
T_{v}
(resp.
S_{v}
) with eigenvalue
a_{v}
(resp.
d_{v}
) for all
v\not\in Q
, where
Q
is a finite set of primes containing all those dividing the level or
p
. Then there exists a continuous representation

\rho_{f}:{\operatorname{Gal}\,}(\overline{F}/F)\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(E)

such that if
v\not\in Q
, then
\rho_{f}
is unramified at
v
and the characteristic polynomial of
\rho_{f}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{v})
(where
{\operatorname{Frob}}_{v}
is a Frobenius element at
v
) is

X^{2}-a_{v}X+\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)d_{v}.

This was proved in
[
RX17
]
under the assumption that all components
k_{\sigma}
of
\vec{k}
have the same parity. The idea is to use congruences to forms of higher weight and cohomological vanishing (provided by ampleness results) for automorphic bundles in order to lift
f
to a characteristic zero eigenform for which one already has associated Galois representations. A difficulty that arises without the parity hypothesis is that such lifts will not have level prime to
p
, so one also needs cohomological vanishing results for certain Hilbert modular varieties with bad reduction. Under the assumption that
p
is unramified in
F
, we removed the parity hypothesis in
[
DS23
]
using this approach, with the cohomological vanishing provided by
[
DKS23
]
.
These vanishing results were generalized to the ramified case in

[
Dia25
]
, so that one can prove the above theorem by combining the methods of
[
RX17
]
and
[
DS23
]
.

Here however, we present a different proof, inspired by remarks of David Loeffler and George Boxer. The idea is to lift (a twist of)
f
to a (non-algebraic) characteristic zero eigenform of level prime to
p
. We lack a Galois representation associated to the lift, but we can nonetheless apply the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence to transfer our problem to the setting of automorphic forms on quaternion algebras. This allows us to construct the desired congruences to forms with primes over
p
in the level, without having to appeal to the more delicate analysis of Hilbert modular varieties with bad reduction.

In §
5.3
, we combine the approach with techniques from Hida theory in order to prove that if (a twist of)
f
is ordinary at a prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
over
p
, then the restriction of
\rho_{f}
to
{\operatorname{Gal}\,}(\overline{F}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}/F_{{\mathfrak{p}}})
is reducible, with subrepresentation and quotient determined by the (invertible)
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
-eigenvalue (and central character).

In Section 6, we formulate our conjecture predicting which Galois representations
\rho
are
geometrically modular
of weight
\vec{k}
, in the sense that they arise from some eigenform
f
of weight
\vec{k}
as above.

Conjecture B
.

Suppose that
\vec{k}\in\Xi_{\min}^{+}
and
\rho:{\operatorname{Gal}\,}(\overline{F}/F)\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
is continuous, irreducible and totally odd. Then
\rho
is geometrically modular of weight
\vec{k}
if and only if
\chi_{\operatorname{cyc}\,}\otimes\rho|_{{\operatorname{Gal}\,}(\overline{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}/F_{\mathfrak{p}})}
has a crystalline lift with labelled Hodge–Tate weights

(0,k_{\sigma}-1)_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
for each prime
{\mathfrak{p}}
dividing
p
, where
\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}
is the (mod
p
) cyclotomic character and
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
is the set of embeddings
\sigma\in\Sigma
factoring through
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

See §
6.2
for notions from
p
-adic Hodge theory, including the definition of labelled weights, and
Conjecture
6.3.1
for a more refined version of the conjecture covering all weights
\vec{k}
.

In §
6.4
, we discuss what happens as we vary

\vec{m}
(the weight component we assumed here to be trivial), and in particular its interaction with twisting and with partial
\Theta
-operators.

In §
6.5
, we discuss the relation with the notion of being modular of weight
\vec{k}
in the sense of
[
BDJ10
]
and its generalizations, which we call
algebraic modularity
. In particular, we conjecture that the notions are equivalent for
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}^{\Sigma}\cap\Xi_{\min}
. Furthermore, using ingredients of the proof of Theorem
A
, we prove one direction of this equivalence. (For partial results in the other direction, under the assumption that
p
is unramified in
F
, see
[
Yan25a
]
.)
More precisely, we prove the following (see Theorem
6.5.5
), which allows one to transfer algebraic modularity results of Gee and others (
[
GLS15
,
GK19
,
New14
]
into ones towards Conjecture
B
.

Theorem C
.

If
\rho:{\operatorname{Gal}\,}(\overline{F}/F)\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
is irreducible and algebraically modular of weight
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}^{\Sigma}
, then
\rho
is geometrically modular of weight
\vec{k}
.

We also present a conjectural converse (Conjecture
6.6.6
) to our result on Galois representations associated to ordinary forms, i.e., that if
\rho
is as in Conjecture
B
, then it arises from a form
f
of weight
\vec{k}
which is ordinary at all primes
{\mathfrak{p}}
dividing
p
such that
\rho|_{{\operatorname{Gal}\,}(\overline{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}/F_{\mathfrak{p}})}
has the requisite shape. We also explain how results in this direction follow from analogous ones in the context of algebraic modularity.

In Section 7, we prove results towards Conjecture
B
in the case of partial weight one, where
F
is a real quadratic field in which
p
ramifies. Note that such weights are outside the setting where results on algebraic modularity apply, so we use the approach developed in
[
DS23
]
and
[
Wie21a
]
. The idea is to relate the properties of crystalline liftability and geometric modularity in these irregular weights to those properties in regular weights, so that one can apply the results of Gee and others on algebraic Serre weight conjectures.

Before specializing to the case of real quadratic
F
, we generalize the results we need from
[
DS23
]
on stabilized eigenforms to the case where
p
is ramified in
F
. We also generalize results of
[
GLS15
]
and
[
Wie25
]
on reductions of crystalline representations to the case of irregular weight. In §
7.3
, we specialize the
p
-adic Hodge theory results to the ramified quadratic case in order to relate crystalline liftability in irregular and regular weights.
Finally in the last section, we use partial Hasse invariants and

\Theta
-operators to obtain analogous relations for geometric modularity. Putting everything together gives the following result:

Theorem D
.

Let
F
be a real quadratic field in which
p
is ramified,
{\mathfrak{p}}
the prime of
F
dividing
p
, and
\vec{k}=(1,w)\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
for some

w\in[2,p]
. Suppose that
\rho
is irreducible, geometrically modular of some weight, and satisfies a Taylor–Wiles hypothesis. If
\rho|_{{\operatorname{Gal}\,}(\overline{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}/F_{\mathfrak{p}})}
has a crystalline lift of weight
\vec{k}
, and is reducible if
w=2
, then
\rho
is geometrically modular of weight
\vec{k}
.

The Taylor–Wiles hypothesis (on the image of
\rho
; see the statements of Theorems
7.4.1
and
7.4.6
for details) is needed in order to apply results on algebraic Serre weight conjectures. We also prove a converse (see Theorems
7.4.3
and
7.4.4
), assuming our conjecture that geometric implies algebraic modularity for weights
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}^{\Sigma}\cap\Xi_{\min}
. On the other hand, we are able to use ad hoc arguments to remove the Taylor–Wiles hypothesis from our results in this direction.

Finally, we remark that the additional reducibility hypothesis on the local Galois representation in the case
w=2
is needed in order obtain the relation between crystalline liftability, as well as geometric modularity, in irregular and regular weights.

1.3.
Notation and conventions

We fix a totally real field
F\neq\mathbb{Q}
throughout the paper, and let
d=[F:\mathbb{Q}]
denote its degree. We denote its ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
and let
{\mathfrak{d}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
denote its different.

For any field
L
, we write
\overline{L}
for its algebraic closure, and we view
{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}
as a subfield of
\mathbb{C}
.
We let
\Sigma
denote the set of embeddings
F\hookrightarrow{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}
, which we often identify with the set
\Sigma_{\infty}
of embeddings
F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

We write
F_{+}^{\times}
for the subgroup of totally positive elements of
F^{\times}
. More generally, we use the subscript “+” to denote the set of totally positive elements of any subset of
F_{\infty}=F\otimes\mathbb{R}=\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\infty}}\mathbb{R}
. We extend this usage to matrices via the determinant, so for example
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F)_{+}
denotes the set of elements of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F)
with totally positive determinant.

For any finite place
v
of
F
, we write
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,v}
(resp.
F_{v}
) for the completion of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
(resp.
F
) at
v
. We let
{\mathbb{A}}
denote the ring of adeles of
\mathbb{Q}
and
{\mathbb{A}}_{F}
the adeles of
F
, so
{\mathbb{A}}_{F}=F\otimes\mathbb{A}=F_{\infty}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}
where
{\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}=F\otimes\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\prod_{v}F_{v}
, the last product being over all finite places
v
of
F
.

We fix a rational prime
p
and an embedding
{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}\to\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
, and let
S_{p}
be the set of primes of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
containing
p
. We write
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)}
for the localization
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
at the prime ideal
(p)=p\mathbb{Z}
of
\mathbb{Z}
. We let
\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}
denote the prime-to-
p
finite adeles of
F
; i.e., the kernel of the natural projection
\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}\to F_{p}:=\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}}F_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

For each
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
, write
\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}
for the
residue field
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}/{\mathfrak{p}}
. Denote the maximal unramified subextension of
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
by
F_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}
, and identify it with the field of fractions of the ring of Witt vectors
W(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})
. Let
f_{\mathfrak{p}}
denote the residue degree
[F_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}:{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}]=[\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}:\mathbb{F}_{p}]
, and
e_{\mathfrak{p}}
the ramification index
[F_{\mathfrak{p}}:F_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}]
.
We choose a uniformizer
\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}

such that
\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)}={\mathfrak{p}}{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)}
. On the other hand, if
v\not\in S_{p}
, then
\varpi_{v}
will simply denote a uniformizer in
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,v}
.

We let
\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
denote the set of embeddings

F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\to\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
, and let
\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}
denote
the set of embeddings
F_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}\to\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}

(or equivalently
W(\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}})\to W(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
, or

\mathbb{F}_{\mathfrak{p}}\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}
).
We fix a choice of embedding
\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}
,
and for
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/f_{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathbb{Z}}
, we let
\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}=\phi^{i}\circ\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}
where
\phi

is the Frobenius automorphism of
\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}
(sending
x
to
x^{p}
). We also fix an ordering

\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,1},\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,2},\ldots,\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,e_{\mathfrak{p}}}
of the embeddings

\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
restricting to
\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}
.
Our choice of embedding
{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}\to\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
allows us to identify
\Sigma
with
\coprod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}}\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, so that

\Sigma=\{\,\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j}\,|\,{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p},i\in\mathbb{Z}/f_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathbb{Z},1\leq j\leq e_{\mathfrak{p}}\,\}.

We also let
\Sigma_{0}=\coprod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}}\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}=\{\,\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}\,|\,{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p},i\in\mathbb{Z}/f_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathbb{Z}\,\}
,
and define a permutation
\varphi
of
\Sigma
by

\varphi(\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j})=\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j+1}
if
j\neq e_{\mathfrak{p}}
and

\varphi(\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,e_{\mathfrak{p}}})=\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i+1,1}
.

Let
K
be a finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
(in
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
), which we assume is sufficiently large to contain the images
of all
\sigma\in\Sigma
; let
{\mathcal{O}}
denote
its ring of integers,
\varpi
a uniformizer, and
E
its residue field.

We write
\vec{k}
for the element of
\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
(or
\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
or
\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
) with
\sigma
-component
k_{\sigma}
. We often abbreviate
k_{\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j}}
by
k_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j}
. For
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, we write
\vec{k}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for its image in
\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
under the canonical projection (induced by the inclusion
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}\hookrightarrow\Sigma
).
For
\sigma\in\Sigma
, we let
\vec{e}_{\sigma}
denote the standard basis vector associated to
\sigma
(so
e_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}=1
or
0
according to whether or not
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
). A special role
4
4

4

As weights of partial Hasse invariants; see §
4.1
will be played by certain elements
\vec{h}_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, defined as follows: Let
\vec{h}_{\sigma}=\nu_{\sigma}\vec{e}_{\varphi^{-1}\sigma}-\vec{e}_{\sigma}
, where
\nu_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j}=p
or
1
according to whether or not
j=1
.

For a non-zero ideal
{\mathfrak{n}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
, we write
U({\mathfrak{n}})
and
U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}})
for the following standard open compact subgroups
5
5

5

By contrast,
U_{0}({\mathfrak{n}})
(for
{\mathfrak{n}}
prime) will be defined below relative to an open compact subgroup
U
, and only used in the context of quaternion algebras; see below.
of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
:
U({\mathfrak{n}})
denotes the kernel of the
projection
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F})\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F}/{\mathfrak{n}})
and

U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}})=\left\{\left.\,{\mbox{\scriptsize$\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{{a}}&{{b}}\\
{{c}}&{{d}}\end{array}\right)$}}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F})\,\right|\,c,d-1\in{\mathfrak{n}}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F}\,\right\}.

When we consider more general open compact subgroups
U
of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
, they are often assumed to be of the form
\prod_{v}U_{v}
with each
U_{v}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})
, in which case we write
U=U_{p}U^{p}
, where
U_{p}=\prod\limits_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}}U_{\mathfrak{p}}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})
and
U^{p}=\prod\limits_{v\not\in S_{p}}U_{v}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
.

For a (non-split) quaternion algebra
B
over
F
, we let
\Sigma^{B}
denote the set of places of
F
at which
B
is ramified, and let
\Sigma^{B}_{\infty}=\Sigma^{B}\cap\Sigma_{\infty}
. We choose
F_{\sigma}
-algebra isomorphisms
B_{\sigma}\cong M_{2}(\mathbb{R})
for each
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\infty}-\Sigma^{B}
, and
B_{\sigma}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\cong M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for each
\sigma\in\Sigma^{B}_{\infty}
. We also fix a maximal order
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
in
B
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,v}
-algebra isomorphisms
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}\cong M_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})
for each finite
v\not\in\Sigma^{B}
, and write
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,(p)}
for
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
. We let
B_{\mathbb{A}}=B\otimes\mathbb{A}=B_{\infty}\times B_{\mathbf{f}}
, where
B_{\infty}=B\otimes\mathbb{R}=\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\infty}}B_{\sigma}
and
B_{\mathbf{f}}=B\otimes\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}=B\otimes_{F}{\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}\subset\prod B_{v}
, and write
B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}
for
B\otimes_{F}{\mathbb{A}}^{(p)}_{F,\mathbf{f}}\subset\prod_{v\not\in S_{p}}B_{v}
. For
\gamma\in B
, we write
\gamma_{\infty}
(resp.
\gamma_{\mathbf{f}}
,
\gamma_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}
) for its image in
B_{\infty}
(resp.
B_{\mathbf{f}}
,
B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}
). We write

\det
(resp.
{\operatorname{tr}\,}
) for the reduced norm (resp. trace) map

B\to F
, as well as its extension to maps
B_{v}\to F_{v}
,
B_{\infty}\to F_{\infty}
, etc., and again use the subscript
+
for subsets of
B
(or
B_{\infty}
) to denote those elements with totally positive image under
\det
. We again generally only consider open compact subgroups
U
of
B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
of the form
\prod_{v}U_{v}
with
U_{v}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}^{\times}
for all
v
, and write
U=U_{p}U^{p}
with
U_{p}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{B,p}^{\times}=\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}}{\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
and
U^{p}\subset\prod_{v\not\in S_{p}}{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}^{\times}\subset(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
. For
v\not\in\Sigma^{B}
such that
U_{v}={\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}^{\times}
, we let
U_{0}(v)=U\cap hUh^{-1}
, where
h
is any element of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}
such that
\det(h)
is a uniformizer in
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,v}
(so
h\in B_{v}^{\times}\subset B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
, and
U_{0}(v)
only depends up to conjugacy in
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}^{\times}
on the choice of
h
).

For any field
L
, we let
G_{L}
denote its absolute Galois group.
If
L
has characteristic different from
p
, then we let
\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}:G_{L}\to{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
denote the
p
-adic cyclotomic character (or the mod
p
cyclotomic character
G_{L}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{p}^{\times}
if clear from the context).
We use
\phi
(as above) to denote the Frobenius automorphism in
G_{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}
, as well as its lifts to automorphisms of
W(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
and its field of fractions.

If
L=F_{v}
(or a finite extension thereof), then we write
I_{L}
for the inertia subgroup of
G_{L}
. We let
{\operatorname{Frob}}_{v}
denote the
geometric
Frobenius element of
G_{F_{v}}/I_{F_{v}}=G_{{\mathcal{O}}_{F}/v}
. (In particular, if

{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
, then
{\operatorname{Frob}}_{\mathfrak{p}}=\phi^{-f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
.) We often view
G_{F_{v}}
as a subgroup of
G_{F}
via the inclusion
G_{F_{v}}\hookrightarrow G_{F}
, determined up to conjugacy in
G_{F}
by a choice of
F
-embedding
\overline{F}\hookrightarrow\overline{F}_{v}
. We let
W_{F_{v}}
denote the Weil group of
F_{v}
, i.e., the preimage of
\langle{\operatorname{Frob}}_{v}\rangle
in
G_{F_{v}}
, and normalize the Artin isomorphism
W_{F_{v}}^{{\operatorname{ab}}}\cong F_{v}^{\times}
of local class field theory so that lifts of
{\operatorname{Frob}}_{v}
correspond to uniformizers in
F_{v}^{\times}
. In particular if
v\not\in S_{p}
, then the restriction of
\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}
corresponds to
|\cdot|_{v}
(where
\varpi_{v}
has absolute value
\mathrm{Nm}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(v)^{-1}
).
We normalize the local Langlands correspondence for
\operatorname{GL}_{2}
so that the principal series representation

I(\psi_{1}|\cdot|^{1/2},\psi_{2}|\cdot|^{1/2})
of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F_{v})

(for
\psi_{2}\neq\psi_{1}|\cdot|^{\pm 1}
) corresponds to the representation
\psi_{1}\oplus\psi_{2}
of
W_{F_{v}}
(conflating continuous characters of
W_{F_{v}}
and
F_{v}^{\times}
).

For
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we let
\omega_{\sigma}:I_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{\times}
denote the fundamental character associated to
\Sigma
, defined as the composite

I_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}\mathrel{{\mathop{\hookrightarrow}\limits}}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{\times}

(where the first map is induced by the Artin isomorphism). Note in particular that
\omega_{\sigma}
depends only on the restriction of
\sigma
to
F_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}
.

For
\alpha\in F^{\times}
and
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, we let
\alpha^{\vec{k}}
denote
\prod_{\sigma}\sigma(\alpha)^{k_{\sigma}}\in{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}^{\times}
. We use the same notation similarly in various related settings; for example, if
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)}^{\times}
, then we may view
\alpha^{\vec{k}}
as an element of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
, or more generally in
R^{\times}
for an
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
R
that should be clear from the context. We sometimes further extend this notation to allow exponents in
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
for totally positive
\alpha
.

Note that if
\vec{\ell}\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
and
R
is a an
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra such that
p^{n}R=0
, then there exist continuous characters
\xi:(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to R^{\times}
such that
\xi(\alpha)=\alpha^{\vec{\ell}}
for all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
. We make use of such characters, especially for
R=E
, in various constructions and arguments. We often use
e_{\xi}
to denote a basis for a rank one
R
-module on which
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
or
(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
acts via
\xi\circ\det
. Note that if
\vec{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, then
\xi
extends uniquely to a continuous character of
\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}/F^{\times}F_{\infty,+}^{\times}
, which we also denote by
\xi
. Furthermore, we have
\xi(u)=\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}\sigma(u)^{-\ell_{\sigma}}
for all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
and
u\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
. In particular, if
R=E
, then the restriction to
I_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
of the character of
G_{F}
corresponding to
\xi
via class field theory is
\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{\sigma}^{-\ell_{\sigma}}
.

For notation related to
p
-adic Hodge theory, see §
6.2
. We mention here only our convention that
\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}:G_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
has Hodge–Tate weight
1
.

Remark 1.3.1
.

We caution that our conventions in this paper with regard to Hecke actions differ by a twist from those in the prequel
[
DS23
]
. More precisely, the definition of the action of
g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
on spaces of Hilbert modular forms in
[
DS23
, §4.2]
(for
p
unramified in
F
) involves a factor of
||\det(g)||
which is not included here, so for example, our Hecke operator
T_{v}
is
\mathrm{Nm}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(v)
times the one denoted
T_{v}
in
[
DS23
]
. The reason for this is that the normalizations in
[
DS23
]
were chosen for consistency with the more familiar setting of classical modular forms, but the ones in this paper were chosen for consistency with a more general framework of automorphic forms, including Hilbert modular forms over
\mathbb{C}
of non-paritious weight, of which we make repeated use.
In the same vein, our representation
D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}}
of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F}/p)
is the twist by the character
\prod_{\sigma}(\sigma\circ\det^{-1})
of the one denoted
V_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}
in
[
DS23
]
.

2.
Hilbert modular forms over
\mathbb{C}

2.1.
Hilbert modular varieties over
\mathbb{C}

For an open compact subgroup
U
of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
,
the Hilbert modular variety of level
U
over
\mathbb{C}
is defined as the quotient

Y_{U}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F)\backslash\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F})/UU_{\infty}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F)_{+}\backslash(\mathfrak{H}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}\times\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})/U),

where
U_{\infty}=\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\infty}}{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}\operatorname{SO}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F_{\infty})
,
\mathfrak{H}
is the complex upper half-plane, and
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F)_{+}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})_{+}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}

acts componentwise on
\mathfrak{H}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
via linear fractional transformations. Note that we have a natural right action of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
by right-multiplication on the inverse system of the
Y_{U}
(for varying
U
). More precisely, if

U
and
U^{\prime}
are open compact subgroups of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
,
and
h\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
is such that
U^{\prime}\subset hUh^{-1}
,
then we have the map
\rho_{h}:Y_{U^{\prime}}\to Y_{U}
induced by

(\tau,gU^{\prime})\mapsto(\tau,ghU)
, and these satisfy

\rho_{h}\circ\rho_{h^{\prime}}=\rho_{h^{\prime}h}

(for
U^{\prime\prime}\subset h^{\prime}U^{\prime}h^{\prime-1}
).
Furthermore the determinant map induces a bijection between the set of connected components of
Y_{U}
and the set

{\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\times}/F^{\times}F_{\infty,+}^{\times}\det(U),

compatibly with the obvious action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
.

If
U
is sufficiently small, then
Y_{U}
inherits the
structure of a
d
-dimensional complex manifold from
\mathfrak{H}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
.
Furthermore
Y_{U}
also naturally has the structure of a quasi-projective variety over
\mathbb{Q}
, but we shall be primarily interested in the special fibre of an integral model over a (sufficiently large) finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, and only in the case when the level
U
is prime to
p
(see §
3.1
, below). To facilitate statements relating the complex and
p
-adic settings, we shall often view
Y_{U}
as a variety over
{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}
.

The Hilbert modular variety
Y_{U}
admits a minimal compactification
Y_{U}^{\min}
(see for example
[
Dia24
]
).
The complement of the open immersion

i:Y_{U}\hookrightarrow Y_{U}^{\min}
is the finite set of

cusps

C_{U}
of
Y_{U}
, in canonical bijection with

B(F)_{+}\backslash\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})/U

where
B(F)_{+}
denotes the subgroup of upper-triangular
matrices in
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F)_{+}
. We let
j:C_{U}\hookrightarrow Y_{U}^{\min}
denote the closed immersion complementing
i
.

The description of the completion of

Y_{U}^{\min}
at each cusp is given for example in
[
Dia23
, §7.2]
. In particular if
U=U({\mathfrak{n}})
for
some non-zero ideal
{\mathfrak{n}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
, then the completion

{\mathcal{O}}_{Y_{U}^{\min},x}^{\wedge}=(i_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y_{U}})_{x}^{\wedge}

at each cusp
x
of
Y_{U}^{\min}
is isomorphic to

\left\{\left.\sum_{m\in{\mathfrak{n}}^{-1}M_{x,+}\cup\{0\}}r_{m}q^{m}\,\right|\,\mbox{$r_{m}\in\mathbb{C}$, $r_{m}=r_{\nu m}$ for all $\nu\in V_{{\mathfrak{n}},+}$, $m\in{\mathfrak{n}}^{-1}M_{x,+}\cup\{0\}$}\right\},

where
M_{x}
is a certain oriented invertible
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-module and

V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}=\ker({\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times}\to({\mathcal{O}}_{F}/{\mathfrak{n}})^{\times})
.
Similarly if
U=U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),

then we have the same description of each
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y_{U}^{\min},x}^{\wedge}
, but with
{\mathfrak{n}}^{-1}M_{x}
replaced by
M_{x}

and
V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
replaced by
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times}
.

2.2.
Hilbert modular forms over
\mathbb{C}

Suppose now that
\vec{k}=(k_{\sigma})_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\infty}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
. For
g=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})_{+}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{H}
,
let
j(g,\tau)=\det(g)^{-1/2}(c\tau+d)\in\mathbb{C}
.

If
U
is sufficiently small,
then we have the complex line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}={\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},U}
on

Y_{U}
defined by (holomorphic sections of)

\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F)_{+}\backslash(\mathfrak{H}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}\times\mathbb{C}\times\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})/U),

the action of
\gamma\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{+}(F)

being given by the formula

\gamma\cdot(\tau,z,g)=(\gamma_{\infty}(\tau),j(\gamma_{\infty},\tau)^{\vec{k}},\gamma_{\mathbf{f}}g)

where
j(\gamma_{\infty},\tau)^{\vec{k}}=\prod_{\sigma}j(\sigma(\gamma),\tau_{\sigma})^{k_{\sigma}}
for
\tau=(\tau_{\sigma})_{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{H}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
.

A section
f\in M_{\vec{k}}(U,\mathbb{C}):=H^{0}(Y_{U},{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}})
is called a
Hilbert modular form
of
weight

\vec{k}
and level
U
. The natural action of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
on the inverse system of
Y_{U}
gives rise to
a left action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
on

{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k}}=\varinjlim_{U}M_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathbb{C}}).

More precisely for
h\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
such that

U^{\prime}\subset hUh^{-1}
, we have the isomorphism

\rho_{h}^{*}{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},U}\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},U^{\prime}}

induced by
(\tau,gU^{\prime},z)\mapsto(\tau,ghU,z)
, and
hence (injective) maps
M_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathbb{C}})\to M_{\vec{k}}(U^{\prime},{\mathbb{C}})

which yield the action. Furthermore we may identify

M_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathbb{C}})
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k}}^{U}
.

By the Koecher Principle, the direct image

{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\min}:=i_{*}{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}
is a coherent sheaf
on
Y_{U}^{\min}
, and in fact a line bundle if
\vec{k}
is parallel (i.e., if
k_{\sigma}
is independent of
\sigma
). The completion of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\min}
at each
cusp of
Y_{U}^{\min}
is as described in
[
Dia23
, §7.2]
.
In particular if
x
is a cusp of
Y_{U({\mathfrak{n}})}^{\min}
,
then
({\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\min})^{\wedge}_{x}
is isomorphic
6
6

6

The isomorphism depends on a choice of basis for a certain one-dimensional complex vector space depending on the cusp representative and the weight
\vec{k}
; see
[
Dia23
]
for a more canonical description.
to

\left\{\left.\sum_{m\in{\mathfrak{n}}^{-1}M_{x,+}\cup\{0\}}r_{m}q^{m}\,\right|\,\mbox{$r_{m}\in\mathbb{C}$, $r_{m}=\nu^{\vec{k}/2}r_{\nu m}$ for all $\nu\in V_{{\mathfrak{n}},+}$, $m\in{\mathfrak{n}}^{-1}M_{x,+}\cup\{0\}$}\right\}

as an
{\mathcal{O}}_{Y_{U({\mathfrak{n}})}^{\min},x}^{\wedge}
-module
(where
\nu^{\vec{k}/2}=\prod_{\sigma}\sigma(\nu)^{k_{\sigma}/2}
). Again the description
with
U({\mathfrak{n}})
replaced by
U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}})
is the same, but with

{\mathfrak{n}}^{-1}M_{x}
replaced by
M_{x}
and
V_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
replaced by
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times}
. Note in particular that
r_{0}=0
unless
\vec{k}
is parallel.
We recall also the
q
-expansion Principle, which states that if

C
is any set of cusps of
Y_{U}^{\min}
containing at least one cusp on each connected component, then the natural map

M_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathbb{C}})\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{x\in C}({\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\min})^{\wedge}_{x}

is injective.

We call
f\in M_{\vec{k}}(U,\mathbb{C})
a
cusp form
if the
coefficient
r_{0}
of its
q
-expansion at each cusp vanishes.
Note that this holds automatically if
\vec{k}
is not parallel,
so letting
S_{\vec{k}}(U,\mathbb{C})
denote the space of cusp forms of weight
\vec{k}
and level
U
, we have

S_{\vec{k}}(U,\mathbb{C})=H^{0}(Y_{U}^{\min},{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\operatorname{sub}})\subset H^{0}(Y_{U}^{\min},{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\min})=M_{\vec{k}}(U,\mathbb{C}),

where
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\operatorname{sub}}=\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\min}\to j_{*}j^{*}{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\min}
) if
\vec{k}
is parallel, and

{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\operatorname{sub}}={\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\min}
otherwise.

The action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})

on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k}}
restricts to one on

{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{k}}=\varinjlim_{U}S_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathbb{C}}),

and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{k}}=0
unless
k_{\sigma}\geq 1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, in which case it may be described in terms of
cuspidal automorphic representations of weight
\vec{k}
of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F})
. More precisely, for an integer
k
greater than
(or equal to)
1
, let
D^{\prime}_{k}
be the irreducible unitary (limit of)
discrete series representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
of weight
k
.
We then have

{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{k}}\cong\bigoplus_{\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{k}}}\pi_{\mathbf{f}},

where
\mathcal{C}_{\vec{k}}
is the set of cuspidal
automorphic representations
\pi=\pi_{\infty}\otimes\pi_{\mathbf{f}}=\otimes^{\prime}_{v}\pi_{v}
of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
such that
\pi_{\sigma}\cong D^{\prime}_{k_{\sigma}}
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\infty}
.

2.3.
The Jacquet–Langlands Correspondence

We shall also make use of automorphic representations
of
B_{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}
, where
B
is a (non-split)
quaternion algebra over
F
. Recall that
\Sigma^{B}
denotes
the set of places of
F
at which
B
is ramified,
and
\Sigma^{B}_{\infty}=\Sigma^{B}\cap\Sigma_{\infty}
.
Recall also that for each (finite or infinite) place
v\not\in\Sigma^{B}
, we have chosen an isomorphism

B_{v}\cong M_{2}(F_{v})
of
F_{v}
-algebras, as well as
an isomorphism

B_{\sigma}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\cong M_{2}(\mathbb{C})
of
\mathbb{C}
-algebras for
for each

\sigma\in\Sigma^{B}_{\infty}
. For
k\geq 2
, let
D_{k}
denote the representation of
B_{\sigma}^{\times}
defined by
\det^{(2-k)/2}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{k-2}\mathbb{C}^{2}
. (Note that
\det(B_{\sigma}^{\times})\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{\times}
if
\sigma\in\Sigma^{B}_{\infty}
, so this is well-defined.)

Recall that if
v\in\Sigma^{B}
, then the local Jacquet-Langlands correspondence (see
[
GJ79
, §8]
) defines a bijection
{\operatorname{JL}\,}_{v}
between the sets of (isomorphism classes of) smooth irreducible representations of
B_{v}^{\times}
and discrete series representations of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F_{v})
. In particular, if
k\geq 2

then
{\operatorname{JL}\,}(D_{k})=D_{k}^{\prime}
.

Suppose now that
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
is such that

k_{\sigma}\geq 1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\infty}
and

k_{\sigma}\geq 2
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\infty}^{B}
.
We let
\mathcal{C}_{\vec{k}}^{B}
denote the set of all infinite-dimensional automorphic representations
\pi=\otimes^{\prime}_{v}\pi_{v}
of
B_{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}=(B\otimes\mathbb{A})^{\times}
such
that
\pi_{\sigma}\cong D_{k_{\sigma}}
if
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\infty}^{B}
and
\pi_{\sigma}\cong D^{\prime}_{k_{\sigma}}
if
\sigma\not\in\Sigma_{\infty}^{B}
.

The (global) Jacquet–Langlands correspondence can then be stated as follows:

Theorem 2.3.1
.

There is an injection
{\operatorname{JL}\,}_{\vec{k}}^{B}:\mathcal{C}^{B}_{\vec{k}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\vec{k}}
such that if

\Pi=\otimes^{\prime}_{v}\Pi_{v}\in\mathcal{C}^{B}_{\vec{k}}
, then

{\operatorname{JL}\,}_{\vec{k}}^{B}(\Pi)=\pi=\otimes^{\prime}_{v}\pi_{v}
is characterized by

•

\pi_{v}\cong\Pi_{v}
for all
v\not\in\Sigma^{B}
;

•

\pi_{v}\cong{\operatorname{JL}\,}_{v}(\Pi_{v})
for all
v\in\Sigma^{B}
.

Furthermore the image of
{\operatorname{JL}\,}_{\vec{k}}^{B}
is the set of all

\pi=\otimes^{\prime}_{v}\pi_{v}
such that
\pi_{v}
is a discrete
representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F_{v})
for all
v\in\Sigma^{B}
.

2.4.
Automorphic forms on totally definite quaternion algebras over
\mathbb{C}

We have the following explicit description of
\mathcal{C}_{\vec{k}}^{B}
in the case that
B
is a totally definite quaternion algebra, i.e.,

\Sigma_{\infty}^{B}=\Sigma_{\infty}
. For an open compact
subgroup
U
of
B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}:=(B\otimes{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbf{f}})^{\times}
, let

M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,\mathbb{C})=\{\,f:B_{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}\to D_{\vec{k}}\,|\,\mbox{$f(\gamma xu)=u_{\infty}^{-1}f(x)$ for all $\gamma\in B^{\times}$,
$x\in B_{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}$,
$u\in B_{\infty}^{\times}U$}\,\},

where
D_{\vec{k}}=\otimes_{\sigma}D_{k_{\sigma}}

(and we assume
k_{\sigma}\geq 2
for all
\sigma
).
Note that restriction identifies
M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,\mathbb{C})

with

\{\,f:B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}\to D_{\vec{k}}\,|\,\mbox{$f(\gamma_{\mathbf{f}}xu)=\gamma_{\infty}f(x)$ for all $\gamma\in B^{\times}$, $x\in B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}$,
$u\in U$}\,\}.

Furthermore if
U
is sufficiently small, then
B^{\times}\cap U
is contained in
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,+}^{\times}
, and hence acts trivially on
D_{\vec{k}}
. We may then identify
M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,\mathbb{C})

with
H^{0}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k}})
, where
Y_{U}^{B}
is the finite
set

B^{\times}\backslash B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}/U\cong B^{\times}\backslash B_{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}/B_{\infty}^{\times}U
,
and
\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k}}
is the sheaf on
Y_{U}^{B}
defined by

B^{\times}\backslash(D_{\vec{k}}\times B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}/U)
.

Just as for Hilbert modular forms, we have a natural action
of
B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
on the direct limit

{\mathcal{M}}^{B}_{\vec{k}}:=\varinjlim_{U}M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathbb{C}}).

If
k_{\sigma}=2
for all
\sigma
(so
D_{\vec{k}}
is the
trivial representation), then we define

I^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathbb{C}})\subset M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\mathbb{C}})

to be the subspace of functions
B_{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}

factoring through the map

B_{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}\longrightarrow F^{\times}\backslash{\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\times}/F_{\infty,+}^{\times}\det(U)

(where we use
\det
to denote the reduced norm);
otherwise let
I_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\mathbb{C}})=0
.
Letting
{\mathcal{I}}_{\vec{k}}^{B}=\varinjlim_{U}I_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\mathbb{C}})

and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{k}}^{B}=\varinjlim_{U}S_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\mathbb{C}})
,
where
S_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\mathbb{C}})=M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\mathbb{C}})/I_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\mathbb{C}})
, we have

{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{k}}^{B}={\mathcal{M}}^{B}_{\vec{k}}/{\mathcal{I}}^{B}_{\vec{k}}\cong\bigoplus_{\Pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{k}}^{B}}\Pi_{\mathbf{f}}

as representations of
B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
. Furthermore we may identify
M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\mathbb{C}})
(resp.
S_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\mathbb{C}}))
with

({\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k}}^{B})^{U}
(resp.
({\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{k}}^{B})^{U}
).

2.5.
Automorphic forms on Shimura curves over
\mathbb{C}

Suppose now that
|\Sigma^{B}_{\infty}|=d-1
, and let
\sigma_{0}
be the unique element of
\Sigma_{\infty}-\Sigma_{\infty}^{B}
.
Let
Y_{U}^{B}
denote the Shimura curve

B_{+}^{\times}\backslash((\mathfrak{H}\times B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times})/U),

where
B_{+}^{\times}
acts on
\mathfrak{H}
via its embedding in
B_{\sigma_{0}}^{\times}
(for which we fixed an isomorphism with

\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
). Thus
Y_{U}^{B}
is a compact Riemann surface, and
if
U
is sufficiently small, then the stabilizer of
any point in
(\mathfrak{H}\times B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times})/U
under the action
of
B_{+}^{\times}
is contained in
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,+}^{\times}
.

We view
D_{\vec{k}}=\otimes_{\sigma}D_{k_{\sigma}}
as
a representation of
B_{\infty,+}^{\times}
, and hence
B_{+}^{\times}
,
where
D_{k_{\sigma_{0}}}=\det^{(2-k_{\sigma_{0}})/2}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{\sigma_{0}}-2}\mathbb{C}^{2}
with its action of

B_{\sigma_{0},+}^{\times}\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{R})_{+}
.
For sufficiently small
U
, we have a locally constant
sheaf on
Y_{U}^{B}
defined by (sections of)

B^{\times}_{+}\backslash((\mathfrak{H}\times B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}\times D_{\vec{k}})/U),

which we denote by
{\mathcal{V}}^{B}_{\vec{k},U}
.

Now consider the cohomology groups

M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\mathbb{C}}):=H^{1}(Y_{U}^{B},{\mathcal{V}}^{B}_{\vec{k},U})
.
As in the case of Hilbert modular varieties and forms, for

h\in B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
and
U
,
U^{\prime}
such that
U^{\prime}\subset hUh^{-1}
, we have morphisms
\rho_{h}:Y_{U^{\prime}}\to Y_{U}

and
\rho_{h}^{*}{\mathcal{V}}_{\vec{k},U}^{B}\to{\mathcal{V}}_{\vec{k},U^{\prime}}^{B}
,
yielding maps
M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathbb{C}})\to M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U^{\prime},{\mathbb{C}})

giving rise to an action of
B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
on

{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k}}^{B}:=\varinjlim_{U}M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathbb{C}}).

We then have

{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k}}^{B}\cong\bigoplus_{\Pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{k}}^{B}}\left(\Pi_{\mathbf{f}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}\right)

as representations of
B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}

(as in
[
DT94
, (4)]
, for example).
Again we have the identification
({\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k}}^{B})^{U}=M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathbb{C}})
.

3.
Hilbert modular forms over
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras

3.1.
Hilbert modular varieties over
{\mathcal{O}}

Let
{\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}=F\otimes\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{(p)}
, where

\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^{(p)}=\prod_{\ell\neq p}\mathbb{Z}_{\ell}

denotes the prime-to-
p
completion of
\mathbb{Z}
, so

{\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}={\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}\times\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}}F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
.
Let
U
be a sufficiently small open compact subgroup of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
containing
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})
, so that

U=U_{p}U^{p}
where
U_{p}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})
and
U^{p}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
.

Let
\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{U}
be the
{\mathcal{O}}
-scheme representing
isomorphism classes of data
(A,\iota,\lambda,\eta,{\mathcal{F}}^{\bullet})

as in
[
Dia25
, §2.2]
, i.e., abelian schemes with
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-action and suitable quasi-polarization, level
U^{p}
-structure
and Pappas-Rapoport filtrations. Recall that this is a smooth
quasi-projective scheme of relative dimension
d
over
{\mathcal{O}}

equipped with an action of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
, and the quotient
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}={\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}\backslash\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{U}
is a smooth model for the Hilbert modular variety
Y_{U}
,
i.e., we have an identification of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}}

with
Y_{U,\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}}
as complex manifolds.
For
U
,
U^{\prime}
as above and

h\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
such that
U^{\prime}\subset hUh^{-1}
,
we have a morphism
\widetilde{\rho}_{h}:\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{U^{\prime}}\to\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{U}
, and these morphisms define an action of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
on the inverse system of the
\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{U}
(for varying
U
as above). Furthermore the
morphism
\widetilde{\rho}_{h}
is compatible with the action of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
, inducing a morphism
\rho_{h}:{\mathcal{Y}}_{U^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}
which is compatible (in the obvious sense) with the one previously so denoted,

We will also make use of the minimal compactification
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}^{\min}
of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}
, as defined in
[
Dia23
, §7.2]
. The reduced complement of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}
is a finite
{\mathcal{O}}
-scheme
{\mathcal{Z}}_{U}
, and we assume
{\mathcal{O}}
is sufficiently large that its components (as well as those of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}^{\min}
) are geometrically connected. Thus
{\mathcal{Z}}_{U}
is a disjoint union of copies of
{\operatorname{Spec}\,}({\mathcal{O}})
, which we also call cusps, corresponding to the cusps of
Y_{U}^{\min}
.
The description of the completion of

{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}^{\min}
at each such cusp is the same as for
Y_{U}^{\min}
, but with
\mathbb{C}
replaced by
{\mathcal{O}}
.

We note also that the connected components of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}
(or equivalently
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}^{\min}
) correspond to those of
Y_{U}
.

3.2.
Hilbert modular forms over
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras

For
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, we have the automorphic line bundle

\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}=\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma}{\mathcal{L}}_{\sigma}^{k_{\sigma}+m_{\sigma}}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}^{m_{\theta}}

on
\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{U}
, where

{\mathcal{L}}_{\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j}}
is the subquotient

{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}}^{(j)}/{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}}^{(j-1)}

of the Pappas-Rapoport filtration on the
\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}
-component
of the Hodge bundle of the universal abelian scheme over

\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{U}
, and similarly
{\mathcal{M}}_{\sigma}
is a
certain invertible subquotient of its relative de Rham cohomology (see
[
Dia25
, §2.3]
).
The line bundles
\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}
are equipped with a natural action of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
over its action on

\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{U}
, i.e., isomorphisms

\alpha_{\mu}:{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}\psi_{\mu}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}

for
\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
satisfying
\alpha_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}=\psi_{\mu^{\prime}}^{*}(\alpha_{\mu})\circ\alpha_{\mu^{\prime}}
for
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{+}
, where
\psi_{\mu}
and
\psi_{\mu^{\prime}}
are the underlying automorphisms of
\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{U}
.

If
\vec{k}+2\vec{m}
is parallel (in the sense that

k_{\sigma}+2m_{\sigma}
is independent of
\sigma
), then this action defines descent data to a line bundle
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}
on
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}
.
Furthermore for any
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
R
in which the image of

\mu^{\vec{k}+2\vec{m}}:=\prod_{\sigma}\sigma(\mu)^{k_{\sigma}+2m_{\sigma}}

is trivial for all
\mu\in U\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times}
, we similarly
obtain a line bundle
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}
on
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,R}
.
Note that if
p^{N}R=0
for some
N\geq 1
, then this condition holds for all sufficiently small
U
.
We then define the space of
Hilbert modular forms
over
R
of
weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
and
level
U
to be the
R
-module

M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R):=H^{0}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U,R},{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}).

For the purpose of arguments involving lifting to characteristic zero and applying the Jacquet-Langlands correspondence, we shall also need integral structures on spaces of Hilbert modular forms for which the
k_{\sigma}
differ in parity.
To that end, suppose that
K
is sufficiently large to contain
\sigma(\mu)^{1/2}\in{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}^{\times}\subset\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
for all
\mu\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
,
\sigma\in\Sigma
. We may then define another action of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
on
\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\vec{k},\vec{0}}
by setting
\alpha^{\prime}_{\mu}=\mu^{-\vec{k}/2}\alpha_{\mu}
, yielding well-defined descent data and hence a line bundle on
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}
which we denote by
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},{\mathcal{O}}}
. This is compatible with our previous notation in the sense that the line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}}
over
{{\mathcal{Y}}}_{U,\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}}
may be identified with the base-change of the line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}
on
Y_{U}
(defined over
{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}
). For any
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
R
, we define

M_{\vec{k}}(U,R):=H^{0}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U,R},{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},R}).

The relation between the
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
and

M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
will be described below.

3.3.
q
-expansions

Again letting
i:{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,R}\to{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,R}^{\min}
denote the open immersion of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}
in its minimal compactification, the direct images
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}^{\min}=i_{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},R}^{\min}=i_{*}{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},R}

are coherent sheaves on
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,R}^{\min}
. Furthermore their completion at cusps have a similar description to that of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k}}^{\min}
, but with
\mathbb{C}
replaced by
{\mathcal{O}}
(and
\vec{k}/2
replaced by
-\vec{m}
). Note however that formation of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}^{\min}
(or
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},R}^{\min}
) does not commute with base-change. For example,
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},{\mathcal{O}}}^{\min}

is a line bundle if and only if
\vec{k}
(or equivalently
\vec{m}
) is parallel, but
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}^{\min}

is a line bundle for all
\vec{k}
,
\vec{m}
and sufficiently small
U
.

The
q
-expansion Principle holds in this setting as well, i.e., if
C
contains at least one cusp on each connected component of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}
, then the natural maps

M_{\vec{k,\vec{m}}}(U,R)\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{x\in C}({\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}^{\min})^{\wedge}_{x}\quad\mbox{and}\quad M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{x\in C}({\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},R}^{\min})^{\wedge}_{x}

are injective.

We shall often assume
U=U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}})
or
U({\mathfrak{n}})
for some
{\mathfrak{n}}
prime to
p
, and take
C
to be the set of cusps at
\infty
, in the sense of
[
Dia24
, §6.4]
. Such cusps have the form

B(F)_{+}{\mbox{\scriptsize$\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{{t}}&{{0}}\\
{{0}}&{{1}}\end{array}\right)$}}U
for
t\in(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
(or equivalently
B({\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)})_{+}{\mbox{\scriptsize$\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{{t}}&{{0}}\\
{{0}}&{{1}}\end{array}\right)$}}U^{p}
)
for
t\in(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}

and are in bijection with the set of connected components of
Y_{U}^{\min}
. If
x\in C
is the cusp associated to
t
, then the oriented invertible
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-module
M_{x}
is isomorphic to

t^{-1}{\mathfrak{d}}^{-1}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F}\cap F
, where
{\mathfrak{d}}
is the different of
F
. We then write
7
7

7

Here as well the coefficient is viewed as an element of
R
after choosing a basis for a certain invertible
R
-module; see
[
Dia23
, §7.2]

r_{m}^{t}(f)
for the coefficient of
q^{m}
in the
q
-expansion of
f
at the cusp corresponding to
t
(with
r_{m}^{t}
understood to be
0
if, for example,
U=U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}})
and
m\not\in M_{x,+}\cup\{0\}
). The dependence on the choice of
t
is given by the formula

(1)

r_{m}^{\alpha tu}(f)=\alpha^{\vec{m}}r_{\alpha m}^{t}(f)

for
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
,

u\in(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}^{(p)}_{F})^{\times}
if
U=U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}})
,
(and further that
u\equiv 1\bmod{\mathfrak{n}}
if
U=U({\mathfrak{n}})
), where
\alpha tu
is understood as an element of
(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
(see
[
Dia24
, (6.1)]
).

We define the spaces of cusp forms
S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
and
S_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
as in the complex setting, via vanishing of the constant term of the
q
-expansion at all cusps. We can again identify
S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
with the
R
-module of sections of a coherent subsheaf
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}^{\operatorname{sub}}
of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}^{\min}
; in this generality the kernel of a (possibly trivial) morphism to
j_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{U,R}}
(writing
j
for the closed immersion
{\mathcal{Z}}_{U,R}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,R}^{\min}
). Similarly we have

S_{\vec{k}}(U,R)=H^{0}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U,R},{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},R}^{\operatorname{sub}})
where
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},R}^{\operatorname{sub}}=\ker({\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},R}^{\min}\to j_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}_{U,R}})
. Note that, unlike
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}^{\min}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},R}^{\min}
, formation of
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}^{\operatorname{sub}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},R}^{\operatorname{sub}}
does commute with base-change.

3.4.
Hecke operators

Suppose now that
U
and
U^{\prime}
are sufficiently small open compact subgroups containing

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})
, and that
h\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
is
such that
U^{\prime}\subset hUh^{-1}
. We again have a canonical isomorphism

\rho_{h}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}

of line bundles on
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U^{\prime},R}
(where
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}
is defined as above with
U
replaced by
U^{\prime}
), giving rise to an
R
-linear morphism

M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)\longrightarrow M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U^{\prime},R).

Furthermore (assuming
\vec{k}+2\vec{m}
is parallel or that

p^{N}R=0
for some
N>0
), these morphisms define an action of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
on

{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R):=\varinjlim_{U}M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R),

restricting to one on
{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R):=\varinjlim_{U}S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
.
If
U
is such that
\mu^{\vec{k}+2\vec{m}}
is trivial in
R

for all
\mu\in U\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times}
, then we may identify
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
with
({\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R))^{U^{p}}
; more generally for any
U
containing
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})
, we define
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
to be
({\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R))^{U^{p}}
, and similarly for
S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
. The space

M_{{\operatorname{tot}}}(U,R):=\bigoplus_{\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}}M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)

then forms an
R
-algebra in which

S_{{\operatorname{tot}}}(U,R):=\bigoplus S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
is an ideal.

We may similarly define an action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})

on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k}}(R):=\varinjlim_{U}M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
for any
\vec{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Sigma}
and
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
R
. As above, this restricts to an action on
{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{k}}(R):=\varinjlim_{U}S_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
, and we recover the previously defined

M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
(and
S_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
) by taking
U^{p}
-invariants.

For any open compact subgroups
U
,
U^{\prime}
of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
containing
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})
, and

h\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
, we define the

R
-linear double coset
operator

[U^{\prime}hU]:M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)=({\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R))^{U}\longrightarrow({\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R))^{U^{\prime}}=M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U^{\prime},R)

as
\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}
, where
U^{\prime}hU=\coprod_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}U
, and the action of the
h_{i}
on

{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R)
is the one defined above.
(For
U
,
U^{\prime}
sufficiently small, this can be written
as a suitably normalized composite of pull-back and trace maps,
as in
[
DS23
, §4.3]
, but see Remark
1.3.1

for a discussion of the difference between the conventions
here and in
[
DS23
]
.)
Note that the operator
[U^{\prime}hU]
restricts to a map
S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)\longrightarrow S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U^{\prime},R)
.

In particular if
v
is a prime not dividing
p
and

U
contains
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})
, then we have the usual Hecke operators

T_{v}=U{\mbox{\scriptsize$\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{{\varpi_{v}}}&{{0}}\\
{{0}}&{{1}}\end{array}\right)$}}U\quad\mbox{and}\quad S_{v}=U{\mbox{\scriptsize$\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{{\varpi_{v}}}&{{0}}\\
{{0}}&{{\varpi_{v}}}\end{array}\right)$}}U

as endomorphisms of
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
and
M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)

(where
\varpi_{v}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,v}
is any uniformizer at
v
).
If
U=U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}})
or
U({\mathfrak{n}})
, the effect of
T_{v}
on Fourier expansions at cusps at
\infty
is given by
[
Dia24
, Prop 6.5.1]
, which translated to our conventions becomes the
formula

r_{m}^{t}(T_{v}f)=\mathrm{Nm}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(v)r_{m}^{\varpi_{v}t}(f)+r_{m}^{\varpi_{v}^{-1}t}(S_{v}f).

In the case that

U=U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}):=\left\{\,\left.{\mbox{\scriptsize$\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{{a}}&{{b}}\\
{{c}}&{{d}}\end{array}\right)$}}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F})\,\right|\,\mbox{$c_{v}\in{\mathfrak{n}}{\mathcal{O}}_{F,v}$ for all $v$}\,\right\}

for some ideal
{\mathfrak{n}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
prime to
p
,
we also define
T_{v}
as above for
v|{\mathfrak{n}}
.
The effect on Fourier expansions at cusps at
\infty
is then
given by the formula

r_{m}^{t}(T_{v}f)=\mathrm{Nm}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(v)r_{m}^{\varpi_{v}t}(f).

Furthermore if
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
and

(2)

\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\min(m_{\sigma}+1,m_{\sigma}+k_{\sigma})\geq 0,

then we have the endomorphism
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
of

M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
defined as in
[
Dia25
, §5.4]

for arbitrary
U
containing
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})
.
More precisely, assuming (
2
)
instead of
[
Dia25
, (54)]
, we may replace

\psi^{*}
by
p^{f_{\mathfrak{p}}}\psi^{*}
in the definition of
the morphism
[
Dia25
, (55)]
in order
to conform to the conventions of this paper.
Similarly if
\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(k_{\sigma}+2m_{\sigma})\geq 0
, then the operator
S_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is given
by
\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\vec{k}+2\vec{m}}S_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
,
where
p^{-f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\vec{1}-\vec{k}-2\vec{m}}

is replaced by
\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{-\vec{k}-2\vec{m}}

in the definition of
S_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
in
[
Dia24
, §6.7]
.
Translating the formula in
[
Dia24
, Prop. 6.8.1]

for the effect of
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
on
q
-expansions then gives

r_{m}^{t}(T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}f)=\epsilon\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\vec{m}+\vec{1}}r_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}m}^{x^{-1}t}(f)+\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\vec{k}+\vec{m}}r_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{-1}m}^{xt}(S_{\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}}f),

where
\epsilon=p^{f_{\mathfrak{p}}}\mathrm{Nm}(\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})^{-1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}^{\times}
and
x=\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{(p)}
. In particular
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
restricts to an endomorphism of
S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
.

We also have the usual Hecke operator
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
on

M_{\vec{k}}(U,K)
(assuming also now that

\sigma(\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}})^{1/2}\in K
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
),
whose effect on
q
-expansions is given by the same formula as above, but with
\vec{m}
replaced by
-\vec{k}/2

(with
S_{\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}}
being the usual
S_{\mathfrak{p}}
). Note in
particular that it does not preserve integrality of
q
-expansions, but that
T_{\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}}:=p^{-f_{\mathfrak{p}}}\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{\vec{k}/2}T_{\mathfrak{p}}
does,
provided
\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}(k_{\sigma}-1)\geq 0
.
Therefore it follows from the
q
-expansion Principle that
(under this assumption on
\vec{k}
)
T_{\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}}
restricts
to an endomorphism of
M_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathcal{O}})
(and
of course
S_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathcal{O}})
) satisfying

r_{m}^{t}(T_{\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}}f)=r_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}m}^{x^{-1}t}(f)+p^{-f_{\mathfrak{p}}}\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\vec{k}}r_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{-1}m}^{xt}(S_{{\mathfrak{p}}}f).

Finally we recall that all the operators defined above commute
whenever defined.
See in particular
[
Dia24
, Cor. 6.8.2]
for the case of

T_{v}
and
T_{v^{\prime}}
when
v
and
v^{\prime}
are in
S_{p}
.

3.5.
Twisting

We now explain how the spaces
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
for varying
\vec{m}
(and fixed
\vec{k}
) are related.

Recall from
[
Dia23
, Prop. 3.2.2]
that the action of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{0},\vec{\ell}}(R)
factors
through the determinant, and as a representation of

({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
is isomorphic to the smooth
co-induction of the character
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}\to R^{\times}

defined by
\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{\vec{\ell}}
.

It follows (as in
[
DS23
, §4.6]
) that if

\xi:({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to R^{\times}

is a continuous character such that
8
8

8

Note that such characters always exist with
R=E
sufficiently large.

\xi(\alpha)=\alpha^{\vec{\ell}}
for all

\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{F,(p),+}
, then the corresponding eigenspace

\{\,f\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{0},\vec{\ell}}(R)\,|\,\mbox{$gf=\xi(\det(g))f$ for all $g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})$}\,\}

is free of rank one over
R
. Multiplication by a basis
element thus defines a
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
-equivariant isomorphism

{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R)(\xi\circ\det)\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k},\vec{\ell}+\vec{m}}(R)
.
In particular if
\xi
is
trivial on
\det(U^{p})
, then we obtain an isomorphism

(3)

e_{\xi}:M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}M_{\vec{k},\vec{\ell}+\vec{m}}(U,R)

such that
T_{v}(e_{\xi}f)=e_{\xi}\xi(\varpi_{v})T_{v}(f)
and
S_{v}(e_{\xi}f)=e_{\xi}\xi(\varpi_{v})^{2}S_{v}(f)
for all
v\nmid p
such that
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})\subset U
. This holds for example
if
U=U({\mathfrak{n}})
and
\xi
is trivial on
V_{{\mathfrak{n}},+}
, in which
case we may choose
e_{\xi}
so that its effect on
q
-expansions is given by
the formula

r_{m}^{t}(e_{\xi}f)=\xi(t)r_{m}^{t}(f).

We may similarly relate the spaces
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
and

M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
. (Recall the implicit assumption that either
\vec{k}+2\vec{m}
is parallel or that
p^{N}R=0
for some
N>0
in order to define
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
.) For
U
sufficiently small that
\mu^{\vec{m}+\vec{k}/2}
is trivial in
R
, we may write
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}={\mathcal{L}}_{\vec{k},R}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}
for a line bundle
{\mathcal{N}}={\mathcal{N}}_{U}
on
Y_{U,R}
, and define in the usual way an action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
on

\varinjlim_{U}H^{0}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U,R},{\mathcal{N}}_{U})
. The same argument as the proof of
[
Dia23
, Prop. 3.2.2]
then yields the same description of the representation as an inflation of a smooth
co-induction, but with
\vec{\ell}
replaced by

\vec{m}+\vec{k}/2
.

Suppose now that

\xi:({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to R^{\times}

is a continuous character
such that

\xi(\alpha)=\alpha^{\vec{m}+\vec{k}/2}.

for all

\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
.
(Again such characters necessarily exist with
R=E
, possibly after enlarging
E
, but
note that they do not extend to continuous characters of

{\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\times}/F^{\times}F_{\infty,+}^{\times}
unless

\vec{k}\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
.)
As above we can twist by a basis
e_{\xi}

for the corresponding eigenspace to obtain
a
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
-equivariant isomorphism

{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k}}(R)(\xi\circ\det)\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R)
.
It follows that for sufficiently small
U
containing

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})
, we have an isomorphism

(4)

e_{\xi}:M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)

with the same behavior as above with respect to Hecke operators and
q
-expansions.

3.6.
Totally definite quaternionic forms over
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras

Let
B
be a totally definite quaternion algebra
unramified at all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
. Recall that

{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
denotes a maximal order in
B
, and we have fixed isomorphisms
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}\cong M_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})
for all
v\not\in\Sigma^{B}
.

Let
T
be an
{\mathcal{O}}
-module equipped with a smooth action of an open subgroup
U_{p}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,p}^{\times}\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})
.
For
U
an open compact subgroup of
B_{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}
such that

u_{p}\in U_{p}
for all
u\in U
, we define

M^{B}(U,T)=\{\,f:B_{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}\to T\,|\,\mbox{$f(\gamma xu)=u_{p}^{-1}f(x)$ for all $\gamma\in B^{\times}$,
$x\in B_{\mathbb{A}}^{\times}$,
$u\in B_{\infty}^{\times}U$}\,\}.

In particular, if
U=U_{p}U^{p}
for some open compact subgroup
U^{p}
of
(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
, then we may identify

M^{B}(U,T)
with

\{\,f:(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to T\,|\,\mbox{$f(\gamma_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}xu)=\gamma\cdot f(x)$ for all $\gamma\in B^{\times}\cap U_{p}$,
$x\in(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}$,
$u\in U^{p}$}\,\}.

Taking the limit over such
U
, we have a natural action of

(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
on

{\mathcal{M}}^{B}(U_{p},T):=\varinjlim_{U}M^{B}(U,T)

identifying
M^{B}(U,T)
with
{\mathcal{M}}^{B}(U_{p},T)^{U^{p}}
. In particular, we have a commuting family of Hecke operators
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
on
M^{B}(U,T)
for all

v\not\in\Sigma^{B}\cup S_{p}
such that
U
contains

{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}^{\times}
.

Note that taking
T={\mathcal{O}}
with trivial action of
U_{p}={\mathcal{O}}_{B,p}^{\times}
, we have a natural action of
B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
on
{\mathcal{M}}^{B}({\mathcal{O}}):=\varinjlim_{U}M^{B}(U,{\mathcal{O}})
(where the limit is now over all open compact
U
), from which we recover

M^{B}(U,T)=({\mathcal{M}}^{B}({\mathcal{O}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}T)^{U}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathcal{M}}^{B}(U_{p},T)=({\mathcal{M}}^{B}({\mathcal{O}})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}T)^{U_{p}}

for arbitrary
U_{p}
,
T
and
U
as above.

Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
with
k_{\sigma}\geq 2
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, and let
R
be an
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra such that
p^{N}R=0
for some
N>0
. Consider the
R
-module

D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}:=\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\det{}^{m_{\sigma}+1}\otimes_{R}\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{\sigma}-2}R^{2}

equipped with the action of

{\mathcal{O}}_{B,p}^{\times}\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})\cong\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}}\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}})

provided by the identification of
\Sigma
with the set of embeddings
F\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
. Thus the action on the factor of the tensor product indexed by
\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is defined by the corresponding homomorphism

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}})\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}})
. We then let

M^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)=M^{B}(U,D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}).

Taking the limit over open compact
U
containing
U_{p}={\mathcal{O}}_{B,p}^{\times}
, we have a natural action of
(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
on

{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}^{B}(R):=\varinjlim_{U}M^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)

identifying
M^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
with
{\mathcal{M}}^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R)^{U^{p}}
. Suppose, as in §
3.5
, that
\vec{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Sigma}
and
\xi:({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to R^{\times}
is a character such that
\xi(\alpha)=\alpha^{\vec{\ell}}
for all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
. Multiplication by the element

e_{\xi}=\xi\circ\det\in{\mathcal{M}}^{B}_{\vec{0},\vec{\ell}}(R)
then defines a

(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
-equivariant
isomorphism
{\mathcal{M}}^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R)(\xi\circ\det)\cong{\mathcal{M}}^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{\ell}+\vec{m}}(R)
, and hence isomorphisms
as in (
3
) for sufficiently small
U
.

Suppose now that
U=U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}U^{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, where
U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
corresponds to either

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}})
or

U_{1}({\mathfrak{p}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\left\{\left.\,{\mbox{\scriptsize$\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{{a}}&{{b}}\\
{{c}}&{{d}}\end{array}\right)$}}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}})\,\right|\,c-1,d\in{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}}\,\right\}

(and
U^{{\mathfrak{p}}}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbf{f}}^{({\mathfrak{p}})})
).
In addition to the operators
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
as above, we may also define
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, provided
9
9

9

In fact we shall only make use of
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}

in the case that
\vec{m}=-\vec{1}
.

\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(m_{\sigma}+1)\geq 0

(or
p
is invertible in
R
).
Indeed in this case the action of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
on
