D^{\prime}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}
extends to that of the multiplicative monoid
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}
, and we let

(T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}f)(x)=\sum h_{i}f(xh_{i})
,
where the sum is over
h_{i}
such that
U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}hU_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\coprod_{i}h_{i}U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
and
h
corresponds to
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}&{{0}}\\
{{0}}&{{1}}\end{array}\right)
under the chosen isomorphism

{\mathcal{O}}_{B,{{\mathfrak{p}}}}=M_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}})
. It is straighforward to check that this gives a well-defined endomorphism
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
of
M^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
commuting with the operators
T_{v}
and
S_{v}

for
v
as above (or of
{\mathcal{M}}^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R)
commuting with the action of
(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
). Note that we may similarly define an operator

S_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
if
U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}={\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
and
\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(k_{\sigma}+2m_{\sigma})\geq 0

(or
p
is invertible in
R
).

Remark 3.6.1
.

In anticipation of consideration of the case of indefinite quaternion algebras
B
, we remark that

M^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
may be identified with
H^{0}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R})
, where
\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}
is the sheaf on the (finite discrete) set
Y_{U}^{B}=B^{\times}\backslash B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}/U
associated to
D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}
. We can then interpret the Hecke operators defined above in terms of pull-back and trace morphisms relative to
the action of
B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
on the inverse system of sets
Y_{U}^{B}
. In particular, if
U=U^{{\mathfrak{p}}}U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
with
U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}={\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
and
U^{{\mathfrak{p}}}
sufficiently small, we can identify
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
can with the composite

H^{0}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R})\to H^{0}(Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B},\rho_{h}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R})\to H^{0}(Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B},\rho_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R})\to H^{0}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}),

where
U^{\prime}=U\cap hUh^{-1}
(for
h
as above), the first
map is pull-back (relative to the map
\rho_{h}:Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B}\to Y_{U}^{B}

defined by right-multiplication by
h
),
the second is given by the morphism of sheaves induced by
h
on

D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}
, and the third is the trace relative to the projection
\rho_{1}:Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B}\to Y_{U}^{B}
.

For any
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}^{\Sigma}
and
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
R
, we may also consider the action of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,(p)}^{\times}
on

D_{\vec{k},R}:=\det{}^{\vec{1}-\vec{k}/2}\bigotimes_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{\sigma}-2}R^{2}.

For
U=U_{p}U^{p}
with
U_{p}={\mathcal{O}}_{B,p}^{\times}
, we define
M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,R)
to be

\{\,f:(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to D_{\vec{k},R}\,|\,\mbox{$f(\gamma_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}xu)=\gamma\cdot f(x)$ for all $\gamma\in{\mathcal{O}}_{B,(p)}^{\times}$,
$x\in(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}$,
$u\in U^{p}$}\,\}.

We again have a natural action of
(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
on
the direct limit
{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k}}^{B}(R)
over open compact
U
containing
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,p}^{\times}
, from which we recover

M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,R)
as
{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k}}^{B}(R)^{U^{p}}
and
equip it Hecke operators
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for

v\not\in\Sigma^{B}\cup S_{p}
such that
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}^{\times}\subset U
. The definitions are consistent with those in §
2.4
in the sense that there are identifications

(5)

M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{C}\otimes_{{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}}M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}})\quad\mbox{and}\quad M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p})=\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\otimes_{{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}}M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}})

compatible with Hecke actions, where
M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}})
is defined as above with
R={\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}
.

We may also define an operator
T_{\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}}
on

M_{\vec{k}}^{B}(U,R)
for
U
containing
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
.
We choose
\delta\in B^{\times}
such that
\delta_{p}\in hU_{p}

(where
h
is as in the definition of
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
),
and write
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,(p)}^{\times}\delta{\mathcal{O}}_{B,(p)}^{\times}=\coprod_{i}\delta_{i}{\mathcal{O}}_{B,(p)}^{\times}
. Note in particular
that each
\det(\delta_{i})\in\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)}^{\times}
,
so that
p^{-f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\vec{k}/2}\delta_{i,p}
defines
an endomorphism of
D_{\vec{k},R}
, and we let

(T_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}f)(x)=\sum_{i}(p^{-f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\vec{k}/2}\delta_{i,p})\cdot f((\delta_{i,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{-1}x).

Just as for
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
on
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}^{B}(U,R)
, this gives a well-defined operator
T_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
on
M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
commuting with the
T_{v}
and
S_{v}

(or on
{\mathcal{M}}^{B}_{\vec{k}}(R)
commuting with
(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
). Furthermore, the usual Hecke operator
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}

on
M_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathbb{C}})
corresponds to
p^{f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{-\vec{k}/2}T_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
under the identifications of
(
5
). (For completeness we note that if
U
contains
{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}
, then
the usual Hecke operator
S_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is well-defined on
M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
for any
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
R
.)

If
p^{N}R=0
for some
N>0
, and
\xi:({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to R^{\times}
is such that
\xi(\alpha)=\alpha^{\vec{m}+\vec{k}/2}
for all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
, we can again twist by
e_{\xi}=\xi\circ\det
to obtain

{\mathcal{M}}^{B}_{\vec{k}}(R)(\xi\circ\det)\cong{\mathcal{M}}^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(R)
, and hence isomorphisms
as in (
4
) for sufficiently small
U
.
In particular we have
T_{v}(e_{\xi}f)=e_{\xi}\xi(\varpi_{v})T_{v}(f)

and
S_{v}(e_{\xi}f)=e_{\xi}\xi(\varpi_{v})^{2}S_{v}(f)
for
v
as above; furthermore it is straightforward to check that

(6)

T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(e_{\xi}f)=\epsilon\xi(x)^{-1}\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\vec{m}+\vec{1}}e_{\xi}T_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(f),

where
\epsilon=p^{f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\mathrm{Nm}(\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})^{-1}

and
x=\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{(p)}
.

Note that
M^{B}_{\vec{2}}(U,R)=M^{B}(U,R)
, as defined above with
T=R
and
U_{p}={\mathcal{O}}_{B,p}^{\times}
acting trivially. More generally we let
M_{\vec{2}}^{B}(U,R)=M^{B}(U,R)
for arbitrary open compact
U\subset B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
. Note that (
5
) holds in this context as well, and that
M^{B}_{\vec{2}}(U,R)
coincides with
M^{B}_{\vec{2},-\vec{1}}(U,R)
as defined above if
p^{N}R=0
for some
N>0
.

3.7.
Cohomology of Shimura curves over
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebras

Now suppose that
B
is a quaternion algebra over
F
which is
unramified at all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
and at a unique
\sigma_{0}\in\Sigma_{\infty}
. Recall that we have chosen a maximal order
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
and isomorphisms
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}\cong M_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})
for all finite
v\not\in\Sigma^{B}
. For open compact subgroups
U
of
B_{{\mathbb{A}},\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
, we consider the compact Riemann surface

Y_{U}^{B}=B_{+}^{\times}\backslash((\mathfrak{H}\times B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times})/U)

defined in §
2.5
.

Let
T
be an
{\mathcal{O}}
-module with a smooth action of an open subgroup
U_{p}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,p}^{\times}
. For sufficiently small
U

(and in particular satisfying
u_{p}\in U_{p}
for all
u\in U
), consider the locally constant sheaf
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{T}}^{B}_{U}
on
Y_{U}^{B}
defined by (sections of)

B_{+}^{\times}\backslash((\mathfrak{H}\times B_{\mathbf{f}}^{\times}\times T)/U),

where the actions of
B_{+}^{\times}
and
U
are defined by

\gamma\cdot(z,x,t)u=(\gamma_{\infty}(z),\gamma_{\mathbf{f}}xu,u_{p}^{-1}t).

Note that if
U=U_{p}U^{p}
for some open compact subgroup
U^{p}
of
(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
, then we may rewrite

Y_{U}^{B}
as
(B_{+}^{\times}\cap U_{p})\backslash((\mathfrak{H}\times(B^{(p)}_{\mathbf{f}})^{\times})/U^{p})

and
{\mathcal{T}}^{B}_{U}
as

(B_{+}^{\times}\cap U_{p})\backslash((\mathfrak{H}\times(B^{(p)}_{\mathbf{f}})^{\times}\times T)/U^{p}),

where the actions are defined by
\gamma\cdot(z,x,t)u=(\gamma_{\infty}(z),\gamma^{(p)}_{\mathbf{f}}xu,\gamma_{p}t)
.

Note that for
U
,
U^{\prime}
as above and
h\in(B^{(p)}_{\mathbf{f}})^{\times}
such that
U^{\prime}\subset hUh^{-1}
, the identity on
T
induces an isomorphism
\rho_{h}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}^{B}_{U}\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}{\mathcal{T}}^{B}_{U^{\prime}}
,
where
\rho_{h}:Y^{B}_{U^{\prime}}\to Y^{B}_{U}
is the covering map induced by right multiplication by
h
. We thus obtain pull-back and trace morphisms

H^{i}(Y_{U}^{B},{\mathcal{T}}_{U}^{B})\longrightarrow H^{i}(Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B},{\mathcal{T}}_{U^{\prime}}^{B})\quad\mbox{and}\quad H^{i}(Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B},{\mathcal{T}}_{U^{\prime}}^{B})\longrightarrow H^{i}(Y_{U}^{B},{\mathcal{T}}_{U}^{B})

relative to
\rho_{h}
. More generally, for any
U
,
U^{\prime}
as above and
h\in(B^{(p)}_{\mathbf{f}})^{\times}
, we define the double coset operator
[U^{\prime}hU]
to be the composite

H^{i}(Y_{U}^{B},{\mathcal{T}}_{U}^{B})\longrightarrow H^{i}(Y_{U^{\prime\prime}}^{B},{\mathcal{T}}_{U^{\prime\prime}}^{B})\longrightarrow H^{i}(Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B},{\mathcal{T}}_{U^{\prime}}^{B}),

where
U^{\prime\prime}=U^{\prime}\cap hUh^{-1}
, the first map is the pull-back relative to
\rho_{h}:Y^{B}_{U^{\prime\prime}}\to Y^{B}_{U}
, and the second is

[U^{\prime}\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times}:U^{\prime\prime}\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times}]
times
10
10

10

Note that this normalization factor is
[U^{\prime}:U^{\prime\prime}]/\deg(\rho_{1})
.
the trace relative to
\rho_{1}:Y^{B}_{U^{\prime\prime}}\to Y^{B}_{U^{\prime}}
. These maps satisfy the usual compatibilities, so in particular we obtain a commuting family of operators
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
on
H^{i}(Y^{B}_{U},{\mathcal{T}}_{U}^{B})
for
all
v\not\in\Sigma^{B}\cup S_{p}
such that
U
contains
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}^{\times}
. We let
11
11

11

We avoid considering direct limits over
U
of the
M^{B}(U,T)
since the natural maps
M^{B}(U,T)\to M^{B}(U^{\prime},T)^{U}
(for
U^{\prime}
normal in
U
) are not necessarily isomorphisms.

M^{B}(U,T)=H^{1}(Y_{U}^{B},{\mathcal{T}}_{U}^{B})
.

Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}
with all
k_{\sigma}\geq 2
, and
R
is an
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra such that
p^{N}R=0
for some
N>0
.
For sufficiently small
U
, we let
\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}
denote the locally constant sheaf on
Y_{U}^{B}
associated to
D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}
(defined as in §
3.6
), and
let

M^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)=M^{B}(U,D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R})=H^{1}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}).

Suppose as usual that

\vec{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Sigma}
and
\xi:({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to R^{\times}
is a character such that

\xi(\alpha)=\alpha^{\vec{\ell}}
for all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
.
Then
\xi\circ\det
defines an element
e_{\xi}\in H^{0}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{0},\vec{\ell},R})
for sufficiently small
U

(in particular if
U=U_{p}U^{p}
and
\det(U^{p})\subset\ker(\xi)
).
Furthermore if
h\in(B^{(p)}_{\mathbf{f}})^{\times}
and
U^{\prime}\subset hUh^{-1}
, then the pull-back

H^{0}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R})\longrightarrow H^{0}(Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R})

relative to
\rho_{h}
sends
e_{\xi}
to
\xi(\det(h))e_{\xi}
.
It follows that the cup product with
e_{\xi}
defines an
isomorphism as in (
3
) for sufficiently small
U
.

As in the case of definite quaternion algebras, we may define operators
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
such that

U=U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}U^{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, where
U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
corresponds to either
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}})
or
U_{1}({\mathfrak{p}})_{\mathfrak{p}}
and

\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(m_{\sigma}+1)\geq 0

(or
p
is invertible in
R
). Indeed consider the
two covering maps
\rho_{1},\rho_{h}:Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B}\to Y_{U}^{B}
,
where
h\in{\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}
corresponds to

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}&{{0}}\\
{{0}}&{{1}}\end{array}\right)

and
U^{\prime}=U\cap hUh^{-1}
. The endomorphism
h

of
D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}
then
induces a morphism

\rho_{h}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}\longrightarrow\rho_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}

of sheaves on
Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B}
(defined by

B_{+}^{\times}(z,xh,t)U^{\prime}\mapsto B_{+}^{\times}(z,x,ht)U^{\prime}

on the fibre over
B_{+}^{\times}(z,x)U^{\prime}
).
We then define
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
on

H^{i}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R})
as in Remark
3.6.1
, i.e., as the composite

H^{i}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R})\to H^{i}(Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B},\rho_{h}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R})\to H^{i}(Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B},\rho_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R})\to H^{i}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}).

In particular this defines an endomorphism
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
of
M^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,R)
commuting with the
T_{v}
and
S_{v}

(for
v
as above), and we may similarly define

S_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
(again assuming
U_{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
and
that
\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}(k_{\sigma}+2m_{\sigma})\geq 0

or
p
is invertible in
R
).

For
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}^{\Sigma}
and
R
any
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra, we define the locally constant sheaf
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\vec{k},R}
on
Y_{U}^{B}
by (sections of)

{\mathcal{O}}_{B,(p),+}^{\times}\backslash((\mathfrak{H}\times(B^{(p)}_{\mathbf{f}})^{\times}\times D_{\vec{k},R})/U^{p}),

where
U=U_{p}U^{p}
,
U_{p}={\mathcal{O}}_{B,p}^{\times}
and
D_{\vec{k},R}
is defined as in §
3.6
. For such
U
,
U^{\prime}
and
h\in(B^{(p)}_{\mathbf{f}})^{\times}
satisfying
U^{\prime}\subset hUh^{-1}
, we again have the isomorphism
\rho_{h}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},R}\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},R}
induced by the identity on
D^{\prime}_{\vec{k},R}
, and hence pull-back and trace morphisms between
M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
and
M_{\vec{k}}(U^{\prime},R)
.
We can therefore define double coset operators
[U^{\prime}hU]
as above (without the assumption that
U^{\prime}\subset hUh^{-1}
), and hence obtain a commuting family of Hecke operators
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
on
M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
for
v\not\in\Sigma^{B}\cup S_{p}
such that
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}^{\times}\subset U
. The definitions are again consistent with those in
§
2.5
in the sense that there are identifications
as in (
5
) compatible with Hecke actions.

To define
T_{\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}}
for
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
and
U
containing
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
, choose
\delta\in B_{+}^{\times}
such that

\delta_{p}\in hU_{p}
(for
h
is as in the definition of
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
). We can then write

Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B}=\Gamma\backslash((\mathfrak{H}\times B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times})/U^{p}),

where
\Gamma=B_{+}^{\times}\cap U_{p}^{\prime}=\delta{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{B,(p),+}\delta^{-1}\cap{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{B,(p),+}
, so that
\rho_{h}:Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B}\to Y_{U}^{B}
takes the form
\Gamma(z,x)U^{p}\mapsto{\mathcal{O}}_{B,(p),+}^{\times}(\delta_{\infty}^{-1}(z),(\delta_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{-1}x)U^{p}
.
We then have a morphism
\rho_{h}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},R}\to\rho_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},R}
of sheaves on
Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B}

given by

\Gamma(\delta_{\infty}^{-1}(z),(\delta_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{-1}x,t)U^{p}\mapsto\Gamma(z,x,(p^{-f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\vec{k}/2}\delta_{p})t)U^{p},

on the fibre over
\Gamma(z,x)U^{p}
, and we define
T_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
on
H^{i}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},R})
as the resulting composite

H^{i}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},R})\to H^{i}(Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B},\rho_{h}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},R})\to H^{i}(Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B},\rho_{1}^{*}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},R})\to H^{i}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},R}).

In particular we obtain an operator

T_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
on
M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
, which it is
straighforward to check commutes with the
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
,
and corresponds to
p^{-f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\vec{k}/2}T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
under the identifications as in
(
5
). (Again for completeness we note that
if
U
contains
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
, then the usual definition
yields an operator
S_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
on
M_{\vec{k}}(U,R)
for any
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
R
.)

If
p^{N}R=0
and
\xi:({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to R^{\times}
is such that
\xi(\alpha)=\alpha^{\vec{m}+\vec{k}/2}
for all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
, we again have an isomorphism
e_{\xi}:\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},R}\mathrel{{\mathop{\to}\limits}}\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},R}
defined by

\xi\circ\det
, i.e.,

{\mathcal{O}}_{B,(p),+}^{\times}(z,x,t)U^{p}\mapsto{\mathcal{O}}_{B,(p),+}^{\times}(z,x,\xi(\det(x))t)U^{p}

on the fibre over
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,(p),+}^{\times}(z,x,)U^{p}
. This
gives rise to isomorphisms
as in (
4
) for sufficiently small
U
,
and satisfying the same compatibilities with Hecke
operators, and in particular (
6
).

Finally, we let
M^{B}_{\vec{2}}(U,R)=M^{B}(U,R)=H^{1}(Y_{U}^{B},R)
for any sufficiently small open compact
U
, noting again that
(
5
) still holds, and that
M^{B}_{\vec{2}}(U,R)=M^{B}_{\vec{2},-\vec{1}}(U,R)
if
p^{N}R=0
for some
N>0
.

4.
Hilbert modular forms in characteristic
p

4.1.
Partial Hasse invariants

We first recall the existence of certain partial Hasse invariants
(as constructed by
[
Gor01
]
,
[
AG05
]
and in this generality by
[
RX17
]
).

For each
\sigma\in\Sigma
, we let
\vec{h}_{\sigma}=\nu_{\sigma}\vec{e}_{\varphi^{-1}\sigma}-\vec{e}_{\sigma}
, where

\nu_{\sigma}=p
if
\sigma=\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,1}
for some
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
,
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/f_{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\nu_{\sigma}=1
otherwise. (In particular
n_{\sigma}=p
for all
\sigma
if
p
is unramified in
F
.) We then have the partial Hasse invariant

H_{\sigma}\in M_{\vec{h}_{\sigma},\vec{0}}(U,E).

Furthermore, multiplication by
H_{\sigma}

defines an injective
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
-equivariant
homomorphism

M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)\hookrightarrow M_{\vec{k}+\vec{h}_{\sigma},\vec{m}}(U,E)

for all
\vec{k}
,
\vec{m}
and
U
as in §
3
.
Similarly we have elements

G_{\sigma}\in M_{\vec{0},\vec{h}_{\sigma}}(U,E)

inducing
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
-equivariant isomorphisms

M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}+\vec{h}_{\sigma}}(U,E)

(see
[
Dia23
, §4.1]
). Note in particular that
H_{\sigma}

and
G_{\sigma}
commute with the operators
T_{v}
and
S_{v}

(whenever defined) for
v\nmid p
. Furthermore the

q
-expansions
H_{\sigma}
and
G_{\sigma}
at every cusp
are non-zero constants (see
[
Dia23
, §8.1]
).

Recall from
[
DK23b
, §8]
that for each non-zero
f\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
and
\sigma\in\Sigma
, there is a maximal integer
r_{\sigma}
such that
f
is divisible by
H_{\sigma}^{r_{\sigma}}
in the ring
M_{{\operatorname{tot}}}(U,E)
. Then
f
is divisible by
\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma}H_{\sigma}^{r_{\sigma}}
, and
we define

\vec{k}_{\min}(f)=\vec{k}-\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}r_{\sigma}\vec{h}_{\sigma}.

The main result of
[
DK23b
]
then states:

Theorem 4.1.1
.

If
0\neq f\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
, then

\vec{k}_{\min}(f)\in\Xi_{\min}
, where

\Xi_{\min}=\{\,\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}\,|\,\mbox{$n_{\sigma}k_{\sigma}\geq k_{\varphi^{-1}(\sigma)}$
for all $\sigma\in\Sigma$}\,\}.

Since
\Xi_{\min}
is contained in the cone spanned (rationally) by the partial Hasse invariants, we obtain the following:

Corollary 4.1.2
.

If
\vec{k}\not\in\{\sum\limits_{\sigma\in\Sigma}r_{\sigma}\vec{h}_{\sigma}|\mbox{$r_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}$ for all $\sigma\in\Sigma$}\}
, then
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)=0
.

4.2.
Weight-shifting operators

We recall also the existence and properties of certain
weight-shifting operators in characteristic
p
.
For each element
\tau=\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}\in\Sigma_{0}
, we have a
partial
\Theta
-operator

\Theta_{\tau}:M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)\longrightarrow M_{\vec{k}+\vec{t}_{\tau},\vec{m}-\vec{e}_{\sigma}}(U,E),

where
\sigma=\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,e_{\mathfrak{p}}}
and

\vec{t}_{\tau}=\vec{h}_{\sigma}+2\vec{e}_{\sigma}

(see
[
Dia23
, §5]
).
Then each
\Theta_{\tau}
is
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
-equivariant, and hence commutes with the Hecke operators

T_{v}
and
S_{v}
(whenever defined) for
v\nmid p
.
Furthermore the
\Theta_{\tau}
are
E
-linear derivations
on
M_{{\operatorname{tot}}}(U,E)

which commute with each other (see
[
Dia23
, §8.2]
),
as well as with multiplication
by partial Hasse invariants (since
\Theta_{\tau}(H_{\sigma^{\prime}})=0

for all
\tau\in\Sigma_{0}
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma
).
Their effect on
q
-expansions is given by the formula

r_{m}^{t}(\Theta_{\tau}(f))=\pi_{\tau}(m)r_{m}^{t}(f),

where
\pi_{\tau}
is an isomorphism
(M/{\mathfrak{p}}M)\otimes_{{\mathbb{F}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}E\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}E
(again depending on a choice of basis; see
[
Dia23
, (30)]
for a more precise statement).

The partial
\Theta
-operators also have the following property (
[
Dia23
, Thm. 5.2.1]
):

Theorem 4.2.1
.

Suppose that
\tau=\tau_{p,i}
,
\sigma=\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,e_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
and
f\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
. Then
\Theta_{\tau}(f)
is divisible by
H_{\sigma}
if and only if
f
is divisible by
H_{\sigma}
or
k_{\sigma}
is divisible by
p
.

Finally for each
{\mathfrak{p}}\in\Sigma_{p}
, we have the partial Frobenius operator

V_{\mathfrak{p}}:M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)\longrightarrow M_{\vec{k}^{\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime}}(U,E)

defined in
[
Dia23
, §6]
, where
\vec{k}^{\prime}=\vec{k}+\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}k_{\sigma}\vec{h}_{\sigma}
and

\vec{m}^{\prime}=\vec{m}+\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}m_{\sigma}\vec{h}_{\sigma}
. These too are
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
-equivariant, and hence commute with the Hecke operators

T_{v}
and
S_{v}
(whenever defined) for
v\nmid p
.
Furthermore they commute with each other, and define

E
-algebra endomorphisms of
M_{{\operatorname{tot}}}(U,E)
.
Their effect on
q
-expansions is given by the formula

(7)

r_{m}^{t}(V_{\mathfrak{p}}(f))=r_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{-1}m}^{xt}(f),

where again
x=\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{(p)}
(with a more precise
description in
[
Dia23
, §8.3]
).
Finally, we have
\Theta_{\tau}\circ V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=0
for all

\tau\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}
; in fact the following stronger
result holds (
[
Dia23
, Cor. 9.1.2]
):

Theorem 4.2.2
.

Suppose that
f\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)

and
\tau\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}
. Then
\Theta_{\tau}(f)=0

if and only if

f=V_{\mathfrak{p}}(g)\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma}G_{\sigma}^{s_{\sigma}}H_{\sigma}^{r_{\sigma}}

for some
g\in M_{{\operatorname{tot}}}(U,E)
,
\vec{r}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{\Sigma}
,
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
.

We now use an idea of Deo, Dimitrov and Wiese (see
[
DDW24
, Prop. 1.22]
) to refine Theorem
4.1.1
.

Theorem 4.2.3
.

If
0\neq f\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
,
then
\vec{k}_{\min}(f)\in\Xi_{\min}^{+}\cup\{\vec{0}\}
, where

\Xi_{\min}^{+}=\{\,\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\Sigma}\,|\,\mbox{$n_{\sigma}k_{\sigma}\geq k_{\varphi^{-1}(\sigma)}$
for all $\sigma\in\Sigma$}\,\}.

Proof.

Let
\vec{k}=\vec{k}_{\min}(f)
. Note that we may assume
U=U({\mathfrak{n}})
for some sufficiently small
{\mathfrak{n}}
prime to
p
. Furthermore since the isomorphism of (
3
) preserves divisibility by partial Hasse invariants, we may assume
\vec{m}=\vec{0}
.
We must prove that if
k_{\sigma}=0
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma
, then
\vec{k}=\vec{0}
. First note that by Theorem
4.1.1
, we have

k_{\sigma}\geq 0
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, and if
k_{\sigma}=0
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, then
k_{\sigma}=0
for all

\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Suppose then that
k_{\sigma}=0
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Note that this implies that
V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is an endomorphism of

M_{\vec{k},\vec{0}}(U,E)
. We claim that the
q
-expansion is constant at each cusp at
\infty
. Suppose this is not the case, and let

n(f)=\min\{\,v_{\mathfrak{p}}(m)\,|\,\mbox{$r_{m}^{t}(f)\neq 0$ for
some $t\in({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}$}\,\}.

(Note that by (
1
), it suffices to consider
one representative
t
for each cusp, or equivalently each
component of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}
.) Equation (
7
) implies
that
n(V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(f))=n(f)+1
, and hence that
n(V^{i}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(f))=n(f)+i
for all
i\geq 0
. It then follows from the
q
-expansion Principle that the elements
V^{i}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(f)
(for
i\geq 0
) are linearly independent, contradicting the finite-dimensionality of
M_{\vec{k},\vec{0}}(U,E)
.

We now show that
\vec{k}=\vec{0}
. Let
N=\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}}(p^{f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}-1)=|\det(A)|
, where
A
is the
d\times d
-matrix of coefficients of the weights
\vec{h}_{\sigma}
of partial Hasse invariants. Then

N\vec{k}=\sum_{\sigma}r_{\sigma}\vec{h}_{\sigma}
for some

\vec{r}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, where each
r_{\sigma}\geq 0
by
Corollary
4.1.2
. Since the
q
-expansion of
H_{\sigma}
at each cusp is a non-zero constant, it follows that for each connected component
{\mathcal{Y}}_{t}
of
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E}
, there is a constant
c_{t}
such that

f^{N}|_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{t}}=c_{t}\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma}H^{r_{\sigma}}_{\sigma}|_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{t}},

and hence
f^{N}=e\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma}H^{r_{\sigma}}_{\sigma}
for some
e\in H^{0}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E}})
.
In particular, if
r_{\sigma}>0
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma
, then
f^{N}
is divisible by
H_{\sigma}
. Since the vanishing locus of
H_{\sigma}
is reduced, it follows that
H_{\sigma}|f
, contradicting our assumption that
\vec{k}=\vec{k}_{\min}(f)
. Therefore
\vec{r}=\vec{0}
, so
\vec{k}=\vec{0}
.
∎

Corollary 4.2.4
.

If
0\neq f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
,
then
\vec{k}_{\min}(f)\in\Xi^{+}_{\min}
.

4.3.
Ampleness

We assume that
U
sufficiently small, in particular ensuring that the coherent sheaves
{\mathcal{A}}^{\min}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}
are in fact line bundles. We let
\vec{\delta}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
be defined by
\delta_{\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j}}=j-1
. (Note that
\vec{\delta}=\vec{0}
if
p
is unramified in
F
.)

We record the following slight variant of
[
RX17
, Lemma 4.5]
.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

The line bundles
{\mathcal{A}}^{\min}_{\vec{\ell}+\vec{\delta},\vec{m},E}
are ample on
Y_{E}^{\min}
for sufficiently large
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Consider the morphism

\pi:Y_{E}^{\min}\to Y_{-,E}^{\min}
defined by forgetting the Pappas–Rapoport filtrations), where

Y_{-,E}^{\min}
is the minimal compactification of the
Deligne–Pappas model (see
[
Dia24
, §2.10]
), so that

{\mathcal{A}}^{\min}_{\vec{1},\vec{0},E}=\pi^{*}\omega
for an ample
line bundle
\omega
on
Y_{-,E}^{\min}
.

Furthermore recall that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\min}_{\vec{\delta},\vec{0},E}
is relatively ample (with respect to
\pi
). Indeed
\pi

is an isomorphism in a neighborhood of the cusps (more precisely, the ordinary locus), so we can replace
\pi
by
Y_{E}\to Y_{E,-}
, and hence by the morphism

\widetilde{\pi}:\widetilde{Y}_{E}\to\widetilde{Y}_{E,-}
,
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\min}_{\vec{\delta},\vec{0},E}
by

\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\vec{\delta},\vec{0},E}
.
The very definition of
\widetilde{Y}_{E}
factors

\widetilde{\pi}
through a closed immersion

\widetilde{Y}_{E}\hookrightarrow\bigtimes_{\tau\in\Sigma_{0}}\mathrm{Flag}_{\widetilde{Y}_{E,-}}(s_{*}\Omega_{A/\widetilde{Y}_{E,-}})_{\tau},

where the product is fibred over
\widetilde{Y}_{E,-}

and the factors are the (full) flag varieties associated
to the vector bundles

{\mathcal{F}}^{(e_{\mathfrak{p}})}_{\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}}=(s_{*}\Omega_{A/\widetilde{Y}_{E,-}})_{\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}}
(where

s:A\to\widetilde{Y}_{E,-}
is the universal abelian scheme).
The relative ampleness then follows from the fact that the closed immersion identifies the pull-back of
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
under the usual projective embedding of the flag variety with

\bigotimes_{j=1}^{e_{\mathfrak{p}}}\left(\bigwedge{\mathstrut}^{\!\!\!e_{\mathfrak{p}}-j}\left({\mathcal{F}}_{\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}}^{(e_{\mathfrak{p}})}/{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}}^{(j)}\right)\right)\cong\bigotimes_{j=1}^{e_{\mathfrak{p}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j}}^{j-1}.

It follows from
[
StaX
, Lemma 0892]
that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\min}_{\vec{\ell}+\vec{\delta},\vec{0},E}
is ample for sufficiently large
\ell
. Finally since
{\mathcal{A}}^{\min}_{\vec{0},\vec{m},E}
is a torsion line bundle on
Y_{E}^{\min}
, the ampleness of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\min}_{\vec{\ell}+\vec{\delta},\vec{m},E}
follows from

[
StaX
, Lemma 0890]
.
∎

5.
Associated Galois representations

5.1.
Associated Galois representations in characteristic zero

Recall that
\mathcal{C}_{\vec{k}}
is the set of cuspidal automorphic representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
whose local factor
is
D^{\prime}_{k_{\sigma}}
at each archimedean place
\sigma\in\Sigma
.
Thus if
\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{k}}
, then it is

C
-algebraic (in the terminology
of
[
BG14
]
) if and only if all the
k_{\sigma}
are even,
and
L
-algebraic if and only if all the
k_{\sigma}
are odd.
In this case, through the work of many people, we can associate Galois representations to
\pi
compatible with its local factors
\pi_{v}
under the local Langlands correspondence
{\operatorname{rec}}_{F_{v}}
. More precisely, by the results of many authors (in particular,
[
Car86
]
,
[
BR93
]
,
[
RT83
]
,
[
Tay89
]
,
[
Jar97
]
,
[
Sai09
]
and
[
BLGGT14
]
), we have:

Theorem 5.1.1
.

Suppose that
w\in\mathbb{Z}
is such that
w\equiv k_{\sigma}\bmod 2

for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, and let
\pi=\otimes^{\prime}_{v}\pi_{v}
be an irreducible subrepresentation of
|\det|^{w/2}{\mathcal{S}}_{\vec{k}}
. Then there exists
a unique (up to isomorphism) irreducible representation

\rho_{\pi}:G_{F}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p})

such that

(8)

{\operatorname{WD}}(\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{v}}})^{\mathrm{F-ss}}\cong{\operatorname{rec}}_{F_{v}}(|\det|^{-1/2}\pi_{v})

for all but finitely many non-archimedean places
v

of
F
. Furthermore if
k_{\sigma}\geq 2
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, then (
8
) holds
for all
12
12

12

See §
6.2
below for definitions and conventions of notions from
p
-adic Hodge theory. We remark also that local-global compatibility is even known under mild technical hypotheses without the assumption that all
k_{\sigma}\geq 2
; see especially
[
New15
, Thm. 1.4]
for
v\nmid p
and
[
BP21
, Thm. 6.11.2]
for
v|p
.

v
, in which case
\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is de Rham with

\sigma
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(\frac{w+k_{\sigma}-2}{2},\frac{w-k_{\sigma}}{2})
for all
v|p
.

Recall that for all but finitely many non-archimedean places

v
of
F
, the representation
\pi_{v}
is unramified
i.e.,
\pi_{v}^{U_{v}}
is non-trivial, and hence
one-dimensional, for
U_{v}=\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})
.
For such a
v
, let
a_{v}
and
d_{v}
denote the eigenvalues
of the double-coset operators
T_{v}
and
S_{v}

on this one-dimensional space (where
T_{v}
and
S_{v}

are defined as in §
3.4
). In this case,
(
8
) means that if
v\nmid p
, then

\rho_{\pi}
is unramified at
v
,
and that the image of a geometric Frobenius element at
v

has characteristic
polynomial
X^{2}-a_{v}X+\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)d_{v}
.
On the other hand if
v|p
(and
\pi_{v}
is unramified), then
\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{v}}}
is crystalline and this is the characteristic polynomial of

\phi^{f_{v}}
on
D_{\mathrm{crys},F_{v}}(\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{v}}})

(where
\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)=p^{f_{v}}
).
Note also that with our normalizations, the determinant of

\rho_{\pi}
is the product of a finite order character with

\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{w-1}
.

5.2.
Associated Galois representations in characteristic
p

We now prove an analogue of Theorem
5.1.1
for mod
p
Hilbert modular eigenforms. This generalizes
[
DS23
, Thm. 6.1.1]
, which proves such a result under the hypothesis that
p
is unramified in
F
. Under an additional parity hypothesis on the weight, this was previously proved independently by Emerton, Reduzzi and Xiao (
[
ERX17
]
) and Goldring and Koskivirta (
[
GK19
]
), the latter as a special case of much more general results. The parity hypothesis was removed in
[
DS23
]
using congruences to forms of higher weight and level not necessarily prime to
p
.

Allowing
p
to ramify in
F
, the approach of
[
ERX17
]
was generalized by Reduzzi and Xiao (
[
RX17
]
), and recent work of Shen and Zheng (
[
SZ25
]
) does the same for that of
[
GK19
]
, but these results still require a parity hypothesis on the weight. Along with some additional work, the parity hypothesis can be removed by combining the methods of
[
DS23
]
and
[
RX17
]
, but we have decided to give a different argument, which is inspired by remarks of David Loeffler and George Boxer. Again the proof ultimately relies on congruences to forms with level involving primes over
p
, but instead of producing them using the geometry of Hilbert modular varieties with (mildly) bad reduction, we lift (twists) to non-algebraic automorphic forms and apply the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence to transfer the problem to a more amenable setting.

Theorem 5.2.1
.

Suppose that
U
is an open compact subgroup of
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}})

containing
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})
, and
Q
is a finite set of primes containing all
v|p
and
all
v
such that
\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})\not\subset U
. Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
and
that
f\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
is an eigenform for

T_{v}
and
S_{v}
(defined in §
3.4
) for all
v\not\in Q
. Then there is a Galois representation

\rho_{f}:G_{F}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E)

such that if
v\not\in Q
, then
\rho_{f}
is unramified at
v
and the characteristic
polynomial of
\rho_{f}(\mathrm{Frob}_{v})
is

X^{2}-a_{v}X+\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)d_{v},

where
T_{v}f=a_{v}f
and
S_{v}f=d_{v}f
.

Remark 5.2.2
.

Recall from Remark
1.3.1
that the Hecke action defined in
[
DS23
]
differs by a twist from the one defined here. More precisely, if
f
is an eigenform for the operator
T_{v}
(resp.
S_{v}
) of this paper with eigenvalue
a_{v}
(resp.
d_{v}
), then it is so for the operators defined in
[
DS23
]
, but with eigenvalue
\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)^{-1}a_{v}
(resp.
\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)^{-2}d_{v}
). Therefore the Galois representation associated to
f
in
[
DS23
]
is the twist by the mod
p
cyclotomic character of the one in the preceding theorem.

Proof.

First we note that the same arguments as in the start of the proof of
[
DS23
, Thm. 6.1.1]
apply to show that we can replace
E
by any finite extension; furthermore we may assume that
U=U({\mathfrak{n}})
for some sufficiently small
{\mathfrak{n}}
prime to
p
and that
Q
is the set of primes dividing
{\mathfrak{n}}p
. In particular, we choose
{\mathfrak{n}}
so that if
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times}
is such that
(\alpha-1)^{2}\in{\mathfrak{n}}
, then
\alpha\equiv 1\bmod{\mathfrak{p}}
for all
{\mathfrak{p}}|p
.

Let
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}^{Q}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
denote the

E
-subalgebra of

\mathrm{End}_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}}(M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}))
generated by the operators

T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for
v\not\in Q
. Restriction to
E\cdot f
defines an
E
-algebra homomorphism
\mathbb{T}\twoheadrightarrow E
, and hence a maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}_{f}\subset\mathbb{T}
; moreover
{\mathfrak{m}}_{f}
is generated by the elements

\{\,T_{v}-a_{v},\,S_{v}-d_{v}\,|\,v\notin Q\,\}
.
Conversely, every maximal ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}
of
\mathbb{T}
is the kernel of an
E
-algebra homomorphism
\theta:\mathbb{T}\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}
; replacing
E
by a finite extension, we may assume it contains the image of

\theta
, and hence that
{\mathfrak{m}}
is generated by the elements
T_{v}-\theta(T_{v})
,
S_{v}-\theta(S_{v})
for
v\not\in Q
.
As
{\mathfrak{m}}
is in the support of
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E))
, it follows that the eigenspace

\begin{array}[]{rcl}M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)[{\mathfrak{m}}]&=&\{\,g\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)\,|\,\mbox{$Tg=0$ for all
$T\in{\mathfrak{m}}$}\,\}\\
&=&\{\,g\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)\,|\,\mbox{$T_{v}g=a_{v}g$,
$S_{v}g=d_{v}g$ for all
$v\not\in Q$}\,\}\end{array}

is non-zero, i.e., that
{\mathfrak{m}}={\mathfrak{m}}_{f}
for some
f
as in the statement of the theorem.

Recall that, under our assumption that
U
is sufficiently small,
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}^{\min}=i_{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}
is a line bundle on
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E}^{\min}
, in which case
{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}^{\operatorname{sub}}=j_{*}j^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}^{\min}
, where

i
is the open immersion
{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E}^{\min}
, and
j
is the closed immersion
{\mathcal{Z}}_{U,E}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E}^{\min}
. We thus have an exact
sequence

(9)

0\longrightarrow S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)\longrightarrow M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)\longrightarrow C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E),

where
C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)=H^{0}({\mathcal{Z}}_{U,E},j^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}^{\min})
.
Just as for
{\mathcal{M}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
, we obtain an
action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
on

C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(E)=\varinjlim_{U}C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E),

and hence Hecke operators
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
on

C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)=C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(E)^{U^{p}}
for all
v\not\in Q
.
Furthermore these operators commute (for varying
v
) and
are compatible with their action on

M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
.
We may therefore assume that
{\mathfrak{m}}
is a maximal ideal of
either
\mathbb{T}^{0}
or
\mathbb{T}^{c}
, where these (respectively)
denote the
E
-subalgebras of the
endomorphism rings of
S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)

and
C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
generated by
the
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for all
v\not\in Q
.

We first treat the case of
\mathbb{T}^{c}
by explicitly describing
the action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
on

C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})=\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}\otimes_{E}C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(E)
.
Recall that

{\mathcal{Z}}_{U,\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}}=B(F)_{+}\backslash\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})/U=B({\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)})_{+}\backslash\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})/U^{p}

(viewed as a scheme over
\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}
). Furthermore the construction of the minimal compactification provides a trivialization of
j^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}}^{\min}
, identifying it with the coherent sheaf defined by (sections of)

B({\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)})_{+}\backslash(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}\times\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})/U^{p}),

where
B({\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)})_{+}
acts on
\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}
via the character

\xi_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}:{\mbox{\scriptsize$\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{{\alpha}}&{{\beta}}\\
{{0}}&{{\delta}}\end{array}\right)$}}\mapsto{\alpha}^{\vec{m}}{\delta}^{\vec{k}+\vec{m}}
. This identification is compatible with the action of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
, so that
C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
is isomorphic to
{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{B({\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)})_{+}}^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})}\xi_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}
, which we define to be the space of smooth
functions
\phi:\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}
such that

\phi(\gamma gu)=\xi_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(\gamma)\phi(g)

for all
\gamma\in B({\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)})_{+}
,

g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})

(where by
smooth
, we mean that
\phi(gu)=\phi(g)
for
all
g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
and

u
in some open compact subgroup
U^{\prime}
of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
). Letting
[\xi_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}]:B({\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)})_{+}\to W(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})^{\times}
denote the Teichmüller lift of
\xi_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}
, we thus have
C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})=\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}\otimes_{W}\widetilde{C}
, where
\widetilde{C}={\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{B({\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)})_{+}}^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})}[\xi_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}]
and
W=W(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
. Furthermore our hypothesis that
U
is sufficiently small implies that

C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})=C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})^{U^{p}}=\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}\otimes_{W}\widetilde{C}^{U^{p}}
. This identification in turn yields a surjective homomorphism
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}^{c}\to\mathbb{T}^{c}
, where
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}^{c}
is the
W
-algebra of
endomorphisms of
\widetilde{C}^{U^{p}}
generated by
the
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for
v\not\in Q
. As
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}^{c}
is free of finite rank over
W
, it follows that there is a
W
-algebra homomorphism
\widetilde{\theta}:\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}^{c}\to\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}
lifting
\theta
, and hence an eigenvector

\widetilde{f}\in(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\otimes_{W}\widetilde{C})^{U^{p}}
such that
T_{v}\widetilde{f}=\widetilde{a}_{v}\widetilde{f}
and

S_{v}\widetilde{f}=\widetilde{d}_{v}\widetilde{f}
for all
v\not\in Q
, where the
\widetilde{a}_{v}
and
\widetilde{d}_{v}

are lifts of the
a_{v}
and
d_{v}
to
\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}
.

We now determine the possible homomorphisms
\widetilde{\theta}
by analyzing the smooth admissible representation

\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\otimes_{W}\widetilde{C}={\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{B({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})}^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})}{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{B({\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)})_{+}}^{B({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})}[\xi_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}],

where
[\xi_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}]
is now viewed as taking values in
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
. Firstly the density of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)}
in
\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}
implies that

{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{B({\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)})_{+}}^{B({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})}[\xi_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}]
decomposes as the direct sum of all characters
\xi:B({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})\to\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}

of the form

{\mbox{\scriptsize$\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{{a}}&{{b}}\\
{{0}}&{{d}}\end{array}\right)$}}\mapsto\xi_{1}(a)\xi_{2}(d)
,
where
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
are smooth characters
({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
such that

\xi_{1}(\alpha)=[\overline{\alpha}^{\vec{m}}]\,\,\,\mbox{for all}\,\,\,\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{F,(p),+},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\xi_{1}\xi_{2}(\delta)=[\overline{\delta}^{\vec{k}+2\vec{m}}]\,\,\,\mbox{for all}\,\,\,\delta\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)}^{\times}.

It follows that
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\otimes_{W}\widetilde{C}
decomposes as
the direct sum over such
\xi
of the representations

{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{B({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})}^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})}\xi\cong\bigotimes_{v\nmid p}{\!}^{{}^{\displaystyle{\prime}}}{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{B(F_{v})}^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F_{v})}\xi_{v},

where
\otimes^{\prime}
denotes the restricted tensor product and

\xi_{v}
the restriction of
\xi
to
B(F_{v})
. Finally if

({\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{B(F_{v})}^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(F_{v})}\xi_{v})^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})}\neq 0
,
then it is one-dimensional, with
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
acting
by multiplication by

\widetilde{a}_{v}=\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)\xi_{1}(\varpi_{v})+\xi_{2}(\varpi_{v})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\widetilde{d}_{v}=\xi_{1}(\varpi_{v})\xi_{2}(\varpi_{v}),

respectively, and the homomorphisms
\widetilde{\theta}:\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}^{c}\to\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
are precisely those defined by

T_{v}\mapsto\widetilde{a}_{v}
,
S_{v}\mapsto\widetilde{d}_{v}
for pairs of characters
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
as above.

The analogous statement describing the possible homomorphisms

\theta:\mathbb{T}^{c}\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}
now follows. In particular we conclude that

a_{v}=\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)\overline{\xi}_{1}(\varpi_{v})+\overline{\xi}_{2}(\varpi_{v})\quad\mbox{and}\quad d_{v}=\overline{\xi}_{1}(\varpi_{v})\overline{\xi}_{2}(\varpi_{v}),

where
\overline{\xi}_{1}
and
\overline{\xi}_{2}
are smooth characters
({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{\times}
such that

\overline{\xi}_{1}(\alpha)=\overline{\alpha}^{\vec{m}}\,\,\,\mbox{for all}\,\,\,\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{F,(p),+},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\overline{\xi}_{1}\overline{\xi}_{2}(\delta)=\overline{\delta}^{\vec{k}+2\vec{m}}\,\,\,\mbox{for all}\,\,\,\delta\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)}^{\times}.

The desired representation
\rho_{f}
is therefore given by
\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}^{-1}\chi_{1}\oplus\chi_{2}
, where

\chi_{i}
corresponds via class field theory to the unique extension of
\xi_{i}
to a character
{\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\times}\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{\times}
trivial on
F_{\infty,+}^{\times}F^{\times}
(and necessarily also the maximal pro-
p
subgroup of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,p}^{\times}
).

Suppose now that
{\mathfrak{m}}
is a maximal ideal of
\mathbb{T}^{0}
, or equivalently that it is associated to an eigenform
f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)=H^{0}(Y_{U,E}^{\min},{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}^{\operatorname{sub}})
. By Lemma
4.3.1
, we may choose an integer
\ell\geq 0
such that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\min}_{\vec{\ell}+\vec{\delta},\vec{0},E}
is ample. We thus have

H^{1}(Y_{U,E}^{\min},{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k}+N(\vec{\ell}+\vec{\delta}),\vec{m},E}^{\operatorname{sub}})=0

for sufficiently large integers
N
.

Since
\vec{\ell}+\vec{\delta}\in\Xi_{\min}\subset\Xi_{\mathrm{Hasse}}
, some positive multiple is the weight of a product of partial Hasse invariants. More explicitly, we have
(p-1)(\vec{\ell}+\vec{\delta})=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}r_{\sigma}\vec{h}_{\sigma}
, where

r_{\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j}}=\ell(e_{\mathfrak{p}}+(j-1)(p-1))+(e_{\mathfrak{p}}(e_{{\mathfrak{p}}}-1)+(j-1)(j-2)(p-1))/2.

Thus if
N=M(p-1)
, then multiplication by

\prod_{\sigma}H_{\sigma}^{Mr_{\sigma}}
defines a Hecke-equivariant injective map

S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)\hookrightarrow S_{\vec{k}+N(\vec{\ell}+\vec{\delta}),\vec{m}}(U,E).

We may therefore replace
\vec{k}
by
\vec{k}+N(\vec{\ell}+\vec{\delta})
for some sufficiently large
N
divisible by
p-1
so as to assume
H^{1}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E}^{\min},{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}^{\operatorname{sub}})=0
.

Since the character
(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}\to E^{\times}
defined by
\alpha\mapsto\overline{\alpha}^{\vec{m}+\vec{k}/2}
has kernel containing
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}\cap U^{p}
, and
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}U^{p}
has finite index in
(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
, we may extend it to a character
\xi:\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)}\to E^{\times}

(after enlarging
E
and hence
K
if necessary).
Multiplication by the section
e_{\xi}\in H^{0}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E},{\mathcal{N}})
(defined in §
3.5
) yields an isomorphism of sheaves

{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}^{\operatorname{sub}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},E}^{\operatorname{sub}}
, so we have that
H^{1}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E}^{\min},{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},E}^{\operatorname{sub}})=0
. Since the closed immersion
t:{\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E}^{\min}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}_{U}^{\min}
induces an isomorphism
13
13

13

Note that this is false with
\operatorname{sub}
replaced by
\min
unless
\vec{k}
is parallel.

t^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k}}^{\operatorname{sub}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},E}^{\operatorname{sub}}
, we have a short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k}}^{\operatorname{sub}}\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k}}^{\operatorname{sub}}\longrightarrow t_{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},E}^{\operatorname{sub}}\longrightarrow 0,

so the vanishing of
H^{1}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E}^{\min},{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},E}^{\operatorname{sub}})

implies that of the finitely generated
{\mathcal{O}}
-module

H^{1}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U}^{\min},{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k}}^{\operatorname{sub}})
, and hence the surjectivity of the reduction map

S_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathcal{O}})=H^{0}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U}^{\min},{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k}}^{\operatorname{sub}})\to H^{0}({\mathcal{Y}}_{U,E}^{\min},{\mathcal{A}}_{\vec{k},E}^{\operatorname{sub}})=S_{\vec{k}}(U,E).

Composition with the isomorphism
e_{\xi}
of (
4
) thus yields a surjection

\pi:S_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathcal{O}})\to S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E),

such that

T_{v}(\pi(h))=\xi(\varpi_{v})\pi(T_{v}(h))\quad\mbox{and}\quad S_{v}(\pi(h))=\xi(\varpi_{v})^{2}\pi(S_{v}(h))

for all
h\in S_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathcal{O}})
and
v\not\in Q
.

Letting
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}^{0}
denote the finite flat

{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra of endomorphisms of
S_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathcal{O}})

generated by the
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for
v\not\in Q
, we obtain
a surjection

\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}^{0}\to\mathbb{T}^{0}
sending
T_{v}
to
\xi(\varpi_{v})T_{v}
and
S_{v}
to
\xi(\varpi_{v})^{2}S_{v}
for all
v\not\in Q
.
The same argument as for
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}^{c}
thus yields an eigenform
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}\in S_{\vec{k}}(U,\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p})
such that
T_{v}\widetilde{f}^{\prime}=\widetilde{a}^{\prime}_{v}\widetilde{f}
and

for all

, where the
\widetilde{a}^{\prime}_{v}
and
\widetilde{d}^{\prime}_{v}

are lifts of the
\xi(\varpi_{v})^{-1}a_{v}
and
\xi(\varpi_{v})^{-2}d_{v}
to
\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}
.
Viewing the
\widetilde{a}^{\prime}_{v}
and
\widetilde{d}^{\prime}_{v}
as elements of
\mathbb{C}
via the chosen embedding
{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
, we may replace
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}
by an eigenform in
S_{\vec{k}}(U,\mathbb{C})
with the same eigenvalues. We thus obtain an automorphic representation
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{k}}
such that the local factor
\pi^{\prime}_{v}
is the unramified principal series with
T_{v}=\widetilde{a}^{\prime}_{v}
and
S_{v}=\widetilde{d}^{\prime}_{v}
on

(\pi^{\prime}_{v})^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})}
for all
v\not\in Q
.

Suppose now that
d=[F:\mathbb{Q}]
is even, and let
B
be the quaternion algebra over
F
such that
\Sigma^{B}=\Sigma_{\infty}
.
We may then apply the Jacquet-Langlands correspondence (in the form of Theorem
2.3.1
) to obtain an automorphic representation
\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{k}}^{B}
such that
\Pi^{\prime}_{v}\cong\pi^{\prime}_{v}
for all

, and hence an eigenform
\widetilde{\varphi}^{\prime}\in S^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U_{B},\mathbb{C})=M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U_{B},\mathbb{C})
with the same eigenvalues as
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}
for all
v\not\in Q
, where
U_{B}
corresponds to
U
under the isomorphism
B_{{\mathbb{A}},\mathbf{f}}^{\times}\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}})
obtained from a choice of maximal order
{\mathcal{O}}_{B}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}\cong M_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})
for all

.
It then follows from the identifications (
5
) and

\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U_{B},{\mathcal{O}})=M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U_{B},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p})
that there is such an eigenform in
M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U_{B},{\mathcal{O}})
, and hence a non-zero
\varphi^{\prime}\in M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U_{B},E)
such that
T_{v}(\varphi^{\prime})=\xi(\varpi_{v})^{-1}a_{v}\varphi^{\prime}
and

S_{v}(\varphi^{\prime})=\xi(\varpi_{v})^{-2}d_{v}\varphi^{\prime}
for all

. The analogue of (
4
) for

defined in §

\varphi\in M^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{B},E)
with the same eigenvalues as our original

.

Now let
U^{\prime}=\{\,u\in U\,|\,\mbox{$u_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\equiv 1\bmod{\mathfrak{p}}$ for all ${\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}$}\,\}
and define
{\mathbb{T}}^{B}
to be the

E
-subalgebra of endomorphisms of

generated by the operators
T_{v}
and

for all

. Similarly letting

be the finite flat

{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra of endomorphisms of

generated by the same operators, the Hecke equivariant surjection

yields a surjection
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}^{B}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{T}^{B}
.
Combining this with the Hecke-equivariant inclusion

(10)

M^{B}_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{B},E)\subset M^{B}_{\vec{2}}(U^{\prime}_{B},E)\otimes_{E}D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E},

we deduce the existence of an
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra homomorphism

\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}^{B}\to\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
sending

and
S_{v}\mapsto\widetilde{d}_{v}

for all

, where the
\widetilde{a}_{v}
and
\widetilde{d}_{v}
are lifts of the
a_{v}
and

to

.
Furthermore it follows from (
5
) that

\widetilde{a}_{v},\widetilde{d}_{v}\in{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}
, and that
there is a Hecke eigenform

\widetilde{\varphi}\in M^{B}_{\vec{2}}(U_{B}^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
such that

T_{v}\widetilde{\varphi}=\widetilde{a}_{v}\widetilde{\varphi}
and
S_{v}\widetilde{\varphi}=\widetilde{d}_{v}\widetilde{\varphi}
for all
v\not\in Q
.

Suppose that
\widetilde{\varphi}\in I_{\vec{2}}^{B}(U_{B}^{\prime},{\mathbb{C}})=({\mathcal{I}}^{B}_{\vec{2}})^{U_{B}^{\prime}}
(as defined in §
2.4
). As a representation of
B_{{\mathbb{A}},\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
,

{\mathcal{I}}_{\vec{2}}^{B}

decomposes as
\oplus_{\psi}\psi\circ\det
, where
\psi
runs over the smooth characters of
{\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{\times}
which are trivial on
F_{+}^{\times}
. We therefore have

I^{B}_{\vec{2}}(U_{B}^{\prime},\mathbb{C})=\bigoplus_{\psi}\mathbb{C}f_{\psi}

as a
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}_{B}
-module,
where
\psi
runs over characters
{\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{\times}/(F_{+}^{\times}\det(U^{\prime}))\to{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}^{\times}
, and

T_{v}f_{\psi}=(\mathrm{Nm}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(v)+1)\psi(\varpi_{v})\quad\mbox{and}\quad S_{v}f_{\psi}=\psi(\varpi_{v})

for all
v\not\in Q
. It follows that
a_{v}=(\mathrm{Nm}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(v)+1)\overline{\psi}(\varpi_{v})
and
d_{v}=\overline{\psi}(\varpi_{v})
for such a character
\overline{\psi}
, and the conclusion of the theorem is satisfied by
\rho_{f}=\chi^{-1}_{\mathrm{cyc}}\chi\oplus\chi
where
\chi
is the character corresponding to
\psi
by class field theory.

Finally if
\widetilde{\varphi}\not\in I_{\vec{2}}^{B}(U_{B}^{\prime},{\mathbb{C}})
, then its image in
S_{\vec{2}}(U_{B}^{\prime},{\mathbb{C}})
generates a cuspidal automorphic representation
\Pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{2}}^{B}
such that
\Pi_{v}
is the unramified principal series such that
T_{v}=\widetilde{a}_{v}
and
S_{v}=\widetilde{d}_{v}
on
\Pi_{v}^{{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}^{\times}}
for all

. Applying Theorem
2.3.1
to

and then Theorem
5.1.1
to
\pi={\operatorname{JL}\,}(\Pi)
yields a representation
\rho_{\pi}
such that
\rho_{f}=\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
satisfies the conclusion of the theorem.

Suppose now that
d=[F:{\mathbb{Q}}]
is odd. The same proof as in the case of even

then carries over, but with
B
chosen so that

\Sigma^{B}=\Sigma_{\infty}-\{\sigma_{0}\}
for some

and only the following other change.

The analogue of the inclusion (

) is the Hecke-equivariant map

\pi_{U}^{*}:M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}^{B}(U_{B},E)=H^{1}(Y^{B}_{U_{B}},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E})\longrightarrow H^{1}(Y^{B}_{U^{\prime}_{B}},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E})=M_{\vec{2}}^{B}(U^{\prime}_{B},E)\otimes_{E}D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}

induced by the projection
\pi_{U}:Y^{B}_{U^{\prime}_{B}}\to Y^{B}_{U_{B}}
,
which is not necessarily injective. If
U^{p}
is sufficiently small that
\alpha\equiv 1\bmod{\mathfrak{p}}
for all

and

, then

\pi_{U}
is étale with Galois group

G=U_{B}/U_{B}^{\prime}\cong\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}}\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})
, and
the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence identifies
\ker(\pi_{U}^{*})
with

H^{1}(G,H^{0}(Y^{B}_{U^{\prime}_{B}},E)\otimes_{E}{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}),

compatibly
14
14

14

The compatibility is presumably a formal consequence of general functoriality properties of the Grothendieck spectral sequences underlying this identification, but for lack of a clear reference to that effect, we note that it follows from the explicit description of the edge maps given in the Appendix of
[
Mum70
, §I.2]
.
with the action of the Hecke operators.
Moreover note that

H^{0}(Y^{B}_{U^{\prime}_{B}},E)
is isomorphic to the space of functions

, where

.
Assuming further that

Shapiro’s Lemma gives a canonical isomorphism

where

. Note in particular that since

is abelian of order prime to

, the representation

decomposes as a direct sum of characters.
Furthermore if
h\in(B_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
and
U_{2}\subset hU_{1}h^{-1}
for a pair of open compact subgroups

as above, then the map

\mathrm{Coind}_{\delta_{1}(G)}^{C_{U_{1}^{\prime}}}H^{1}(G_{1},D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Coind}_{\delta_{2}(G)}^{C_{U_{2}^{\prime}}}H^{1}(G_{1},D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E})

induced by

is defined by composition with multiplication by the image of

in

. An argument similar to the ones in the analyses of

and

then shows that
a_{v}=(\mathrm{Nm}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(v)+1)\overline{\psi}(\varpi_{v})
and

for some character
\overline{\psi}:{\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{\times}/(F_{+}^{\times}\det(U^{\prime}))\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{\times}
, and hence the conclusion of the theorem is satisfied by a representation
\rho_{f}
of the form
\chi^{-1}_{\mathrm{cyc}}\chi\oplus\chi
.

We may therefore assume
\varphi\in M_{\vec{2}}(U_{B}^{\prime},E)\otimes_{E}D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},E}
and complete the proof as in the case of even
d
. Note also that having obtained an eigenform
\widetilde{\varphi}\in M_{\vec{2}}^{B}(U^{\prime}_{B},{\mathbb{C}})
with eigenvalues lifting those of

, we immediately obtain the desired cuspidal automorphic representation
\Pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{2}}^{B}
without the need to consider the space
I_{\vec{2}}^{B}(U_{B}^{\prime},{\mathbb{C}})
.
∎

We also record the following immediate consequence of the proof of Theorem
5.2.1
:

Proposition 5.2.3
.

If
f
is as in the statement of Theorem
5.2.1
and
\rho_{f}
is absolutely irreducible, then
f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
.

Proof.

If
f\not\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
, then the exact sequence (
9
) produces an eigenform
C_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
with the same eigenvalues as those of
f
. By construction, the associated Galois representation is then reducible (after possibly extending scalars).
∎

Remark 5.2.4
.

If
p>2
, then any embedding
\overline{F}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
gives a complex conjugation
c\in G_{F}
such
\rho_{f}(c)
has distinct eigenvalues
\pm 1\in E
. It follows that absolute irreducibility is equivalent to irreducibility if
p>2
.

5.3.
Ordinariness

Let us now fix a prime
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
, and suppose that
\vec{m}=-\vec{1}
and
k_{\sigma}\geq 1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
. In particular, the inequality (
2
) is satisfied, so there is an endomorphism
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
of
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
; furthermore
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
commutes with
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for all
v
such that
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})\subset U
.

Theorem 5.3.1
.

Suppose that
k_{\sigma}\geq 1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
and that
k_{\sigma}>1
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, and let
f\in M_{\vec{k},-\vec{1}}(U,E)
be as in Theorem
5.2.1
. If
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}f=a_{{\mathfrak{p}}}f
for some
a_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\in E^{\times}
, then

\rho_{f}|_{G_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}\sim\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\chi_{2}&*\\
0&\chi_{\operatorname{cyc}\,}^{-1}\chi_{1}\end{array}\right)

for some characters
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}:G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\to E^{\times}
,
where
\chi_{2}
is the unramified character sending
{\operatorname{Frob}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
to
a_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
.

Remark 5.3.2
.

The key step in the proof (maintaining ordinariness in the shift from weight
\vec{k}
and prime-to-
{\mathfrak{p}}
level to weight
\vec{2}
and level
{\mathfrak{p}}
) is based on a fundamental principle (and technique) conceived by Hida (see
[
Hid88
, §8]
, for example). Aside from this and some technical adaptation of the preceding proof, the rest of the work is in dealing with cases where we know a priori that the global Galois representation is reducible, and therefore so is its restriction to the local Galois group, but we still need to show it has an unramified constituent with the correct Frobenius eigenvalue.

Proof.

As usual, we may replace
U
by an arbitrary small open compact subgroup containing
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})
. In particular we may assume
U=U({\mathfrak{n}})
for some
{\mathfrak{n}}
prime to
p
.

Suppose first that
f\not\in S_{\vec{k},-\vec{1}}(U,E)
. Recall from the proof of Theorem
5.2.1
that

\rho_{f}=\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}^{-1}\chi_{1}\oplus\chi_{2}
, where the

\chi_{i}
correspond to characters

\overline{\xi}_{i}:\mathbb{A}_{F}^{\times}\to F^{\times}
whose
restrictions to
{\mathcal{O}}_{F,p}^{\times}
are determined by

\vec{k}
and
\vec{m}
. In particular, since

\vec{m}=-\vec{1}
, the character
\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}^{-1}\chi_{1}
is unramified at
{\mathfrak{p}}
(in fact at all primes over
p
), so the content of the theorem in this case is the characterization of
a_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
as a Frobenius eigenvalue.

By assumption, the constant term of the
q
-expansion of
f
is non-zero for some cusp
c\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{U,\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}}
. Writing
c=B({\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p)})_{+}gU^{p}
for some
g\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})
, we may replace
f
by
gf
(shrinking
{\mathfrak{n}}
and enlarging
Q
and
E
as necessary) so as to assume
r_{0}^{1}\neq 0
(writing
r_{m}^{t}
for the coefficient of
q^{m}
in the
q
-expansion of
f
at the cusp at
\infty
associated to an element
t\in(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
). Since
T_{\mathfrak{p}}f=a_{\mathfrak{p}}f
, the assumption on
\vec{k}
and the formula for the effect of
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
on
q
-expansions imply that

r_{0}^{t}=a_{\mathfrak{p}}\epsilon^{-1}r_{0}^{xt}

for all
t\in(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
, where

\epsilon=p^{f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1})\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}},+}^{\times}
and
x=\varpi_{\mathfrak{p}}^{(p)}\in(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
.

Choose
v\not\in Q
representing
{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1}
in the strict ray class group
C_{{\mathfrak{n}}\infty}=F_{+}^{\times}\backslash\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{\times}/U_{\mathfrak{n}}
(where
U_{{\mathfrak{n}}}=\ker(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F}^{\times}\to({\mathcal{O}}_{F}/{\mathfrak{n}})^{\times}
), so
x=\alpha\varpi_{v}u
for some
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
and
u\in U_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
, and hence (
1
) implies that

(11)

r_{0}^{t}=a_{\mathfrak{p}}\epsilon^{-1}r_{0}^{\alpha\varpi_{v}tu}=a_{\mathfrak{p}}\epsilon^{-1}\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(\alpha^{-1})r_{0}^{\varpi_{v}t}=a_{\mathfrak{p}}\mathrm{Nm}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(v)r_{0}^{\varpi_{v}t}

for all
t\in(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
.
Since
T_{v}f=a_{v}f
and
S_{v}f=d_{v}f
, the formula for the effect of
T_{v}
on
q
-expansions implies that

a_{v}r_{0}^{t}=\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)r_{0}^{\varpi_{v}t}+d_{v}r_{0}^{\varpi_{v}^{-1}t}

for all
t\in(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
. The description of the Galois representation in this case renders this equality as

(\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)\overline{\xi}_{1}(\varpi_{v})+\overline{\xi}_{2}(\varpi_{v}))r_{0}^{t}=\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)r_{0}^{\varpi_{v}t}+\overline{\xi}_{1}(\varpi_{v})\overline{\xi}_{2}(\varpi_{v})r_{0}^{\varpi^{-1}_{v}t}.

Combining this with (
11
) and the non-vanishing of
r_{0}^{t}
for
t=1
, we deduce that
a^{-1}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a root of

(X-\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)\overline{\xi}_{1}(\varpi_{v}))(X-\overline{\xi}_{2}(\varpi_{v})).

It follows that either

a_{\mathfrak{p}}=(\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)\overline{\xi}_{1}(\varpi_{v}))^{-1}=\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}^{-1}\chi_{1}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{v}^{-1})=\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}^{-1}\chi_{1}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{\mathfrak{p}}),

or that
a_{\mathfrak{p}}=\overline{\xi}_{2}(\varpi_{v})^{-1}=\chi_{2}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{v}^{-1})
for all

v\not\in Q
representing
{\mathfrak{p}}^{-1}
in
C_{{\mathfrak{n}}\infty}
.
The first possibility gives the desired conclusion with the roles of
\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}^{-1}\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
interchanged, and the second
15
15

15

Note that this is only possible if

U_{B,1}^{\prime}=\{\,u\in U_{B,1},|\,\mbox{$u_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\equiv 1\bmod{\mathfrak{q}}$ for all ${\mathfrak{q}}\in S_{p}$, ${\mathfrak{q}}\neq{\mathfrak{p}}$}\,\}

and consider the map

(14)

M^{B}_{\vec{k}^{\prime},-\vec{1}}(U_{B,1},E)\longrightarrow M^{B}(U^{\prime}_{B,1},E)\otimes_{E}D_{\vec{k}^{\prime},-\vec{1},E},

analogous to (
10
). As in the proof of Theorem
5.2.1
, this is Hecke-equivariant (now also with respect to
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
), injective if
d
is even, and has kernel isomorphic to

H^{1}(G^{\prime},H^{0}(Y^{B}_{U^{\prime}_{B,1}},E)\otimes_{E}D_{\vec{k}^{\prime},-\vec{1},E})\cong{\operatorname{Coind}\,}_{\delta(G^{\prime}_{1})}^{C_{U^{\prime}_{B,1}}}H^{1}(G_{1}^{\prime},D_{\vec{k}^{\prime},-\vec{1},E})

if
d
is odd, where now
G^{\prime}=U_{B,1}/U^{\prime}_{B,1}
is isomorphic to the product of the
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})
for
{\mathfrak{q}}\in S_{p}
,
{\mathfrak{q}}\neq{\mathfrak{p}}
. The same analysis as in the preceding proof shows that
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is
p^{f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
times composition with multiplication by the image of
(\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{\mathfrak{p}}
in
C_{U^{\prime}_{B,1}}
, and hence that
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
annihilates the kernel. It follows that
\varphi
is not in this kernel, and we may deduce as before the existence of an eigenform
\widetilde{\varphi}\in M_{\vec{2}}(U^{\prime}_{B,1},{\mathbb{C}})
with eigenvalues lifting those of
f
, for
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
as well as
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for all
v\not\in Q
.

If
d
is even and
\widetilde{\varphi}\in I_{\vec{2}}^{B}(U_{B,1}^{\prime},\mathbb{C})
, then the same analysis as in the proof of Theorem
5.2.1
shows that
\widetilde{\varphi}
is in the span of
f_{\psi}
for some character
\psi
of
{\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{\times}/F_{+}^{\times}\det(U_{B,1}^{\prime})
(in particular of conductor prime to
{\mathfrak{p}}
). Now however we have

T_{\mathfrak{p}}f_{\psi}=p^{f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\psi((\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{\mathfrak{p}})f_{\psi},

which contradicts the assumption that
a_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\neq 0
.
We may therefore replace
\widetilde{\varphi}
by its image in
S_{\vec{2}}(U^{\prime}_{B,1},{\mathbb{C}})
(whether
d
is even or odd).

As in the proof of Theorem
5.2.1
, we obtain a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{2}}
such that
\pi_{v}
is the unramified principal series such that
T_{v}=\widetilde{a}_{v}
(lifting
a_{v}
) and
S_{v}=\widetilde{d}_{v}
(lifting
d_{v}
) on
\pi_{v}^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,v})}
for all
v\not\in Q
, and moreover that
\pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{U_{1}({\mathfrak{p}})}
contains an eigenvector for
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
with eigenvalue
\widetilde{a}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
lifting
a_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
. In particular
\pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
has conductor dividing
{\mathfrak{p}}
, and more precisely is

•

either a principal series representation of the form
I(\psi_{1}|\cdot|^{1/2},\psi_{2}|\cdot|^{1/2})
with
\psi_{2}
an unramified character such that
\psi_{2}(\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})=\widetilde{a}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
(and
\psi_{1}
at most tamely ramified),

•

or a special representation of the form
\psi\otimes\operatorname{St}
,
where
\psi
is an unramified character such that
\psi(\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})=\widetilde{a}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
and
\operatorname{St}
is the Steinberg representation.

In the first case, Theorem
5.1.1
implies that

\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
is potentially crystalline with

\sigma
-labelled Hodge-Tate weights
(0,1)
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
and
{\operatorname{WD}}(\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}})\cong\psi_{1}\oplus\psi_{2}
. It is then a standard exercise in
p
-adic Hodge theory to conclude that
\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
has the form

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\widetilde{\chi}_{2}}&{*}\\
{0}&{\widetilde{\chi}_{1}\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}^{-1}}\end{array}\right),

where
\widetilde{\chi}_{2}
corresponds to
\psi_{2}
and

\widetilde{\chi}_{1}
to
\psi_{1}|\cdot|
by local class field theory. Similarly in the second case, we get that

\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
has the form

\widetilde{\chi}\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{1}&{*}\\
0&{\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}^{-1}}\end{array}\right),

where
\widetilde{\chi}
corresponds to
\psi
. It therefore follows in either case that
\rho_{f}|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}\cong\overline{\rho}^{{\mathrm{ss}}}_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
has the
desired form.
∎

6.
Geometric weight conjectures

In this section we generalize the geometric Serre weight conjecture of
[
DS23
]
to the case where
p
is ramified in
F
, and discuss the relation with the corresponding generalization due to Gee (see
[
Gee11a
, §4]
) of the algebraic Serre weight conjecture of
[
BDJ10
]
.

6.1.
Geometric modularity

Let

\rho:G_{F}=\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{F}/F)\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})

be an irreducible, continuous, totally odd representation of the absolute Galois group of
F
.

We make the following definition as in
[
DS23
]
:

Definition 6.1.1
.

We say that
\rho
is
geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
if

\rho
is equivalent to the extension of scalars of
\rho_{f}

for some open compact subgroup
U

and eigenform
f\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U;E)
as in the statement of Theorem
5.2.1
.

Thus
\rho
is geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
if there
is a non-zero element
f\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U;E)
, for some
U\supset\mathrm{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,p})

and
E\subset\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
, such that

T_{v}f=\mathrm{tr}(\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_{v}))f\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)S_{v}f=\det(\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_{v}))f

for all but finitely many primes
v
.

Note also that by Proposition
5.2.3
(and our running assumption that
\rho
is irreducible), we can replace
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
by
S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)
in the definition of geometric modularity.

As in the setting of
[
DS23
]
, it is a folklore conjecture that every
\rho
as above is geometrically modular of
some

weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
, and our aim is to predict exactly
which
weights in terms of
of the local behaviour of
\rho
at primes over
p
.

We first note an immediate consequence of the existence of partial Hasse invariants. By the discussion in §
4.1
, we have injective Hecke-equivariant maps

M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U,E)\hookrightarrow M_{\vec{k}+\vec{\ell},\vec{m}}(U,E)

for all
\vec{\ell}\in\Xi_{\mathrm{Hasse}}
, where

\Xi_{\mathrm{Hasse}}=\{\,\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma}r_{\sigma}\vec{h}_{\sigma}\,|\,\mbox{$r_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$ for all $\sigma\in\Sigma$}\,\}

is the (integral) Hasse cone. We therefore have the following:

Proposition 6.1.2
.

Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
and that
\vec{\ell}\in\Xi_{\mathrm{Hasse}}
. If
\rho
is geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
, then
\rho
is geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k}+\vec{\ell},\vec{m})
.

6.2.
Crystalline lifts and labelled Hodge-Tate weights

Suppose now that
\rho:G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\to{\operatorname{Aut}\,}_{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}}(V)\cong\operatorname{GL}_{d}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p})
is a continuous representation of
G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
, given by its
action on a
d
-dimensional vector space
V
over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
.
Recall that
D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)=(V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{crys}})^{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
is a finitely-generated module over

(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{crys}})^{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}=\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}F_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}\cong\prod_{\tau\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}}\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p},

and hence decomposes as the direct sum of finite-dimensional
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
-vector spaces
D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)_{\tau}
for
\tau\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}
(on which
F_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}
acts via
\tau
).
Furthermore
D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)
is equipped with a semi-linear automorphism
\phi
, sending
D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)_{\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i}}
to

D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)_{\tau_{{\mathfrak{p}},i-1}}
, so that
D_{\mathrm{crys}}(V)
is in fact free of finite rank over
\prod_{\tau\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}}\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
, and we say that
\rho
is
crystalline
if this is rank is
d
.

Similarly
\rho
is
de Rham
(resp.
Hodge–Tate
) if the
filtered (resp. graded)
module
D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)=(V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{dR}})^{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
(resp.
D_{\mathrm{HT}}(V)=(V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}B_{\mathrm{HT}})^{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
)
is free of rank
d
over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cong\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
.
Furthermore, if
\rho
is crystalline, then it is de Rham, and hence also Hodge–Tate, in which case

D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V)\cong D_{\mathrm{crys}}\otimes_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}}F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}

and
D_{\mathrm{HT}}(V)\cong{\operatorname{gr}\,}(D_{\mathrm{dR}}(V))
.
In particular, for each

\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, the corresponding component

D_{\mathrm{HT}}(V)_{\sigma}
of
D_{\mathrm{HT}}(V)

is a graded
d
-dimensional vector space over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
.

Definition 6.2.1
.

If
\rho
is Hodge–Tate and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, then the
\sigma
-
labelled weights

of
V
are the
d
-tuple of integers
(w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
such that

w_{1}\geq w_{2}\geq\cdots\geq w_{d}

and
D_{\mathrm{HT}}(V)_{\sigma}
is isomorphic to
\oplus_{i=1}^{d}\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}[-w_{i}]
,
where
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}[-w_{i}]
has degree
-w_{i}
.

We restrict our attention now to the case
d=2
.

Definition 6.2.2
.

Suppose that

\rho:G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
is a continuous representation and that
(\vec{k},\vec{m})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
.
We say that
\rho
has a
crystalline lift
of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
if there exists a continuous representation:

\widetilde{\rho}:G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\to\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p})

such that
\widetilde{\rho}\otimes_{\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}}\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
is isomorphic
to
\rho
, and
\widetilde{\rho}\otimes_{\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}}\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
is crystalline with
\sigma
-labelled
Hodge–Tate weights
(-1-m_{\sigma},-k_{\sigma}-m_{\sigma})
for all

\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
.

Note that this definition differs slightly from the one in
[
DS23
, Def. 7.2.2]
, reflecting the difference in conventions in the definitions of the Hecke action and

\sigma
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights.

6.3.
Statement of the conjecture

First recall the definition of the (positive) minimal cone appearing in Theorem
4.2.3
:

\Xi_{\min}^{+}=\left\{\,\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{\Sigma}\,\left|\begin{array}[]{c}k_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,1}\leq k_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,2}\leq\cdots\leq k_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,e_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\leq pk_{{\mathfrak{p}},i+1,1}\\
\mbox{for all ${\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}$, $i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}{\mathbb{Z}}$}\end{array}\right.\right\},

where we have written
k_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j}
for
k_{\sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},i,j}}
.

Recall also that for
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, we let
\vec{k}_{\mathfrak{p}}
denote its image in
\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}
under the natural projection.

Our generalization of Conjecture 7.3.1 of
[
DS23
]
is then:

Conjecture 6.3.1
.

Let
\rho:G_{F}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p})

be an irreducible, continuous, totally odd representation, and let
\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
.
There exists
\vec{k}_{\mathrm{min}}=\vec{k}_{\mathrm{min}}(\rho,\vec{m})\in\Xi_{\mathrm{min}}^{+}
such that the following hold:

(1)

\rho
is geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
if and only if
\vec{k}-\vec{k}_{\mathrm{min}}\in\Xi_{\mathrm{Hasse}}
;

(2)

if
\vec{k}\in\Xi_{\mathrm{min}}^{+}
, then
\vec{k}-\vec{k}_{\mathrm{min}}\in\Xi_{\mathrm{Hasse}}
if and only if
\rho|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}

has a crystalline lift of weight
(\vec{k}_{\mathfrak{p}},\vec{m}_{\mathfrak{p}})
for all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
.

We can view the conjecture as comprising several parts:

•

Firstly, we incorporate the folklore conjecture that every continuous, irreducible, totally odd

\rho
is modular, in the sense that it arises as
\rho_{f}
from a mod
p
Hilbert modular eigenform
f
of
some
weight. While this can be called the “weak version” of (the analogue of) Serre’s Conjecture in this context, it is almost certainly the hardest part.

We remark on a possible alternative formulation of the weak version of the conjecture. Note that it follows from the construction of
\rho_{f}
in the proof of Theorem
5.2.1
that if it is irreducible, then it arises as
{\overline{\rho}}_{\pi}
for some
\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{2}}
; note however that the conductor of
\pi
need not be prime to
p
.
Conversely, we will see below (Corollary
6.5.7
) that if
\pi
is as in the statement of Theorem
5.1.1
(for any
\vec{k}
and
w
all of the same parity), and
{\overline{\rho}}_{\pi}
is irreducible, then it arises as
\rho_{f}
for some
f
as in Theorem
5.2.1
. The weak version is therefore equivalent to the assertion that every continuous, irreducible, totally odd
\rho
is of the form
{\overline{\rho}}_{\pi}
for some
\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{2}}
.

•

Assuming
\rho
is geometrically modular (of some weight), the existence of
\vec{k}_{\min}
as in (1) is an assertion purely about mod
p
Hilbert modular forms. Recall from Theorem
4.2.3
that for each eigenform
f
giving rise to
\rho
, we have
\vec{k}_{\min}(f)\in\Xi_{\min}^{+}
(the irreducibility of
\rho_{f}
ruling out the possibility that

\vec{k}_{\min}(f)=\vec{0}
). The conjecture thus predicts that among all such
f
, i.e., those (for fixed
\vec{m}
) with the same systems of Hecke eigenvalues outside a finite set of primes, there are those for which
\vec{k}_{\min}(f)
is uniquely minimal with respect to the partial ordering induced by
\Xi_{\mathrm{Hasse}}
.

The difficulty in proving this arises when
\rho_{f}|_{G_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}
is reducible for multiple primes
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
. More precisely, if
f
is ordinary at
{\mathfrak{p}}
, in the sense of the hypothesis of Theorem
5.3.1
, then
\rho_{f}
will also arise from Hecke eigenforms
g
, also with
\vec{m}=-\vec{1}
but different
\vec{k}_{\min}
, such that
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(g)=0
. The conjectured existence of
\vec{k}_{\min}
as in (1) is based on the expectation that
\rho
arises from an
f
(with
\vec{m}=-\vec{1}
) which is simultaneously ordinary at all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
for which
\rho|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
is ramified and ordinary (in the sense of the conclusion of Theorem
5.3.1
), and with
k_{\sigma}=1
for all
\sigma\in S_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for which
\rho|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
is unramified. The fact that this holds (at least under mild technical hypotheses) follows from the main results of
[
BLGG12
,
BLGG13
]
and forthcoming work of the authors.

•

Complementarily, the existence of
\vec{k}_{\min}
as in (2) is a statement purely about integral
p
-adic Hodge theory. Indeed let
\rho:G_{L}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
be any continuous representation, where
L
is any finite extension of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
,
and (re)define
\Xi_{\min}^{+}
and
\Xi_{\mathrm{Hasse}}
as subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, where
\Sigma
is the set of embeddings
\{\,\sigma:L\to\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}\,\}
. The assertion then amounts
18
18

18

The local statement is a priori stronger, but in fact equivalent by
[
GK19
, Prop. A.1]
. Note also that we have assumed here that
\vec{m}=-\vec{1}
, to which the general case reduces by twisting arguments discussed below.
