\varphi\in M^{B}_{\vec{k},-\vec{1}}(U,\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})[{\mathfrak{m}}_{\rho}^{Q}]
(with
B
as in Definition
6.6.4
) such that for each
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S
, we have
T_{\mathfrak{p}}\varphi=a_{\mathfrak{p}}\varphi

from some
a_{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
.

We then proceed exactly as in the proof of part (2) of Theorem
6.5.5
, except that instead of
\mathbb{T}
, we use the polynomial ring
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
over (some sufficiently large)
{\mathcal{O}}
in the variables
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for
v\not\in Q
, and
T_{\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
for
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S
. Letting
\xi
,
a_{v}^{\prime}
and
d_{v}^{\prime}
(for
v\not\in Q
) be as in the proof of Theorem
6.5.5
and setting
a_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\prime}=\epsilon^{-1}\xi(\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{(p)})a_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S
,
we get that
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}
is in the support of
M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,E)
, where

\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}
is the
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra homomorphism defined by

T_{v}\mapsto a_{v}^{\prime}\,\,\mbox{and}\,\,S_{v}\mapsto d_{v}^{\prime},\,\,\mbox{for $v\not\in Q$;}\quad T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\mapsto a_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\prime},\,\,\mbox{for ${\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}$.}

It then follows as in the proof there that
M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathcal{O}})_{\widetilde{{\mathfrak{m}}}}
is non-zero and torsion-free. This implies the existence of an eigenform in
M^{B}_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathcal{O}})
for the operators in
\widetilde{\mathbb{T}}
, with eigenvalues lifting those above (after possibly enlarging
{\mathcal{O}}
). This in turn gives an automorphic representation to which we may apply the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence to obtain an eigenform in
M_{\vec{k}}(U,{\mathcal{O}})
with the same property. Finally reducing mod
\varpi
and untwisting yields an eigenform
f\in M_{\vec{k},-\vec{1}}(U,E)
with the same eigenvalues as the original
\varphi
for
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for all
v\not\in Q
, as well as the
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S
. In particular
\rho_{f}\sim\rho
and
f
is
{\mathfrak{p}}
-ordinary for all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S
, as required.
∎

Remark 6.6.10
.

The proof of part (1) of Theorem
6.5.5
can be similarly modified to show that if
\rho
is
S
-ordinarily definitely modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
and
D_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}
is irreducible, then
\rho
is
S
-ordinarily algebraically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
.

7.
Partial weight one modularity

In the final sections, we will specialize to the case where
F
is a real quadratic field in which
p
ramifies.
We will determine precisely when the conjecture predicts that a representation
\rho
arises from a Hilbert modular form of partial weight one and prove strong results in this direction, analogous to those of
[
DS23
, §11]
for the quadratic inert case.

In order to do this, we need some extensions of results from
[
DS23
]
and
[
GLS15
]
. We do this in more general settings, as it requires no extra work and could be useful in extending our
methods beyond the ramified quadratic case.

7.1.
Normalized and stabilized eigenforms

We start with slight generalizations of various notions and results from Chapter 10 of
[
DS23
]
. Recall that it is assumed there that
p
is unramified in
F
, and also that
k_{\sigma}\geq 2
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
in statements involving Hecke operators at primes over
p
. Using the results of
[
Dia25
]
(and
[
Dia24
]
), we may remove the assumption that
p
be unramified and relax the assumption on the weight to require only that
k_{\sigma}\geq 1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
.
The proofs in
[
DS23
]
carry over essentially without change, except to adapt them to our notation and conventions.

Firstly, we have the following strengthening of
[
DS23
, Lemma 10.2.1]
:

Lemma 7.1.1
.

If
\rho:G_{F}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
is irreducible and geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
, then there exist an ideal
{\mathfrak{n}}
prime to
p
and a finite extension
E
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
in
\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}

such that
\rho
is equivalent to
\rho_{f}
for some

f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
, where
f
is an eigenform for the operators
T_{v}
for all
v\nmid p
and
S_{v}
for all
v\nmid{\mathfrak{n}}p
. Furthermore if
\vec{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}^{\Sigma}
,
\vec{m}=-\vec{1}
,
S\subset S_{p}
and
\rho
is
S
-ordinarily geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
, then we may take
f
to be an eigenform for
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
for all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
, with
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}f\neq 0
for all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S
.

Indeed the argument in
[
DS23
]
starts with an eigenform, say
g
, in
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
for the operators
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for all
v\nmid{\mathfrak{n}}p
giving rise to
\rho
, and produces one in
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
after possibly shrinking
{\mathfrak{n}}
and enlarging
E
. By the remark after Definition
6.1.1
, we may replace
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
by
S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
, and the fact that we may take
f
to be an eigenform for the
T_{v}
for
v|{\mathfrak{n}}
follows from their commutativity with each other and the
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for
v\nmid{\mathfrak{n}}p
. Under the additional hypothesis, the commutativity of the
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for
v\nmid{\mathfrak{n}}p
with the
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S
allows us to assume
g
is also an eigenform for the
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, with eigenvalue in
E^{\times}
if
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S
. The fact that the same will be true for the resulting eigenform in
M_{\vec{k},-\vec{1}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
follows from the commutativity between the
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
and the double coset operators in the construction of
f
. (Note that we allow
S
to be empty.)

As in
[
DS23
, §10.3]
, we may refine the twisting construction of §
3.5
in order to work with eigenforms of level
U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}})
(for varying
{\mathfrak{n}}
). To align with the conventions of this paper, let us fix
t_{1}\in(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
and

m_{1}\in F_{+}^{\times}
such that

m_{1}t_{1}
generates
\widehat{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{-1}={\mathfrak{d}}^{-1}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{F}}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F}
, and let
c=(t_{1}m_{1})^{-1}
.

Suppose now that
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
and
\xi:{\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\times}/F^{\times}F_{\infty,+}^{\times}\to E^{\times}
is a continuous character such that
\xi(u_{p})=\overline{u}_{p}^{-\vec{\ell}}
for all
u_{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,p}^{\times}
, and let
{\mathfrak{m}}
denote its conductor, i.e., the largest (prime-to-
p
) ideal
{\mathfrak{m}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
such that
\xi
is trivial on
\ker((\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F}^{(p)})^{\times}\twoheadrightarrow({\mathcal{O}}_{F}/{\mathfrak{m}})^{\times})
. Note that the restriction of
\xi
to
({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
is a character as in §
3.5
, i.e.,
\xi(\alpha)=\alpha^{\vec{\ell}}
for all
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
.

The construction in
[
DS23
, §10.3]
then yields the following:

Lemma 7.1.2
.

Suppose that
f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
. Then there exists an element
f_{\xi}\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{\ell}+\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{m}}^{2}{\mathfrak{n}}),E)
with
q
-expansion coefficients given by

r^{t}_{m}(f_{\xi})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\xi((ctm)^{({\mathfrak{m}})})r^{t}_{m}(f),&\mbox{if $(ctm)_{v}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,v}^{\times}$ for all $v|{\mathfrak{m}}$;}\\
0,&\mbox{otherwise;}\end{array}\right.

for all
t\in(\mathbb{A}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
and
m\in t^{-1}\widehat{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{-1}\cap F^{\times}_{+}
(where
\cdot^{({\mathfrak{m}})}
denotes the projection to components prime to
{\mathfrak{m}}
). In particular, if

r^{t}_{m}(f)\neq 0
for some
t,m
such that
(ctm)_{v}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,v}^{\times}
for all
v|{\mathfrak{m}}
, then
f_{\xi}\neq 0
, in which case if
f
is an eigenform for
T_{v}
for all
v\nmid p
and
S_{v}
for all
v\nmid{\mathfrak{n}}p
, then so is
f_{\xi}
for
T_{v}
for all
v\nmid p
and
S_{v}
for all
v\nmid{\mathfrak{m}}{\mathfrak{n}}p
, and
\rho_{f_{\xi}}\sim\chi\otimes\rho_{f}
where
\chi
is the character corresponding to
\xi
via class field theory.

In transporting the argument from
[
DS23
, §10.3]
, note that our
\xi
is the character denoted there by
\xi^{\prime}
. We also point out two typos there:
\xi
should be
\xi^{-1}
in the definition of
\xi_{{\mathfrak{m}}}^{-1}
just before (10.7), and the level
{\mathfrak{m}}{\mathfrak{n}}^{2}
should
{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}{\mathfrak{n}}
just before (10.8). Furthermore the assumption that
{\mathfrak{m}}|{\mathfrak{n}}
has implicitly been dropped at that point. (In fact, if we maintain the assumption that
{\mathfrak{m}}|{\mathfrak{n}}
, then the level could be taken as
{\mathfrak{m}}{\mathfrak{n}}
there, as well as the conclusions of
[
DS23
, Lemma 10.3.2]
and Lemma
7.1.2
above.)

Remark 7.1.3
.

Note that we necessarily have
ctm\in\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F}
in the formula for the
q
-expansion coefficient, and the condition prescribing the first case is that the ideal it defines be prime to
{\mathfrak{m}}
. With this interpretation, the lemma holds also for
f\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
(where now
f_{\xi}\in M_{\vec{k},\vec{\ell}+\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{m}}^{2}{\mathfrak{n}}),E)
); more precisely, in the case
{\mathfrak{m}}={\mathcal{O}}_{F}
, replace
(ctm)^{({\mathfrak{m}})}
by
ct
to obtain a formula that applies also to
m=0
.

We generalize
[
DS23
, Defn. 10.5.1]
as follows (restricted to cusp forms and adapted to our conventions):

Definition 7.1.4
.

Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, with
k_{\sigma}\geq 1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
. We say that an element
f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
is a
normalized eigenform
if the following hold:

•

r_{t_{1}}^{m_{1}}(f)=1
;

•

f
is an eigenform for
T_{v}
for all
v\nmid p
, and
S_{v}
for all
v\nmid{\mathfrak{n}}p
;

•

f_{\xi}\in S_{\vec{k},-\vec{1}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{m}}^{2}{\mathfrak{n}}),E^{\prime})
is an eigenform for
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
for all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
and all characters
\xi:{\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\times}/F^{\times}F_{\infty,+}^{\times}\to E^{\prime\times}
such that
\xi(u_{p})=\overline{u}_{p}^{\vec{m}+\vec{1}}
for all
u_{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,p}^{\times}
(where
E^{\prime}
is any finite extension of
E
and
{\mathfrak{m}}
is the prime-to-
p
conductor of
\xi
).

Note that the first condition implies that the forms
f_{\xi}
in the third are non-zero. Furthermore, if
f
is a normalized eigenform, then so is
f_{\xi^{\prime}}\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{\ell}+\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime 2}{\mathfrak{n}}),E^{\prime})
for any character
\xi^{\prime}:{\mathbb{A}}_{F}^{\times}/F^{\times}F_{\infty,+}^{\times}\to E^{\prime\times}

such that
\xi^{\prime}(u_{p})=\overline{u}_{p}^{-\vec{\ell}}
for all
u_{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,p}^{\times}
(where
{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}
is the prime-to-
p
conductor of
\xi^{\prime}
).

Proposition 7.1.5
.

If
\rho:G_{F}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
is irreducible and
S
-ordinarily geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
with
k_{\sigma}\geq 1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, then there exist an ideal
{\mathfrak{n}}
prime to
p
and a finite extension
E
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
in
\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}

such that
\rho
is equivalent to
\rho_{f}
for some
normalized eigenform
f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
such that
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}f_{\xi}\neq 0
for all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S
and
\xi
as in Definition
7.1.4
.

Proof.

The argument is essentially the same as the proof of
[
DS23
, Prop. 10.5.2]
.

Suppose first that
\vec{m}=-\vec{1}
. Then by Lemma
7.1.1
, there exist
{\mathfrak{n}}
and
E
such that
\rho
arises from an eigenform
f\in S_{\vec{k},-\vec{1}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
for
T_{v}
for all
v
and
S_{v}
for all

v\nmid{\mathfrak{n}}p
, with
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}f\neq 0
for all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S
. Therefore it suffices to prove that

r_{t_{1}}^{m_{1}}(f)\neq 0
, as then Lemma
7.1.2

implies the third condition in Definition
7.1.4
holds (and we may rescale
f
to achieve the first condition).

We prove that if
r_{t_{1}}^{m_{1}}(f)=0
, then
r_{t}^{m}(f)=0
for all
t\in({\mathbb{A}}_{F,\mathbf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
and
m\in t^{-1}\widehat{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{-1}\cap F^{\times}_{+}
, contradicting that
f\neq 0
. We proceed
by induction on
||ctm||^{-1}=\mathrm{Disc}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}^{-1}||tm||^{-1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
(where
c=(t_{1}m_{1})^{-1}
).

Firstly if
||ctm||=1
, then letting
u=(ctm)^{(p)}\in(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F}^{(p)})^{\times}
and
\alpha=m^{-1}m_{1}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,(p),+}^{\times}
, we have
r_{m}^{t}(f)=r_{m_{1}}^{t_{1}}(f)=0
by (
1
).

For the induction step, suppose that
||ctm||<1
and that
r^{t^{\prime}}_{m^{\prime}}(f)=0
for all
t^{\prime},m^{\prime}
such that
||tm||<||t^{\prime}m^{\prime}||
. Since
ctm\in\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F}
and
||ctm||<1
, there is a prime
v
such that
m\in vt^{-1}\widehat{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{-1}
.

Suppose first that
v\nmid p
. If
v|{\mathfrak{n}}
or
m\not\in v^{2}t^{-1}\widehat{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{-1}
, then the formula for the effect of
T_{v}
on
q
-expansions gives

\mathrm{Nm}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(v)r_{m}^{t}(f)=r_{m}^{\varpi_{v}^{-1}t}(T_{v}f)=a_{v}r_{m}^{\varpi_{v}^{-1}t}(f)=0

by the induction hypothesis, where
a_{v}
is the eigenvalue of
T_{v}
on
f
.

If
v\nmid{\mathfrak{n}}
and
m\in v^{2}t^{-1}\widehat{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{-1}
, then
we proceed similarly using instead that

\mathrm{Nm}_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(v)r_{m}^{t}(f)=r_{m}^{\varpi_{v}^{-1}t}(T_{v}f)-r_{m}^{\varpi_{v}^{-2}t}(S_{v}f).

If
v|p
, then the argument is similar to the case
v\nmid p
, with the subcase where
k_{\sigma}=1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{v}
corresponding to the one where
v\nmid{\mathfrak{n}}
.

This completes the proof in the case
\vec{m}=-\vec{1}
, and the general case follows by twisting as in
[
DS23
]
.
∎

Definition 7.1.6
.

Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, with
k_{\sigma}\geq 1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
. We say that a normalized eigenform
f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
is
stabilized
if
T_{v}f=0
for all
v|{\mathfrak{n}}
.

We have the following refinement of
[
DS23
, Lemma10.6.2]

(by the same proof):

Lemma 7.1.7
.

Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, with
k_{\sigma}\geq 1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, and that
{\mathfrak{n}}\subset{\mathfrak{m}}
are non-zero ideals of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
prime to
p
.
If
\rho\sim\rho_{f}
for some normalized eigenform

f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{m}}),E)
, then
\rho\sim\rho_{f^{\prime}}
for some normalized eigenform
f^{\prime}\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E^{\prime})
such that
a_{{\mathfrak{p}},\xi}(f)=a_{{\mathfrak{p}},\xi}(f^{\prime})
for all characters
\xi
as in Definition
7.1.4
, where
E^{\prime}
is (at most) a quadratic extension of
E
.
Furthermore if
{\operatorname{ord}}_{v}({\mathfrak{n}})\geq\min(2,1+{\operatorname{ord}}_{v}({\mathfrak{m}}))
for all
v|{\mathfrak{n}}
, then we may take
f^{\prime}
as above to be a stabilized eigenform.

Note that if
f
is a stabilized eigenform, then
\rho_{f}
and
{\mathfrak{n}}
determine its Hecke eigenvalues for all
T_{v}
for
v\nmid p
and
S_{v}
for
v\nmid{\mathfrak{n}}p
, and this in turn determines the
q
-expansion coefficients
r_{m}^{t}(f)
for all
t
and
m
such that
(ctm)_{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,p}^{\times}
. However this information does not necessarily determine the eigenvalue of
T_{\mathfrak{p}}
on
f_{\xi}
for
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
and characters
\xi
as
in Definition
7.1.4
.
Let us denote this eigenvalue by
a_{{\mathfrak{p}},\xi}(f)
. As in the discussion before Definition
6.6.3
, these are related by the formula

a_{{\mathfrak{p}},\xi_{2}}(f)=\mu({\operatorname{Frob}}_{\mathfrak{p}})a_{{\mathfrak{p}},\xi_{1}}(f)

as
\xi
varies, where
\mu
is the unramified character
G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\to E^{\times}
corresponding via local class field theory to
\xi_{2}\xi_{1}^{-1}|_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}}
.

If
k_{\sigma}>1
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, then Theorem
5.3.1
implies that the
a_{{\mathfrak{p}},\xi}(f)
can only be non-zero if the representation
\chi\otimes\rho_{f}
has a non-trivial unramified subrepresentation, or equivalently, if
\rho_{f}
has a non-trivial subrepresentation on which
I_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
acts as
\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{\sigma}^{-1-m_{\sigma}}
(in the notation of §
6.4
). Note also that these subrepresentations (of which there are at most two) determine the possible (non-zero) systems of values of
a_{{\mathfrak{p}},\xi}(f)
.

On the other hand, if
k_{\sigma}=1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, then
\rho_{f}|^{\mathrm{ss}}_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
is unramified and the
a_{{\mathfrak{p}},\xi}(f)
are determined
by Proposition
6.6.1
.

Definition 7.1.8
.

Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, with
k_{\sigma}\geq 1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, and that
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
is such that
k_{\sigma}>1
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
. We say that a stabilized eigenform
f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
is
strongly
{\mathfrak{p}}
-stabilized
if
T_{\mathfrak{p}}(f_{\xi})=0
for all
\xi
as in Definition
7.1.4
, or equivalently if
r_{m}^{t}(f)=0
for all
t
and
m
such that
(ctm)_{\mathfrak{p}}\not\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
. We say that
f
is
strongly stabilized
if it is strongly
{\mathfrak{p}}
-stabilized for all
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
is such that
k_{\sigma}>1
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

By the discussion preceding Definition
7.1.8
, if
f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}},E))
is a stabilized eigenform and
{\mathfrak{p}}\in S_{p}
is such that
k_{\sigma}>1
for some
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}
, then it is automatically strongly
{\mathfrak{p}}
-stabilized unless
\rho_{f}
has a non-trivial subrepresentation on which
I_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}
acts as
\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{\sigma}^{-1-m_{\sigma}}
. On the other hand, if
\rho_{f}
has such a subrepresentation, then it does not necessarily arise from
a strongly
{\mathfrak{p}}
-stabilized eigenform of the same weight

(\vec{k},\vec{m})
. Note however that
\Theta_{\tau}(f)
is strongly
{\mathfrak{p}}
-stabilized for any
\tau\in\Sigma_{{\mathfrak{p}},0}
.

The same argument as for
[
DS23
, Lemma 10.6.5]
, together with Proposition
6.6.1
, gives the following:

Lemma 7.1.9
.

Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, with
k_{\sigma}\geq 1
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
, and that
{\mathfrak{n}}
is a non-zero ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
prime to
p
. If

\rho:G_{F}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(E)
is absolutely irreducible, then

\rho\sim\rho_{f}
for at most one strongly stabilized eigenform
f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
.

7.2.
Quado-Barsotti–Tate representations

In order to describe local Galois representations with repeated

\sigma
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights, we need a slight generalization of results of Gee, Liu and Savitt
[
GLS15
]
. (See
[
Wie25
, §3]
for an extension along these lines in the unramified case.)

Fix a finite extension
L
of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, write
L_{0}
for its maximal unramified subextension, and let
f=[L_{0}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}]
,
e=[L:L_{0}]
. Let
\Sigma_{L}
denote the set of embeddings
\sigma:L\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
, which we index as
\sigma_{i,j}
for
i\in\mathbb{Z}/f\mathbb{Z}
,
j=1,\ldots,e
, with our usual convention that for each
i
, the embeddings
\sigma_{i,1},\ldots,\sigma_{i,e}
have the same restriction to
L_{0}
, say
\tau_{i}
, and that
\tau_{i+1}=\phi\circ\tau_{i}
.

Recall that
[
GLS15
]
gives a complete description of reductions of certain crystalline representations
\widetilde{\rho}:G_{L}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(K)
which they call
pseudo-Barsotti–Tate
, meaning that for each
i
, the
\sigma_{i,j}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights have the form
(r_{i,j},0)
, where
r_{i,e}\in[1,p]
and
r_{i,j}=1
for
j<e
. We wish to allow some of the
r_{i,j}
to equal
0
, and we will refer to such representations as
quado-Barsotti–Tate
(a concatenation of
quasi-pseudo-Barsotti–Tate
). Since the results are independent of the choice of ordering of
\sigma_{i,1},\ldots,\sigma_{i,e}
for each
i
, we may further assume that

0\leq r_{i,1}\leq r_{i,2}\leq\cdots\leq r_{i,e}\leq p,

so that the quado-Barsotti–Tate condition becomes
r_{i,e-1}\leq 1
for each
i
. For notational convenience, we set
r_{i,0}=0
.

Suppose now that
\widetilde{\rho}
is quado-Barsotti–Tate with
\sigma_{i,j}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(r_{i,j},0)
as above. We define the
degeneracy degree
of
\widetilde{\rho}
to be the element
\vec{\delta}\in[0,e]^{f}
such that
\delta_{i}=\max\{\,j\,|\,r_{i,j}=0\,\}
. (Thus a quado-Barsotti–Tate representation is pseudo-Barsotti–Tate if and only if it has degeneracy degree
\vec{0}
.)

We then have the following extension of
[
GLS15
, Cor. 2.3.10]
(as extended in
[
Wan22
]
to include the case
p=2
) to the quado-Barsotti–Tate setting. We adopt the same notation as in
[
GLS15
]
(except that our
L
is their
K
, our
K
is their
E
, their indexing of embeddings is the reverse of ours, and we let
\prod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
denote the matrix product
A_{n}A_{n-1}\cdots A_{1}
).

Proposition 7.2.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be the Kisin module corresponding to a lattice in a quado-Barsotti–Tate representation
\widetilde{\rho}
as above. There exist matrices
Z^{\prime}_{i,j}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{K})
for
j=0,\ldots,e-1
such that
\mathrm{Fil}^{p,p,\ldots,p}\mathfrak{M}_{i}^{*}=\mathfrak{S}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K},i}\alpha_{i,e-1}\oplus\mathfrak{S}_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K},i}\beta_{i,e-1}
with

(\alpha_{i,e-1},\beta_{i,e-1})=(\mathfrak{e}_{i}^{\prime},\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{\prime})\Lambda^{\prime}_{i,e}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{e-1}Z_{i,j}^{\prime}\Lambda_{i,j}^{\prime}\right),

where
\mathfrak{e}_{i}^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{f}_{i}^{\prime}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{M}_{i}^{*}
(as in
[
GLS15
, Prop.2.3.5(2)]
if
\delta_{i}<e
) and

\Lambda_{i,j}^{\prime}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(u-\pi_{i,j})^{p}&0\\
0&(u-\pi_{i,j})^{p-r_{i,j}}\end{array}\right).

We omit the proof, which is essentially the same as that of
[
GLS15
, Cor.2.3.10]
, except that the induction step (now downwards on
m
due to our different conventions) becomes easier for
m\leq\delta_{i}
, as does the base case if
\delta_{i}=e
. Indeed, if
\delta_{i}=e
, then the base case is immediate from
[
GLS15
, Lemma 2.2.2(3)]
. Furthermore, if
m\leq\delta_{i}
, then the arguments in the third through fifth paragraphs of the proof are unnecessary. To obtain the analogue of the displayed equation in the final paragraph, one may simply take
Z_{i,m}^{\prime}
to be the identity matrix, and apply
21
21

21

Note that the reference there to Lemma 2.2.2(2) should be to Lemma 2.2.2(3).

[
GLS15
, Lemma 2.2.2(3)]
using that
{\operatorname{Fil}\,}^{1}D_{L,i,m}=0
.

We then obtain from this the obvious generalization of
[
GLS15
, Thm. 2.4.1]
with
\Lambda_{i,j}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&(u-\pi_{i,j})^{r_{i,j}}\end{array}\right)
. The proof of Proposition 3.1.3 of
[
GLS15
]
then carries over without change as well, giving the same statement, but with
s_{i}=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}r_{i,j}
for some subset
J_{i}\subset\{1,\ldots,e\}
, so that
s_{i}=x_{i}
or
r_{i,e}+x_{i}
for some
x_{i}\in[0,e-1-\delta_{i}]
, unless
r_{i,e}=0
(i.e.,
\delta_{i}=e
), in which case
s_{i}=0
. This in turn yields the following generalization of
[
GLS15
, Thm. 3.1.4]
:

Theorem 7.2.2
.

Let
T
be a lattice in a quado-Barsotti–Tate representation as above, with
\sigma
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(r_{\sigma},0)
for
\sigma\in\Sigma_{L}
. If
\overline{T}=T\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}E
is reducible, then there is a subset
J\subset\Sigma_{L}
and a basis for
\overline{T}
with respect to which the action of
I_{L}
has the form

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\prod_{\sigma\in J}\omega_{\sigma}^{r_{\sigma}}&*\\
0&\prod_{\sigma\not\in J}\omega_{\sigma}^{r_{\sigma}}\end{array}\right),

where
\omega_{\sigma}:I_{L}\to E^{\times}
is the fundamental character corresponding via local class field theory to the character
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}^{\times}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{\times}\to E^{\times}
induced by
\sigma
.

Note that
\omega_{\sigma_{i,j}}
depends only on
i
, and we henceforth denote it
\omega_{i}
, so
\omega_{i}=\omega_{i-1}^{p}
has order
p^{f}-1
for all
i
.

The same arguments as in
[
GLS15
]
give the following generalization of their Theorem 5.1.5 (using their notation, modified as above):

Theorem 7.2.3
.

Let
T
be a
G_{L}
-stable lattice in a quado-Barsotti–Tate representation as above, with
\sigma_{i,j}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(r_{i,j},0)
for
\sigma_{i,j}\in\Sigma_{L}
. Let
{\mathfrak{M}}
be the Kisin module associated to
T
, and let
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}={\mathfrak{M}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}E
.

Suppose that
\overline{T}:=T\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{K}}E
is reducible, so there exist rank one Kisin modules
\overline{{\mathfrak{N}}}={\mathfrak{M}}(s_{0},\ldots,s_{f-1};a)
and
\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}}={\mathfrak{M}}(t_{0},\ldots,t_{f-1};b)
such that
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}
is an extension of
\overline{{\mathfrak{N}}}
by
\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}}
. Then for each
i
there is an integer
x_{i}\in[0,\varepsilon_{i}]
such that
\{\,s_{i},t_{i}\,\}=\{\,r_{i}+x_{i},\varepsilon_{i}-x_{i}\,\}
, where
r_{i}=r_{i,e}
and
\varepsilon_{i}=\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{e-1}r_{i,j}=\max\{\,0,e-1-\delta_{i}\,\}
.

Furthermore we can choose bases
e_{i},f_{i}
of
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}_{i}
so that
\varphi
has the form

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varphi(e_{i+i})&=&(b)_{i}u^{t_{i}}e_{i}\\
\varphi(f_{i+1})&=&(a)_{i}u^{s_{i}}f_{i}+y_{i}e_{i}\end{array}

where

•

y_{i}\in E[[u]]
is a polynomial with
\deg(y_{i})<s_{i}
;

•

if
t_{i}<r_{i}
, then the nonzero terms of
y_{i}
have degrees in the set
[t_{i}]\cup[r_{i},s_{i}-1]
;

•

except that when there is a nonzero map
\overline{{\mathfrak{N}}}\to\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}}
, we must also allow
y_{\iota}
to have a term of degree
s_{\iota}+\alpha_{\iota}(\overline{{\mathfrak{N}}})-\alpha_{\iota}(\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}})
for any one choice of
\iota
.

Finally, we need that the proofs of Lemmas 6.1.2 and 6.1.3 of
[
GLS15
]
carry over
22
22

22

These are in fact only needed in the case that the semisimplification of
\overline{T}
is a direct sum of two characters whose ratio is cyclotomic.

without change for quado-Barsotti–Tate representations. Thus in the setting of Theorem
7.2.3
, the exact sequence
0\to\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}}\to\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}\to\overline{{\mathfrak{N}}}\to 0
extends to one of
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-modules

0\longrightarrow\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{N}}}}\longrightarrow 0

with
E
-action such that
\overline{T}\cong\widehat{T}(\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}})
and for all
x\in\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}
, there exist
\alpha\in R
and
y\in R\otimes_{\varphi,{\mathfrak{S}}}\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}
such that
\tau(x)-x=\alpha y
and
\nu_{R}(\alpha)\geq\frac{p}{p-1}+\frac{p}{e}
. Furthermore this
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module is unique, except in the case
\vec{r}=\vec{p}
,
\vec{\delta}=\vec{t}=\vec{0}
.

To make the statements involving
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-modules less cumbersome, we will say that a
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module with
E
-action is
typical
if it satisfies the condition that for all
x\in\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}
, there exist
\alpha\in R
and
y\in R\otimes_{\varphi,{\mathfrak{S}}}\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}
such that
\tau(x)-x=\alpha y
and
\nu_{R}(\alpha)\geq\frac{p}{p-1}+\frac{p}{e}
.

7.3.
The ramified quadratic case:
p
-adic Hodge theory

Now we assume that
L
is a ramified quadratic extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We simplify some of the notation of the preceding section as follows: We write
\Sigma=\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}
for
\Sigma_{L}
,
(k_{1},k_{2})
for the element
(k_{\sigma_{1}},k_{\sigma_{2}})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, and
\omega:I_{L}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
for the fundamental character (which was
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}
in the preceding section).

For
(\vec{k},\vec{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}\times\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, where
\vec{k}=(k_{1},k_{2})
is of the form
(1,w)
with
2\leq w\leq p+1
, we will describe precisely which representations
\rho:G_{L}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
have crystalline lifts of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
in the sense of Definition
6.2.2
, i.e., with
\sigma_{i}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
-(1+m_{i},k_{i}+m_{i})
for
i=1,2
.

In order to provide the desired characterization in the case that
\rho
is reducible, we first define a certain subspace of

H^{1}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}(\chi))
for any character
\chi:G_{L}\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{\times}
. Note that we may write

\chi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{n-1}
for a unique
n\in[1,p-1]
. Let
\widetilde{\chi}:G_{L}\to\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}^{\times}
be any crystalline lift of
\chi
with
\sigma_{1}
-labeled weight
-1
and
\sigma_{2}
-labeled weight
n
.

Recall that
H^{1}_{f}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}))
is defined as the kernel of the natural map

H^{1}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}))\longrightarrow H^{1}(G_{L},B_{\mathrm{crys}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi})),

and
H^{1}_{f}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}))
as the preimage of

H^{1}_{f}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}))
in

H^{1}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}))
. We then define
V_{\chi}
as
image of
H^{1}_{f}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}))
in
H^{1}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}(\chi))
. Since
H^{1}_{f}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}))
is one-dimensional over
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}
(by
[
FPR94
, 3.3.11]
, for example), it follows that

H^{1}_{f}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}))/H^{1}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}))_{\mathrm{tor}}

is free of rank one over
\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}
, and therefore that

\dim_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}}V_{\chi}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&\mbox{if $\chi\neq 1$;}\\
2,&\mbox{if $\chi=1$.}\end{array}\right.

We then have the following characterization of the classes
23
23

23

See
[
BS22
]
for a more explicit description of such spaces in terms of Kummer theory.
in
V_{\chi}
in terms of
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-modules:

Proposition 7.3.1
.

Suppose that
\rho:G_{L}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(E)
is a representation of the form
\psi\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\chi&c\\
0&1\end{array}\right)
, where
\psi|_{I_{L}}=\omega
,
\chi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{n-1}
for some
n\in[1,p-1]
, and

c\in Z^{1}(G_{L},E(\chi))
. Then
c
represents a class in
V_{\chi}
if and only if
\rho
arises from a typical
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module with
E
-action
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}}
fitting in an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{N}}}}\longrightarrow 0,

where the underlying Kisin modules
\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}}
,
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}
and
\overline{{\mathfrak{N}}}
are as in the conclusion of Theorem
7.2.3
with
\overline{{\mathfrak{N}}}={\mathfrak{M}}(n;a)
and
\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}}={\mathfrak{M}}(1;b)
for some
a,b\in E^{\times}
.

In particular,
V_{\chi}
is independent of the choice of the character
\widetilde{\chi}
in its definition.

Proof.

Suppose first that
c
represents a class in
V_{\chi}
. Letting
\widetilde{\psi}:G_{L}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{\times}
be a crystalline lift of
\psi
with
\sigma_{1}
(resp.
\sigma_{2}
)-labelled Hodge–Tate weight
0
(resp.
1
). Enlarging
K
if necessary so that
\widetilde{\chi}
is valued in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{\times}
, it follows from the definition of
V_{\chi}
that
\rho
is given by the reduction of a
G_{L}
-stable lattice
T
in a crystalline representation
\rho:G_{L}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(K)
with
\sigma_{1}
(resp.
\sigma_{2}
)-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(1,0)
(resp.
(n,0)
).
It follows (from cases already covered by
[
GLS15
]
and
[
Wan22
]
) that the reduction
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}
of the Kisin module
{\mathfrak{M}}
associated to
T
is as in the conclusion of
Theorem
7.2.3
with
\{s,t\}=\{n,1\}
or
\{n+1,0\}
.

First suppose that
\{s,t\}=\{n+1,0\}
, in which case we may assume that
s=n+1
and
t=0
(as otherwise the extension of Kisin modules splits and we can exchange
s
and
t
). This implies that
\rho
has a subrepresentation whose restriction to
I_{L}
is
\omega^{n+1}
, which is only possible if
n=p-1
and either
\rho
is decomposable or
p=2
. In the first case, the conclusion of the proposition is clear, so suppose that
p=2
. In that case we obtain a contradiction using Kisin’s classification of connected
p
-divisible groups and finite flat group schemes in
[
Kis09
]
. More precisely, using the notation and terminology of
[
Kis09
, §1]
, we have that
T
is the Tate module of a bi-connected
p
-divisible group, and therefore
{\mathfrak{M}}
is formal and
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}
is connected, contradicting that

\psi(\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}})=\varphi^{*}(\overline{{\mathfrak{P}}})
.

We may therefore assume that
\{s,t\}=\{n,1\}
. Furthermore if
s=1
and
t=n>1
, then the extension of Kisin modules splits, so we can exchange
s
and
t
. Finally applying
[
GLS15
, Lemma 6.1.2]
(as extended to
p=2
in
[
Wan22
]
) yields the desired conclusion.

To prove the converse, let
V_{\chi}^{\prime}
denote the set of extension classes arising from
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-modules as in the statement of the proposition. We have thus already shown that
V_{\chi,E}\subset V_{\chi}^{\prime}
(where
V_{\chi,E}=V_{\chi}\cap H^{1}(G_{L},E(\chi))
), so it suffices to prove that
\#V_{\chi}^{\prime}\leq\#V_{\chi,E}
. Note that
a
and
b
are determined by
\psi
and
\chi
, and
[
GLS15
, Lemma 6.1.3]
(again extended to
p=2
by
[
Wan22
]
) implies that each extension
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}
as above is the underlying Kisin module for at most one
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module as in the statement of the proposition. Therefore it suffices to note that the number of equivalence classes of extensions of
{\mathfrak{M}}(n;a)
by
{\mathfrak{M}}(1;b)
as in the statement of Theorem
7.2.3
is
\#E
, unless
n=1
and
a=b
, in which case it is
\#E^{2}
; in either case, this coincides with
\#V_{\chi,E}
.
∎

We need to introduce some more notation to handle the case where
\rho
is irreducible, let
M
denote the unramified quadratic extension of
L
, denote the embeddings of its residue field into
\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}
by
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
, and the corresponding characters
I_{L}=I_{M}\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{\times}
by
\omega_{\tau}
and
\omega_{\tau^{\prime}}
, so that
\omega_{\tau^{\prime}}=\omega_{\tau}^{p}
and
\omega=\omega_{\tau}\omega_{\tau^{\prime}}=\omega_{\tau}^{p+1}
. (Note that
\omega_{\tau}
has order
p^{2}-1
,
\omega
has order
p-1
and
\omega^{2}
is the cyclotomic character.)

Proposition 7.3.2
.

Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
are as above, i.e.,
k_{1}=1
and
k_{2}=w
for some
2\leq w\leq p+1
, and let
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
. A representation
\rho:G_{L}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
has a crystalline lift of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
if and only if

\rho\sim\psi\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\chi&c\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

for some characters
\psi
and
\chi
such that
\psi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{-1-w-m}
,
\chi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{w-1}
and
c\in V_{\chi}
, or

\rho\sim{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{G_{M}}^{G_{L}}\xi

for some character
\xi:G_{M}\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{\times}
such that

\xi|_{I_{M}}=\omega^{-1-w-m}\omega_{\tau}^{w-1}
.

Proof.

Firstly, by part (2) of Proposition
6.4.2
(for any
{\mathfrak{p}}
and
F
such that
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
is isomorphic to
L
), we may replace
\rho
by
\mu\otimes\rho
for any character
\mu:G_{L}\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{\times}
such that
\mu|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{m+w+1}
so as to assume that
\vec{m}=-\vec{k}
. In this case, the assertion characterizes
\rho
with quado-Barsotti–Tate lifts with
\sigma_{j}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(r_{j},0)
, where
r_{1}=0
and
r_{2}=w-1
.

Suppose first that
\rho
is reducible and that it has such a lift
\widetilde{\rho}:G_{L}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(K)
for some sufficiently large finite extension
K
of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
. By Theorem
7.2.2
, we have that

\rho|_{I_{L}}\sim\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\omega^{w-1}&*\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\quad\mbox{or}\quad\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&*\\
0&\omega^{w-1}\end{array}\right),

according to whether or not
\sigma_{2}\in J
.
Furthermore, in the notation of Theorem
7.2.3
(suppressing the subscript
i=0
), we have
\delta=1
,
\varepsilon=0
,
r=w-1
and
\{s,t\}=\{w-1,0\}
. If
s=0
and
t=w-1
, then the extension of Kisin modules splits, so we may assume
s=w-1
and
t=0
. We thus have an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{M}}(0;b)\longrightarrow\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{M}}(w-1;a)\longrightarrow 0

for some
a,b\in E^{\times}
, extending to one of typical
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-modules with
E
-action.
In particular, we have

\rho\sim\psi\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\chi&c\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

for some characters
\psi
and
\chi
such that
\psi
is unramified and
\chi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{w-1}
, and it remains to show that
c
represents a class in
V_{\chi}
.

Consider the
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}}
obtained by tensoring
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}}
with
\widehat{{\mathfrak{M}}}(1;1)
; i.e., its underlying Kisin module is
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}=\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}\otimes_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{E}}{\mathfrak{M}}(1;1)
, and the
\widehat{G}
-action is obtained from those on the tensor product via the canonical isomorphism

\widehat{\mathcal{R}}\otimes_{\varphi,{\mathfrak{S}}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}\otimes_{{\mathfrak{S}}_{E}}{\mathfrak{M}}(1;1))\cong(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}\otimes_{\varphi,{\mathfrak{S}}}\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}})\otimes_{\widehat{\mathcal{R}}_{E}}(\widehat{\mathcal{R}}\otimes_{\varphi,{\mathfrak{S}}}{\mathfrak{M}}(1;1)).

Thus
\widehat{T}(\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}})\sim(\psi^{\prime}\otimes\rho)
for some character
\psi^{\prime}:G_{L}\to E^{\times}
such that
\psi^{\prime}|_{I_{L}}=\omega
, and we have an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\widehat{{\mathfrak{M}}}(1;b)\longrightarrow\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}}\longrightarrow\widehat{{\mathfrak{M}}}(w;a)\longrightarrow 0

of typical
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-modules with
E
-action.
In particular, if
w<p
, then
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}}
is as in the statement of Proposition
7.3.1
(with
n=w
), so
c
represents a class in
V_{\chi}
.

Suppose now that
w=p
, so that
n=1
in the definition of
V_{\chi}
. In this case, we will define an injective morphism
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}}\to\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}}
for some
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}}
as in the statement of Proposition
7.3.1
. Note that Theorem
7.2.3
provides a basis
(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
for
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}
such that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varphi(e^{\prime})&=&bue^{\prime}\\
\varphi(f^{\prime})&=&au^{p}f^{\prime}+cue^{\prime}\,(+\,du^{p+1}e^{\prime}),\end{array}

for some
c
(and
d
) in
E
, where the term in parentheses occurs only if
a=b
. We then define
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}
to be the Kisin module with basis
(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})
and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varphi(e^{\prime\prime})&=&bue^{\prime\prime}\\
\varphi(f^{\prime\prime})&=&auf^{\prime\prime}+ab^{-1}ce^{\prime\prime}\,(+\,due^{\prime\prime}).\end{array}

The map
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}\to\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}
defined by
e^{\prime}\mapsto e^{\prime\prime}
and
f^{\prime}\mapsto uf^{\prime\prime}+b^{-1}ce^{\prime\prime}
is then compatible with
\varphi
, hence defines the desired injective morphism of Kisin modules. Letting
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}}
denote the unique extension to a typical
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module as in the statement of Proposition
7.3.1
, it is straightforward to check (as in the proof of
[
GLS15
, Prop. 6.1.7]
) that
\widehat{\mathcal{R}}\otimes_{\varphi,{\mathfrak{S}}}\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}
is stable under the action of
\tau
. It follows that the map
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}\to\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}
extends to a morphism of typical
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-modules with
E
-action, which is necessarily of the form
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}}\to\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}}
by
[
GLS15
, Lemma 6.1.3]
. This implies that
\widehat{T}(\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime}})\cong\widehat{T}(\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}})
, and hence that
c
represents a class in
V_{\chi}
.

Finally suppose that
w=p+1
. In this case, we define an injective morphism
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}}\to\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}}
for some typical
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}}
as in the description of
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}}
, but with
w=2
instead of
w=p+1
.
Indeed by Theorem
7.2.3
, we have a basis
(e,f)
for
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}
such that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varphi(e)&=&be\\
\varphi(f)&=&au^{p}f+ce\,(+\,du^{4}e),\end{array}

for some
c
(and
d
) in
E
, where the term in parentheses occurs only if
a=b
and
p=2
. We then define
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}
to be the Kisin module with basis
(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})
and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varphi(e^{\prime\prime})&=&be^{\prime\prime}\\
\varphi(f^{\prime\prime})&=&auf^{\prime\prime}+ab^{-1}ce^{\prime\prime}\,(+\,du^{2}e^{\prime\prime}).\end{array}

The map
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}\to\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}
defined by
e\mapsto e^{\prime\prime}
and
f\mapsto uf^{\prime\prime}+b^{-1}ce^{\prime\prime}
is then compatible with
\varphi
, hence defines the desired injective morphism of Kisin modules. Arguing exactly as in the case
w=p
, we find that the morphism extends to one of typical
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-modules with
E
-action, and hence that
\widehat{T}(\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}})\cong\widehat{T}(\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}^{\prime\prime}})
. It now follows from the case
w=2
(already treated above) that
c
represents a class in
V_{\chi}
.

Conversely, suppose that

\rho\sim\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\chi&c\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

with
\psi
unramified,
\chi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{w-1}
and
c
representing a class in
V_{\chi}
. Choosing an unramified lift
\widetilde{\psi}
of
\psi
and a crystalline lift
\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}
of
\chi
with
\sigma_{i}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weight
r_{i}
, we again have that
H^{1}_{f}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}))
is one-dimensional. Letting
V_{\chi}^{\prime\prime}
denote the image of
H^{1}_{f}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}))
in
H^{1}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}(\chi))
, we find that
\dim_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}}(V_{\chi}^{\prime\prime})=\dim_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}}(V_{\chi})
. Since the classes in
V_{\chi}^{\prime\prime}
correspond to reductions of lattices in crystalline representations of the form

\widetilde{\psi}\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}&c\\
0&1\end{array}\right),

we have already proved that
V_{\chi}^{\prime\prime}\subset V_{\chi}
, and it follows that
V_{\chi}=V^{\prime\prime}_{\chi}
, and hence that
\rho
has a (reducible) crystalline lift with the prescribed labelled Hodge–Tate weights.

Suppose now that
\rho
is irreducible, and therefore that
\rho\sim{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{G_{M}}^{G_{L}}\xi
for some character
\xi:G_{M}\to E^{\times}
. Write
\Sigma_{M}=\{\,\sigma_{0,1},\sigma_{0,2},\sigma_{1,1},\sigma_{1,2}\,\}
where
\sigma_{i,j}
is the embedding whose restriction to
L
is
\sigma_{j}
and whose reduction is
\tau
(resp.
\tau^{\prime}
) if
i=0
(resp.
1
).

If
\rho
has a crystalline lift
\widetilde{\rho}:G_{L}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p})
with
\sigma_{j}
labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(r_{j},0)
, then
\widetilde{\rho}|_{G_{M}}
is a crystalline lift of
\rho|_{G_{M}}
with
\sigma_{i,j}
labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(r_{j},0)
. Since
\rho|_{G_{M}}
is decomposable, Theorem
7.2.2
implies that

\rho|_{I_{M}}\sim\omega^{w-1}\oplus 1\quad\mbox{or }\quad\omega_{\tau}^{w-1}\oplus\omega_{\tau^{\prime}}^{w-1}.

The first possibility contradicts the irreducibility of
\rho
, and the second implies that
\xi|_{I_{M}}=\omega_{\tau}^{w-1}
(after replacing
\xi
with its conjugate if necessary).

Conversely, if
\xi|_{I_{M}}=\omega_{\tau}^{w-1}
, then we may choose a crystalline lift
\widetilde{\xi}:G_{M}\to\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
whose
\sigma_{i,j}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weight is
w-1
if
(i,j)=(0,2)
and
0
otherwise. Then
{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{G_{M}}^{G_{L}}\widetilde{\xi}
is a crystalline lift of
\rho
with
\sigma_{1}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(0,0)
and
\sigma_{2}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(w-1,0)
, as required.
∎

Remark 7.3.3
.

In view of
[
GLS15
, Lemma 5.4.2]
, the arguments involving
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-modules are only needed in the case that
\chi
is the cyclotomic character, which implies that either
w=3
or
w=p=2
. Thus it is only for
p\leq 3
that there is any overlap between these cases and those for which we need the additional arguments in the proof of the proposition comparing extensions (i.e.,
w\geq p
).

Remark 7.3.4
.

It follows from the proposition that
\rho
has a crystalline lift of weight
((1,2),\vec{m})
if and only if it has one of weight
((1,p+1),\vec{m})
(or indeed of weight
((2,1),\vec{m})
). Furthermore it follows from Theorem
4.1.1
that the same equivalence holds with “has a crystalline lift” replaced by “is geometrically modular.” This is further indication that the equivalence in Conjecture
6.3.2
extends to weights slightly outside
\Xi_{\min}^{+}
.

Remark 7.3.5
.

Taking
w=1
in the statement of Proposition
7.3.2
, the corresponding condition on
\rho
is that it be unramified.

We remark also that in the case
w=p
of the proposition (in which case
\chi
is unramified), we could have taken
n=p
instead of
n=1
in the definition of
V_{\chi}
, provided
p>2
. That this gives the same space of extensions follows for example from the main result of
[
BS22
]
(see the proof below of Theorem
7.4.2
).

We need one further observation in another special case. Recall that if
\chi=\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}
is the cyclotomic character, then
\dim_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}}H^{1}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}(\chi))=3
, unless
\chi
is also trivial, in which case
\dim_{\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}}H^{1}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}(\chi))=4
. (Note the latter occurs only if
p=2
, or if
p=3
and
L={\mathbb{Q}}_{3}(\zeta_{3})
.) In this case, letting
\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}:G_{L}\to\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}^{\times}
be the cyclotomic character (or indeed any character whose restriction to inertia is cyclotomic), we have that the image of
H^{1}_{f}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{Z}}}_{p}(\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime})
in
H^{1}(G_{L},\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}(\chi))
has codimension one (consisting of what are commonly called the
peu ramifiées
classes).
Let us denote this subspace by
V^{\mathrm{ty}}_{\chi}
.

Proposition 7.3.6
.

If
\chi
is the cyclotomic character, then
V_{\chi}\subset V^{\mathrm{ty}}_{\chi}
.

Proof.

If
c
represents a class in
V_{\chi}
, then the proof of Proposition
7.3.2
shows that the representation
\rho:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\chi&c\\
0&1\end{array}\right)
arises from a typical
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}}
as in the statement of Theorem
7.2.3
24
24

24

In particular, with
E
-action for some sufficiently large
E
.
with
s=r=2
,

\delta=1
and
t=\varepsilon=x=0
.
In particular, it follows immediately that
\rho
arises from
such a
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module
\widehat{\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}}
with
s=2
,
r=x=\varepsilon=1
and
t=\delta=0
.

We claim that this is equivalent to
c
representing a class in
V_{\chi}^{\mathrm{ty}}
. Indeed if
c
represents a class in
V_{\chi}^{\mathrm{ty}}
, then Theorem
7.2.3
and
[
GLS15
, Lemma 6.1.2]
imply that
\rho
arises from such a
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module if
p>2
. If
p=2
, then Wang’s extension of these results (
[
Wan22
]
) implies that either

\rho
arises from such a
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module, or from one for which
\overline{{\mathfrak{M}}}
is as in the statement of Theorem
7.2.3
with
s=t=r=\varepsilon=1
and

x=\delta=0
. In the latter case, Proposition
7.3.1
implies that
c\in V_{\chi}
, and hence we have already shown in the preceding paragraph that
\rho
arises from a
(\varphi,\widehat{G})
-module of the desired form. This proves one direction of the claimed equivalence, and the other follows from a counting argument as in the last paragraph of the proof of Proposition
7.3.1
.
∎

7.4.
The ramified quadratic case: Partial weight one modularity

We specialize again to the case where
F
is a real quadratic field in which
p
ramifies. We will now prove cases of Conjecture
6.3.2
involving partial weight one, i.e., where
\vec{k}=(1,w)
for some
2\leq w\leq p
.

We first assume
w\geq 3
, since the argument for
w=2
will require additional ingredients and hypotheses. We remark though that a particular case of the following theorem requires the result for
w=2
as input (in the form of Corollary
7.4.8
).

To begin with, in the modularity direction we have the following:

Theorem 7.4.1
.

Let
F
be a real quadratic field in which
p
ramified, and let
{\mathfrak{p}}
be the prime of
F
over
p
.
Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, with
\vec{k}=(1,w)
for some
w\in[3,p]
. Let
\rho:G_{F}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
be irreducible and geometrically modular of some weight. Suppose further that
\rho|_{G_{F(\zeta_{p})}}
is irreducible, and if
p=5
, then the projective image of
\rho|_{G_{F(\zeta_{5})}}
is not isomorphic to
A_{5}
. If
\rho|_{G_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}
has a crystalline lift of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
, then
\rho
is geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
.

Proof.

Let
L
denote
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
,
M
its unramified quadratic extension, and write
\Sigma_{M_{0}}=\{\tau,\tau^{\prime}\}
and
\Sigma_{M}=\{\,\sigma_{0,1},\sigma_{0,2},\sigma_{1,1},\sigma_{1,2},\}
as in the proof of Proposition
7.3.2
. By that proposition, if
\rho|_{G_{L}}
has a crystalline lift of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
, then

\rho|_{G_{L}}\sim\psi\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\chi&c\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

for some characters
\psi
and
\chi
such that
\psi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{-1-w-m}
,
\chi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{w-1}
and
c\in V_{\chi}
, or

\rho\sim{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{G_{M}}^{G_{L}}\xi

for some character
\xi:G_{M}\to\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{\times}
such that

\xi|_{I_{M}}=\omega^{-1-w-m}\omega_{\tau}^{w-1}
.

We claim that in either case,
\rho|_{G_{L}}
has crystalline lifts of weight
(\vec{k}^{\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime})
and of weight
(\vec{k}^{\prime\prime},\vec{m})
(in the sense of Definition
6.2.2
), where
\vec{k}^{\prime}=(2,w+1)
,
\vec{k}^{\prime\prime}=(2,w-1)
and
\vec{m}^{\prime}=\vec{m}-(0,1)
.

Indeed in the first case, it follows from the definition of
V_{\chi}
that
\rho|_{G_{L}}
has a crystalline lift of the form

\widetilde{\psi}\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{\chi}&*\\
0&1\end{array}\right)
, where
\widetilde{\chi}
has

\sigma_{1}
(resp.
\sigma_{2}
)-labelled Hodge–Tate
-1
(resp.
w
) and
\widetilde{\psi}
has

\sigma_{1}
(resp.
\sigma_{2}
)-labelled Hodge–Tate
-1-m_{1}
(resp.
-w-m_{2}
). Therefore
\widetilde{\rho}|_{G_{L}}
has
\sigma_{1}
(resp.
\sigma_{2}
)-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(-1-m_{1},-2-m_{1})
(resp.
(-m_{2},-w-m_{2})
), i.e.,
\sigma_{i}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(-1-m_{i}^{\prime},-k_{i}^{\prime}-m_{i}^{\prime})
.
Similarly, we see that
\rho|_{G_{L}}
has a crystalline lift of the form

\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime}\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}&*\\
0&1\end{array}\right)
(using also Proposition
7.3.6
if
\chi
is cyclotomic), where
\widetilde{\chi}^{\prime}
is a crystalline lift of
\chi
with
\sigma_{1}
(resp.
\sigma_{2}
)-labelled Hodge–Tate weight
1
(resp.
w-2
)
and
\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime}
is a crystalline lift of
\psi
with
\sigma_{1}
(resp.
\sigma_{2}
)-labelled Hodge–Tate weight
-m_{1}-2
(resp.
1-w-m_{2}
). Therefore
\rho|_{G_{L}}
has a crystalline lift with
\sigma_{i}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(-1-m_{i},-k_{i}^{\prime\prime}-m_{i})
.

In the second case, we may choose a crystalline lift
\widetilde{\xi}
of
\xi
with
\sigma_{0,1}
(resp.
\sigma_{0,2}
,
\sigma_{1,1}
,
\sigma_{1,2}
)-labelled Hodge–Tate weight
-2-m_{1}
(resp.
-m_{2}
,
-1-m_{1}
,
-w-m_{2}
), so that
{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{G_{M}}^{G_{L}}
is a crystalline lift of
\rho|_{G_{L}}
of weight
(\vec{k}^{\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime})
. Similarly choosing
\widetilde{\xi}^{\prime}
with
\sigma_{i,j}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
-1-m_{1}
(resp.
-1-m_{2}
,
-2-m_{1}
,
1-w-m_{2}
) yields a crystalline of lift of weight
(\vec{k}^{\prime\prime},\vec{m})
.

We may now apply Theorem
6.5.9
to conclude that
\rho
is algebraically modular of weights
(\vec{k}^{\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime})
and
(\vec{k}^{\prime\prime},\vec{m})
. Applying Theorem
6.5.5
, it follows that
\rho
is geometrically modular of those weights.

By Proposition
7.1.5
and Lemma
7.1.7
, we then have that
\rho\sim\rho_{f^{\prime}}\sim\rho_{f^{\prime\prime}}
for some stabilized eigenforms
f^{\prime}\in S_{\vec{k}^{\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
and
f^{\prime\prime}\in S_{\vec{k}^{\prime\prime},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
for some sufficiently small level
{\mathfrak{n}}
prime to
p
and sufficiently large finite subfield
E
of
\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}
.

We claim furthermore that we may take
f^{\prime}
to be strongly stabilized. Indeed if
f^{\prime}
is not strongly stabilized, then applying Theorem
5.3.1
to
f^{\prime}_{\xi}
, where
\xi
is as in Definition
7.1.4
, implies that
\rho_{f^{\prime}}|_{I_{L}}\sim\omega^{-2-w-m}\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\omega^{w+1}&*\\
0&1\end{array}\right)
. This contradicts the explicit form above for
\rho|_{G_{L}}
, unless
w=p-1
and
\rho|_{I_{L}}\sim\omega^{-1-m}\oplus\omega^{-2-m}
, in which case the claim follows from Corollary
7.4.8
(and a further shrinking of
{\mathfrak{n}}
using Lemma
7.1.7
if necessary).

Now consider the forms
25
25

25

We will systematically write
H_{i}
for
H_{\sigma_{i}}
(and similarly
G_{i}
for
G_{\sigma_{i}}
), and omit the subscript for the unique
\Theta
.

H_{2}f^{\prime}
and
\Theta f^{\prime\prime}\in S_{\vec{k}^{\prime\prime\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{n}},E)
, where
\vec{k}^{\prime\prime\prime}=(3,w)
. Since both are strongly stabilized and give rise to
\rho
, Lemma
7.1.9
implies they are the same, and hence
H_{2}|\Theta f^{\prime\prime}
. Since
p\nmid(w-1)
, it follows from
[
Dia23
, Thm. A]
that
f^{\prime\prime}=H_{2}f
for some
f\in S_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}),E)
, as desired.
∎

For the argument in the other direction, we will need to assume part (2) of Conjecture
6.5.2
. On the other hand, we can remove the Taylor–Wiles hypothesis from the “easier” implications in Theorem
6.5.9
in our setting. In particular, we have the following:

Theorem 7.4.2
.

Suppose that
(\vec{k},\vec{m})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}_{\geq 2}\times\Sigma
, with

k_{1}=2
and
2\leq k_{2}\leq p+1
.
If
\rho
is definitely or algebraically modular of weight

(\vec{k},\vec{m})
, then
\rho|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
has a crystalline lift of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.5.5
, we may assume
26
26

26

Alternatively, a similar (but slightly simpler) argument than we are about to give applies in the case of definite modularity.
that
\rho
is algebraically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
.
Let
B
,
U
and
Q
be as in Definition
6.5.1
(with
B
ramified at exactly one archimedean place), so that
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\rho}^{Q}
is in the support of
M_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}^{B}(U,\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})=H^{1}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{D}_{\vec{k},\vec{m},\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}})
.

Suppose that
\theta:\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})\to{\operatorname{Aut}\,}_{K}(V)

(for some sufficiently large
K
)
is an absolutely irreducible representation that has
D_{\vec{k},\vec{m},\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}}
as a Jordan–Hölder factor in its reduction, i.e., in that of any
\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})
-stable lattice
T
in
V
.
Viewing
\theta
as a representation of
U_{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}})
via inflation, let
\mathcal{T}
be the locally constant sheaf associated to
T
as in §
3.7
. (Note that we may assume
U^{p}
is sufficiently small that this is defined.)
The same argument as in the proof of Lemma
6.5.3
then shows that
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\rho}^{Q}
is in the support of
H^{1}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{T}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}E)
, and hence in that of
H^{1}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{T})
. Similarly, it follows that
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\rho}^{Q}
is in the support of its maximal torsion-free quotient
H^{1}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{T})_{\mathrm{tf}}
, and hence in that of

H^{1}(Y_{U}^{B},\mathcal{T})\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}K\cong(H^{1}(Y_{U^{\prime}}^{B},K)\otimes_{K}V)^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})},

where
U^{\prime}=U^{p}U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\prime}
and
U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\prime}
is the kernel of the projection
U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
. Therefore there is a
\mathbb{T}^{Q}
-eigenform

f\in(M^{B}_{\vec{2}}(U^{\prime},\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{p})\otimes_{K}V)^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})}

such that
T_{v}f=\widetilde{a}_{v}f
and
S_{v}f=\widetilde{d}_{v}
for some lifts
\widetilde{a}_{v}
of
{\operatorname{tr}\,}(\rho({\operatorname{Frob}}_{v}))
and

\widetilde{d}_{v}
of
\mathrm{Nm}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(v)\det(\rho({\operatorname{Frob}}_{v}))
for all
v\not\in Q
, and hence a cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{2}}^{B}
(and embedding
K\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
) such that

(\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{K}V)^{U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\neq 0

and
T_{v}=\widetilde{a}_{v}
and
S_{v}=\widetilde{d}_{v}
on
\pi_{v}^{{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}^{\times}}
for all
v\not\in Q
.

Let us again adopt notation from the proof of Proposition
7.3.2
, so
L=F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
,
M
is its unramified quadratic extension, and
\omega
,
\omega_{\tau}
and
\omega_{\tau^{\prime}}
are fundamental characters.

Applying Theorems
2.3.1
and
5.1.1
, we now have
\rho\sim\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
for some
\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{2}}
with the property that
\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{L}}
is potentially semistable with
\sigma_{i}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(0,-1)
for
i=1,2
, and
\mathrm{WD}(\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{L}})^{\mathrm{F-ss}}
corresponds under local Langlands to a representation

\pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
such that
(\pi_{\mathfrak{p}}\otimes_{K}V)^{\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathcal{O}}_{L})}\neq 0
.
In particular, if
\theta\sim[\det]^{n}
(resp.
[\det]^{n}\otimes\mathrm{St}
), where
[\cdot]
denotes the Teichmüller lift (and
\mathrm{St}
the Steinberg representation), then

\psi\otimes\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{L}}
is crystalline (resp. semistable) with

\sigma_{i}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(1,0)
(for
i=1,2
), where
\psi
is any character whose restriction to inertia is

\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}[\omega]^{n}
. Otherwise, we have that

\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}\otimes\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{L}}
is potentially Barsotti–Tate with inertial type corresponding under local Langlands to the dual of
\theta
.

To complete the proof, suppose first that
k_{2}=2
. In that case, we may apply the conclusion above with
\theta\sim[\det]^{m+2}
, where
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
. Since
\overline{\psi}\otimes\rho
has a crystalline lift with
\sigma_{i}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(1,0)
, and
\overline{\psi}|_{I_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}=\omega^{m+4}
, it follows that
\rho
has a crystalline lift with
\sigma_{i}
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(-1-m_{i},-2-m_{i})
, as required. For consistency with the discussion below, note that this means
D_{\vec{2},-\vec{3}-\vec{m}}=\det^{-m-4}\in W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
, where
W^{\mathrm{cris}}
is as in
[
GLS15
, Def. 4.1.3]
. In general, the task at hand is to prove that

D_{\vec{k},-\vec{1}-\vec{k}-\vec{m}}=\det\nolimits^{-m-k_{2}-2}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{2}-2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\in W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}}).

Suppose next that
k_{2}=p+1
. Taking
\theta\sim[\det]^{m+2}\otimes\mathrm{St}
and letting
n=-m-4
, it follows that either
\det^{n}\in W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
or that

\rho|_{G_{L}}
has the form
\psi\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\overline{\chi}_{\mathrm{cyc}}}&{*}\\
{0}&{1}\end{array}\right)
with

{\psi}|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{n}
.
In either case, we deduce that that
\det^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\in W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
from the equality

W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})=W^{\mathrm{explicit}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
(
[
GLS15
, Thm. 6.1.8]
and
[
Wan22
, Thm. 5.4]
)
and the description of
W^{\mathrm{explicit}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
in
[
BS22
]
. Indeed this is already immediate from the definition of
W^{\mathrm{explicit}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
in the second case, so suppose that
\det^{n}\in W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
. The only irreducible representations with
\det^{n}
as a Serre weight have the form
{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{G_{M}}^{G_{L}}\xi
, where
\xi|_{I_{M}}=\omega_{\tau}^{2}
, and one then finds that
\det^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}
is also a Serre weight. Reducible representations with
\det^{n}
as a Serre weight have the form
\psi\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\chi}&{c}\\
{0}&{1}\end{array}\right)
, where either
\psi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{n}
and
\chi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{2}
(with
c
representating a class in
V_{\chi}^{\mathrm{ty}}
if
\chi=\overline{\chi}_{\mathrm{cyc}}
), or
\psi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{n+1}
,
\chi
unramified and
c
representing a class in
V_{\chi}
. In the first case, or if
p=2
, we again have
\det^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\in W^{\mathrm{explicit}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
by definition, so suppose

p>2
and we are in the second case. By
[
BS22
, Thm. 5.6]
, the space
V_{\chi}
is spanned by the subspaces

\Psi_{\det^{n},J,x}
for
(J,x)\in\{(\Sigma_{0},0),(\emptyset,1)\}

defined in
[
BS22
, Def. 5.2]
. Replacing
\det^{n}
by
\det^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1}
in
[
BS22
, Def. 5.2]
gives the same two possible values for
(J,x)
, but interchanges the corresponding subspaces (the exponents of
v
in their definition still being
0
and
1-p
), so another application of
[
BS22
, Thm. 5.6]
implies that
\det^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\in W^{\mathrm{explicit}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
.

Now suppose that
3\leq k_{2}\leq p
. We have two choices for
\theta
: one of dimension
p+1
(induced from a Borel), and one of dimension
p-1
(cuspidal). We denote these
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
, and let
\theta_{i}^{*}
denote the dual of
\theta_{i}
for
i=1,2
. It follows from the existence of the lift
\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}\otimes\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{L}}
and the definition of the set
W^{\mathrm{BT}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
(see
[
GK19
, Thm. A]
) and its equality with
W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})

(
[
GLS15
, Thm. 6.1.8]
) that

W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\overline{\chi}_{\mathrm{cyc}}\otimes\rho|_{G_{L}})\cap\mathrm{JH}(\overline{\theta}^{*}_{i})\neq\emptyset

(where
\mathrm{JH}(\overline{\vartheta})
denotes the set of Jordan–Hölder factors of the reduction of any stable lattice for
\vartheta
). Since

\theta_{i}
has central character
[\det]^{2m+k_{2}+2}
, we have
\theta_{i}^{*}\sim[\det]^{n-m}\otimes\theta_{i}
where
n=-m-k_{2}-2
, and the above intersection is equivalent to

W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})\cap\mathrm{JH}([\det]^{n-m-2}\overline{\theta}_{i})\neq\emptyset.

Since
\overline{\theta}_{i}
has
D_{\vec{k},\vec{m}}=\det^{m+2}\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{2}-2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}
as a Jordan–Hölder factor, we have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrm{JH}([\det]^{n-m-2}\overline{\theta}_{1})&=&\{\,\det\nolimits^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{2}-2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2},\det\nolimits^{n+k_{2}-2}\operatorname{Sym}^{p+1-k_{2}}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\,\}\\
\mbox{and}\quad\mathrm{JH}([\det]^{n-m-2}\overline{\theta}_{2})&=&\{\,\det\nolimits^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{2}-2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2},\det\nolimits^{n+k_{2}-1}\operatorname{Sym}^{p-1-k_{2}}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\,\},\end{array}

with the last factor absent if
k_{2}=p
. In particular, if
k_{2}=p
, it follows immediately that
\det^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{p-2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\in W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
.

Suppose now that
3\leq k_{2}\leq p-1
and

\det^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{2}-2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\not\in W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
. We must then have

\,\{\det\nolimits^{a}\operatorname{Sym}^{b}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2},\det\nolimits^{a-1}\operatorname{Sym}^{b+2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\,\}\subset W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}}),

where
a=n+k_{2}-1
and
0\leq b=p-1-k_{2}\leq p-4
.
We will again appeal to the explicit description of the set of Serre weights for the local Galois representation. Firstly, if
\rho|_{G_{L}}
is irreducible, then the assumption that
\det\nolimits^{a}\operatorname{Sym}^{b}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\in W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
implies
\rho|_{G_{L}}
is of the form
{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{G_{M}}^{G_{L}}\xi
where

\xi|_{I_{M}}=\omega^{a}\omega_{\tau}^{b+2}
or
\omega^{a+1}\omega_{\tau}^{b}
. Similarly since
\det\nolimits^{a-1}\operatorname{Sym}^{b+2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\in W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
, we have that
\rho|_{G_{L}}
is of the form
{\operatorname{Ind}\,}_{G_{M}}^{G_{L}}\xi^{\prime}
where

\xi^{\prime}|_{I_{M}}=\omega^{a-1}\omega_{\tau}^{b+4}
or
\omega^{a}\omega_{\tau}^{b+2}
. This is only possible if

\xi|_{I_{M}}=\omega^{a}\omega_{\tau}^{b+2}=\omega^{a+b+1}\omega_{\tau^{\prime}}^{p-b-1}=\omega^{n}\omega_{\tau^{\prime}}^{k_{2}},

which contradicts the assumption that
\det^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{2}-2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\not\in W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
.
Similarly, if
\rho|_{G_{L}}
is reducible, then an analysis of the possibilities shows that it must have the form
\psi\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\chi}&{c}\\
{0}&{1}\end{array}\right)
with
\psi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{a}
,
\chi|_{I_{L}}=\omega^{b+2}
and
c
representing a class in
V_{\chi}
. Appealing again to
[
BS22
, Thm. 5.6]
, we find that
V_{\chi}
is the subspace
\Psi_{\sigma,J,x}
in
[
BS22
, Def. 5.2]
with
\sigma=\det^{a-1}\operatorname{Sym}^{b+2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}
,
J=\Sigma_{0}
and
x=0
, giving
-(b+2)
as the exponent of
v
. Furthermore taking
\det^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{2}-2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}
,
J=\emptyset
and
x=1
gives the same subspace, contradicting assumption that
\det^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{k_{2}-2}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\not\in W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{L}})
.
∎

Theorem 7.4.3
.

Let
F
be a real quadratic field in which
p
is ramified, and let
{\mathfrak{p}}
be the prime of
F
over
p
.
Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, with
\vec{k}=(1,w)
for some
w\in[3,p]
.
Suppose further that Conjecture
6.5.2
(2) holds.
If
\rho
is irreducible and geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
, then
\rho|_{G_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}
has a crystalline lift of weight

(\vec{k},\vec{m})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\rho
is geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
. Then by Proposition
6.4.4
, it is
also geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k}^{\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime})
, where
\vec{k}^{\prime}=(2,w+1)
and
\vec{m}^{\prime}=\vec{m}-(0,1)
as in the proof of Theorem
7.4.1
. Furthermore
\rho
is also geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k}^{\prime\prime},\vec{m})
, where
\vec{k}^{\prime\prime}=\vec{k}+\vec{h}_{2}=(2,w-1)
.

Under our assumption of Conjecture
6.5.2
(2), it follows that
\rho
is also algebraically modular of weights
(\vec{k}^{\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime})
and
(\vec{k}^{\prime\prime},\vec{m})
. Therefore by Theorem
7.4.2
,
\rho|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
has crystalline lifts of those weights, and hence

\{\,\det\nolimits^{-m-w-1}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{w-3}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2},\det\nolimits^{-m-w-2}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{w-1}\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p}^{2}\,\}\subset W^{\mathrm{cris}}(\rho|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}).

The same analysis as in the last paragraph of the proof of Theorem
7.4.2
, but with
a=-m-w-1
and

b=w-3
then shows that
\rho|_{G_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}
is of the form described in Proposition
7.3.2
, unless
w=p
and

\rho|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}\sim\psi\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\chi&c\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

for some characters
\psi
and
\chi
such that

\psi|_{I_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}=\omega^{-3-m}
and
\chi|_{I_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}=\omega^{2}
(with
c
defining a class in
V_{\chi}
, but we will not make use of this).

Suppose then that this is the case, i.e.,
\rho
has the form just described and
\rho\sim\rho_{f}
for some eigenform
f\in S_{(1,p),\vec{m}}(U,E)
, and we will derive a contradiction.
By Lemma
7.1.7
, we may furthermore assume that
U=U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}})
for some
{\mathfrak{n}}
prime to
{\mathfrak{p}}
and that
f
is stabilized, strongly so by Theorem
5.3.1
. Note that
k_{2}=p
is divisible by
p
, we have that
\Theta(f)
is divisible by
H_{2}
by Theorem
4.2.1
.
Therefore we have
\Theta(f)=H_{2}f_{1}
for some
f_{1}\in M_{(1,p+2),\vec{m}^{\prime}}(U,E)
where
\vec{m}^{\prime}=\vec{m}-(0,1)
. Furthermore it follows from Theorem
4.1.1
that
H_{1}|f_{1}
, so
\Theta(f)=H_{1}H_{2}f_{2}
for some
f_{2}\in M_{\vec{2},\vec{m}^{\prime}}(U,E)
, and note that (a multiple of)
f_{2}
is strongly stabilized.

In particular,
\rho
is geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{2},\vec{m}^{\prime})
, and our hypothesis therefore implies that
\rho
is also algebraically modular of weight
(\vec{2},\vec{m}^{\prime})
. We claim that it is in fact
{\mathfrak{p}}
-ordinarily so. To that end consider a quaternion algebra
B
and non-zero element
\varphi\in M_{\vec{2},\vec{m}^{\prime}}^{B}(U^{\prime},E)[{\mathfrak{m}}_{\rho}^{Q}]
, where
B
is ramified at exactly one infinite place (and unramified at
{\mathfrak{p}}
) and
U^{\prime}
for some
U^{\prime}
containing
{\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
. We then have that
e_{\xi}\varphi\in M_{\vec{2},-\vec{1}}^{B}(U^{\prime},E)[{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mu\otimes\rho}^{Q^{\prime}}]
for sufficiently large
Q^{\prime}
, where
\xi(u_{p})=\overline{u}_{p}^{m+1}
for all
u_{p}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}
and
\mu
corresponds to
\xi
via class field theory. We may then lift
e_{\xi}\varphi
to an eigenvector
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\xi}\in M_{\vec{2},-\vec{1}}^{B}(U^{\prime},{\mathcal{O}})
for
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
for all
v\not\in Q^{\prime}
(enlarging
{\mathcal{O}}
if necessary). We thus obtain an automorphic representation
\Pi\in\mathcal{C}^{B}_{\vec{2}}
such that
\Pi_{\mathfrak{p}}
is an unramified principal series, as are the
\Pi_{v}
for all
v\not\in Q^{\prime}
, with the same eigenvalues as
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\xi}
for
T_{v}
and
S_{v}
on
\Pi_{v}^{{\mathcal{O}}_{B,v}^{\times}}
. We thus have
\mu\otimes\rho\sim\overline{\rho}_{\pi}
, where
\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\vec{2}}
corresponds to
\Pi
under Theorem
2.3.1
, and
\Pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cong\pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is a principal series representation of the form
I(\psi_{1}|\cdot|^{1/2},\psi_{2}|\cdot|^{1/2})
for some unramified characters
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
. Furthermore
\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
is crystalline with
\sigma
-labelled Hodge–Tate weights
(1,0)
for both
\sigma\in\Sigma
, so
\chi_{\mathrm{cyc}}\otimes\rho_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
arises from a
p
-divisible group over
{\mathcal{O}}_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
with an action of
{\mathcal{O}}
. Noting that

e_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=2\leq p-1
and

\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}\otimes\overline{\rho}_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}\sim\psi^{\prime}\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\chi&c\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

where
\psi^{\prime}=\psi\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}\mu|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
is unramified and
\psi|_{I_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}=\omega^{2}
is cyclotomic, it follows that the
p
-divisible cannot be local-local, and therefore that

\chi_{{\operatorname{cyc}\,}}\otimes{\rho}_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}\sim\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime}\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\widetilde{\chi}&*\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

for some lifts
\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime}
of
\psi
and
\widetilde{\chi}
of
\chi
such that
\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime}
is unramified and
\widetilde{\psi}^{\prime}|_{I_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
is cyclotomic. Therefore
\mathrm{WD}({\rho}_{\pi}|_{G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}})\cong\widetilde{\chi}_{1}\oplus\widetilde{\chi}_{2}|\cdot|^{-1}
for a pair of unramified characters

\widetilde{\chi}_{1},\widetilde{\chi}_{2}:W^{\mathrm{ab}}_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\cong F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
. By local-global compatibility, these coincide with
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}
, from which it follows that
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
acts on
\Pi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{{\mathcal{O}}_{B,{\mathfrak{p}}}^{\times}}
, and hence on
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\xi}
, by
\psi_{1}(\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{p}}})+p\psi_{2}(\varpi_{{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{p}}})\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
. This in turn implies that
e_{\xi}\varphi
is an eigenform for
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
with non-zero eigenvalue, and hence that
\rho
is
{\mathfrak{p}}
-ordinarily algebraically modular of weight
(\vec{2},\vec{m}^{\prime})
.

By Theorem
6.6.9
, we then have that
\rho
is also
{\mathfrak{p}}
-ordinarily geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{2},\vec{m}^{\prime})
. By Proposition
7.1.5
and Lemma
7.1.7
, there exist an ideal
{\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}
prime to
{\mathfrak{p}}
and a stabilized eigenform
f_{3}\in S_{\vec{2},\vec{m}^{\prime}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}),E)
(enlarging
E
if necessary) such that
\rho\sim\rho_{f_{3}}
and
T_{{\mathfrak{p}}}f_{3,\xi}\neq 0
for all
\xi
as in Definition
6.6.3
. By another application of Lemma
7.1.7
, we may assume
{\mathfrak{n}}={\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}
.

Now consider
f_{4}=f_{3}-f_{2}\in S_{\vec{2},\vec{m}^{\prime}}(U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}}^{\prime}),E)
. Since
f_{2}
and
f_{3}
are both stabilized, we have
r_{m}^{t}(f_{4})=0
for all
t\in(\mathbb{A}_{F,\bf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
and
m\in t^{-1}\widehat{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{-1}\cap F_{+}^{\times}
such that
ctm\not\in{\mathfrak{p}}\widehat{O}_{F}
. On the other hand, since

f_{2}
is strongly stabilized, we have
f_{2}\neq f_{3}
, so
f_{4}\neq 0
. It follows that
f_{4}\in\ker(\Theta)
, so by Theorem
4.2.2
, we have

f_{4}=H_{1}^{s_{1}}H_{2}^{s_{2}}G_{1}^{t_{1}}G_{2}^{t_{2}}V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(f_{5})

for some
s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
f_{5}\in M_{\mathrm{tot}}(U,E)
(where
U=U_{1}({\mathfrak{n}})
). Furthermore, by Theorem
4.2.3
, we may assume
f_{5}\in M_{\vec{k}^{\prime\prime\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime\prime}}(U,E)
for some
\vec{k}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\Xi_{\min}^{+}
,
\vec{m}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. We thus have

(2,2)=s_{1}(-1,p)+s_{2}(1,-1)+(k^{\prime\prime\prime}_{2},pk_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime})

with
1\leq k_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq pk_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq pk_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}
. One sees easily that the first equation implies that
p=3
and
\vec{k}^{\prime\prime\prime}=\vec{1}
, so assume this is the case. Note also that

m^{\prime\prime}:=m_{1}^{\prime\prime}+m_{2}^{\prime\prime}\equiv m^{\prime}_{1}+m^{\prime}_{2}=m-1\bmod(p-1)=2.

Since
V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is injective and compatible with
T_{v}
for all
v\neq{\mathfrak{p}}
and
S_{v}
for all
v\nmid{\mathfrak{n}}{\mathfrak{p}}
, it follows that
f_{5}
is an eigenform for all these operators, with the same eigenvalues as
f_{4}
. In particular, we have
\rho_{f_{5}}\sim\rho
and
T_{v}f_{5}=0
for all
v|{\mathfrak{n}}
. The same argument as in the proof of Proposition
7.1.5
then shows that if
r^{t_{1}}_{m_{1}}(f_{5})=0
, then in fact
r_{t}^{m}=0
for all

t\in(\mathbb{A}_{F,\bf{f}}^{(p)})^{\times}
and
m\in t^{-1}\widehat{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{-1}\cap F_{+}^{\times}
such that
ctm\not\in{\mathfrak{p}}\widehat{O}_{F}
, which implies that
f_{5}\in\ker(\Theta)
. In this case, applying Theorem
4.2.2
gives a contradiction, so we conclude that
r^{t_{1}}_{m_{1}}(f_{5})\neq 0
.

Now consider
f_{6}=\Theta(f_{5})\in S_{\vec{2},\vec{m}^{\prime\prime\prime}}(U,E)
, where
\vec{m}^{\prime\prime\prime}=\vec{m}^{\prime\prime}-(0,1)
. Since
T_{v}f_{6}=0
for all
v|{\mathfrak{n}}{\mathfrak{p}}
, some multiple of
f_{6}
is a strongly stabilized eigenform. Note also that since
m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}+m_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\equiv m\bmod 2
, there exist
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(m_{1},m_{2})=(m_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime},m_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime})+u_{1}(-1,3)+u_{2}(1,-1).

Therefore some multiple of
G_{1}^{u_{1}}G_{2}^{u_{2}}f_{6}
is a strongly stabilized eigenform in
S_{\vec{2},\vec{m}}(U,E)
giving rise to
\rho
, as is
H_{2}f
. Therefore
f_{6}=\Theta(f_{5})
is divisible by
H_{2}
. Since
k_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}=1
is not divisible by
p
, Theorem
4.2.1
implies that
f_{5}
is divisible by
\mathrm{Ha}_{2}
, i.e.,
f_{5}=H_{2}f_{7}
for some
f_{7}\in S_{(0,2),\vec{m}^{\prime\prime}}(U,E)
. Finally, this contradicts Theorem
4.2.3
and completes the proof.
∎

We now turn our attention to the case of
w=2
, where our arguments require ordinariness hypotheses. We first treat the easier implication relating geometric modularity to existence of crystalline lifts:

Theorem 7.4.4
.

Let
F
be a real quadratic field in which
p
is ramified, and let
{\mathfrak{p}}
be the prime of
F
over
p
. Suppose that
\vec{k},\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
, with
\vec{k}=(1,2)
. Suppose further that Conjecture
6.5.2
(2) holds.
If
\rho
is irreducible and
{\mathfrak{p}}
-ordinarily geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
, then
\rho|_{G_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}
has a crystalline lift of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.6.5
, we have

\rho\sim\psi\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\chi&c\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

for some characters
\psi
and
\chi
such that
\psi|_{I_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}=\omega^{-m-2}
and
\chi|_{I_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}=\omega
.
Furthermore, by Proposition
6.4.4
,
\rho
is also geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k}^{\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime})
, where
\vec{k}^{\prime}=(2,3)
and
\vec{m}^{\prime}=\vec{m}-(0,1)
.
Our assumption of Conjecture
6.5.2
(2) therefore implies that
\rho
is algebraically modular of weight
(\vec{k}^{\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime})
. By Theorem
7.4.2
,
\rho
has a crystalline lift of weight
(\vec{k}^{\prime},\vec{m}^{\prime})
.
It therefore follows from
[
GLS15
, Thm. 5.4.1]
that
c
defines a class in
V_{\chi}
. Finally, Proposition
7.3.2
implies that
\rho
has a crystalline lift of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
.
∎

Remark 7.4.5
.

The ordinariness hypothesis is needed for our argument since there are irreducible representations of
G_{F_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
with crystalline lifts of weight
((2,p),\vec{m})
and
((2,3),\vec{m}^{\prime})
(where
\vec{m}^{\prime}=\vec{m}-(0,1)
as usual), but no crystalline lift of weight
((1,2),\vec{m})
. This fact also underpins our reliance on such a hypothesis in the proof of Theorem
7.4.6
.

We have the following result in the direction of partial weight one modularity:

Theorem 7.4.6
.

Let
F
be a real quadratic field in which
p
ramified, and let
{\mathfrak{p}}
be the prime of
F
over
p
.
Suppose that
\vec{k}=(1,2)
and that
\vec{m}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Sigma}
. Let
\rho:G_{F}\to\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{p})
be irreducible and geometrically modular of some weight. Suppose further that
p>2
,
\rho|_{G_{F(\zeta_{p})}}
is irreducible, and if
p=5
, then the projective image of
\rho|_{G_{F(\zeta_{5})}}
is not isomorphic to
A_{5}
. If
\rho|_{G_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}
is reducible and has a crystalline lift of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
, then
\rho
is geometrically modular of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
.

Proof.

If
\rho|_{G_{F_{\mathfrak{p}}}}
is reducible and has a crystalline lift of weight
(\vec{k},\vec{m})
, then by Proposition
7.3.2
we have

\rho\sim\psi\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\chi&c\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

for some characters
\psi
and
\chi
such that
